# Mindfield concept library.
#
# Format: "## id" starts a concept; then one "key: value" per line. Under "predict:" list the
# options, "* " for the right one and "- " for the others. "related:" lists ids of concepts close
# enough that they must never be offered as a wrong answer in the review quiz.
# Plain quotes and apostrophes are turned into curly ones when the app loads the file.
#
# Within each category the order is the order of first appearance. The app deals the categories
# round-robin (memory, self, thinking, influence, feelings, connection, decisions, habits, groups).
#
# Evidence: solid = replicated widely; good = real but smaller or conditional; debated = mixed
# replications; busted = failed to replicate or misreported.

# =====================================================================================
# MEMORY & PERCEPTION
# =====================================================================================

## frequency-illusion
title: The frequency illusion
aka: The Baader-Meinhof phenomenon
category: memory
evidence: good
hook: Learn about something today and you'll start seeing it everywhere. It was always there; your filter changed.
what: Once something is on your radar, attention starts flagging every instance of it, and confirmation bias makes each one feel like proof that it's suddenly common. The world didn't change. What you notice did. This app runs on that effect: each morning it puts one idea on your radar so you can spot it in the wild by evening.
study: Linguist Arnold Zwicky named the frequency illusion in 2005, alongside its cousin, the "recency illusion" (believing something you've just noticed is new). The nickname "Baader-Meinhof" came from a 1994 newspaper reader who heard the name of that German militant group twice in one day. The effect rests on two of psychology's best-supported findings: selective attention (we see what we're primed to look for) and confirmation bias (we count the hits and ignore the misses).
case: In late 1976, New York's three daily papers and five TV stations reported a wave of brutal crimes against elderly people. Sociologist Mark Fishman was observing a TV newsroom at the time and traced how the "wave" formed. Once one outlet framed a few muggings as a theme, editors everywhere began recognising crimes against the elderly as a category and pulling them from the stream of police reports they had always received. Of 89 such stories he tracked over six months, 63% fell inside the "wave" weeks. Politicians and police announced crackdowns, yet police statistics showed no matching surge in such crimes. The nuance: nobody invented crimes. Each story was true; what changed was the filter deciding which true events counted as news. That is the frequency illusion working at the scale of a city, and it is why a count over time beats a feeling that "it's everywhere now".
proof: There's no single landmark experiment with this name, but its two ingredients, selective attention and confirmation bias, are among the most replicated findings in the field.
spot: The moment you think "suddenly everyone is talking about X", ask yourself: is X really rising, or did I just start noticing it?
use: Let the illusion work for you: count every time today you notice something you learned recently, a word, a brand, an idea. Tonight, note the most surprising place it turned up.
guard: Before you decide a trend is real ("everyone's getting divorced", "crime is exploding"), look for an actual number over time rather than the examples you happen to have noticed.
predict: After people learn a new word, what usually happens?
- They notice it less, because it already feels familiar
* They notice it far more often, though it isn't actually used more
- They forget it within a day unless they write it down
scenario: Arjun buys a red hatchback. For the next month he's convinced the city has filled up with red hatchbacks, though sales figures show nothing changed.
source: Zwicky, A. (2005). Just between Dr. Language and I. Language Log; with selective attention and confirmation bias as the underlying mechanisms.
related: confirmation-bias, inattentional-blindness, availability-heuristic

## inattentional-blindness
title: Inattentional blindness
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Half of people counting basketball passes miss a gorilla walking through the game. Focus has a price.
what: Attention is a spotlight, not a floodlight. When you concentrate on one thing, you can fail to see something unexpected right in front of you, even something large and bizarre, and afterwards feel certain you'd have noticed it.
study: In 1999, Daniel Simons and Christopher Chabris asked people to count passes in a video of a basketball game. Midway through, a person in a gorilla suit walked into the middle, thumped their chest and walked off. About half the viewers never saw it. In 2013, Trafton Drew and colleagues hid a gorilla 48 times the size of a typical nodule in lung scans; 83% of expert radiologists looking for nodules missed it.
case: In 1995 Boston police officer Kenny Conley was chasing a suspect over a fence when, a few metres away, fellow officers were beating an undercover colleague they had mistaken for a suspect. Conley testified he saw nothing and was convicted of perjury, because jurors could not believe anyone could miss it. In 2011 Christopher Chabris and colleagues recreated the situation: students jogged behind a runner on a 400-metre route, counting how often he touched his head, while three people staged a fight beside the path. At night, 65% of joggers never noticed the fight; even in daylight, over 40% missed it, and missing it was more likely the harder the counting task. The study cannot show what Conley saw, but it shows his claim was plausible. The nuance: "I would have noticed" is a feeling, not evidence, which matters for courts, for drivers on phones, and for anyone checking work while thinking about something else.
proof: Replicated many times with different stimuli, tasks and populations, including experts in their own field.
spot: Watch for moments when you (or someone else) are concentrating hard, on a phone, a spreadsheet, a score, and miss something obvious nearby.
use: Do one ordinary task today, like a walk or a commute, while deliberately looking for something you normally ignore (a colour, a kind of sign, birds). Notice how much was always there.
guard: When it really matters (driving, checking a contract, reviewing work), assume you'll miss the unexpected. Use a checklist or a second pair of eyes that's looking for different things.
predict: Of experienced radiologists searching lung scans for nodules, how many noticed a gorilla 48 times the size of a nodule?
- Almost all of them; experts see everything on a scan
- About half
* Fewer than one in five
scenario: Meera is so focused on finding a parking spot that she drives straight past the friend who is waving at her from the pavement.
source: Simons, D. J., & Chabris, C. F. (1999). Gorillas in our midst. Perception; Drew, T., Võ, M. L.-H., & Wolfe, J. M. (2013). The invisible gorilla strikes again. Psychological Science.
related: change-blindness, cocktail-party-effect, frequency-illusion

## misinformation-effect
title: The misinformation effect
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: One word in a question, "smashed" instead of "hit", can make people remember broken glass that was never there.
what: Memory isn't a recording. Every time you recall something you rebuild it, and information you meet afterwards, a leading question, someone else's version, a photo, can get stitched into the memory itself. You then remember the edited version with full confidence.
study: In 1974 Elizabeth Loftus and John Palmer showed people a film of a car crash, then asked how fast the cars were going when they "smashed into" or "contacted" each other. The "smashed" group estimated about 41 mph; the "contacted" group about 32 mph. A week later, 32% of the "smashed" group remembered seeing broken glass, against 14% of those asked about cars that "hit". There was no broken glass in the film.
case: Memory research changed how police interview witnesses. In the US, mistaken eyewitness identification played a part in roughly 70% of the convictions later overturned by DNA evidence, often after witnesses were shown suggestive line-ups or told "good, you picked the suspect", which inflated their confidence by the time of trial. In response, psychologists Ronald Fisher and Edward Geiselman developed the Cognitive Interview: witnesses describe events in their own words, without interruption or leading questions, and mentally reinstate the scene. A 1999 meta-analysis by Günter Köhnken and colleagues found it elicited far more correct details than standard police interviews, with only a small rise in errors. The nuance: memory isn't hopelessly unreliable. Initial, uncontaminated memories can be quite accurate; the damage comes from what happens to them afterwards, through questions, feedback and retelling.
proof: One of the most replicated findings in memory research, central to how police and courts now handle eyewitness interviews.
spot: Notice when a question carries its own answer ("How rude was he?") or when a group retelling slowly shifts what everyone remembers.
use: Ask one open question today instead of a leading one: "What happened?" instead of "Was it awful?" Notice how the answer differs from what you expected.
guard: If you might need to remember an event accurately (an accident, an argument, a meeting), write down what happened before you discuss it with anyone.
predict: People saw a crash film and were asked how fast the cars were going when they "smashed". A week later, how many remembered broken glass that wasn't there?
- Almost none; the question only changed their speed estimate
* About a third
- Nearly everyone
scenario: After hearing her friends describe the party as "a disaster", Kavya finds herself remembering it as far tenser than she felt at the time.
source: Loftus, E. F., & Palmer, J. C. (1974). Reconstruction of automobile destruction. Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior.
related: false-memories, choice-blindness

## testing-effect
title: The testing effect
aka: Retrieval practice
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Trying to recall something cements it far better than rereading it, even though rereading feels more productive.
what: Pulling information out of memory changes the memory: it becomes stronger and easier to find next time. Rereading feels fluent, so it tricks you into thinking you've learned. Effortful recall feels harder and works better. That's why this app quizzes you instead of just showing you old concepts.
study: In 2006 Henry Roediger and Jeffrey Karpicke had students learn a prose passage. Some read it four times; others read it once and then practised recalling it three times. A week later the recall group remembered about 61% of the material versus 40% for the rereaders, yet the rereaders had predicted they'd do better.
case: Researchers Mark McDaniel, Pooja Agarwal and colleagues worked inside a middle school in Illinois, randomly assigning parts of the real science curriculum to be quizzed or not. Students took short, low-stakes clicker quizzes with immediate feedback on some topics; other topics were simply reviewed. At the end of the school year, eight months after the lessons, students scored about 82% on material that had been quizzed three times versus 69% on material that wasn't, one of the largest long-term effects found in a real classroom. Review quizzes just before the unit exam helped most. The nuance: the quizzes counted for almost nothing, so the benefit came from retrieval itself, not from studying harder for a grade, and feedback mattered, because practising a wrong answer without correction can strengthen the error.
proof: Replicated hundreds of times across ages, materials and classrooms; meta-analyses find a reliable, sizeable benefit.
spot: Notice when you (or a student, or a colleague) prepare by rereading notes or highlighting, which feels productive but works poorly.
use: Pick something you read this week. Close it and write down everything you remember in two minutes, then check what you missed.
guard: Feeling fluent isn't the same as knowing. If studying feels easy, you're probably not testing yourself enough.
predict: A week after learning a passage, how did students who practised recalling it compare with students who reread it?
* They remembered about half as much again
- About the same; time spent was what mattered
- Worse; recalling without the text locked in errors
scenario: Before an exam, Imran stops highlighting his notes and instead covers them and writes out everything he can remember, then checks the gaps.
source: Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning. Psychological Science.
related: spacing-effect, pretesting-effect

## cocktail-party-effect
title: The cocktail party effect
category: memory
evidence: good
hook: You can tune out a noisy room yet still hear your name across it. Your brain is listening to what you're ignoring.
what: You can focus on one conversation among many, filtering out the rest. But the filter isn't a wall: personally important things, especially your own name, can break through from the conversations you weren't listening to, which means your brain was processing them all along.
study: Colin Cherry described the problem in 1953 using headphones that played different messages to each ear. In 1959 Neville Moray found that people who were repeating one message out loud remembered almost nothing of the ignored ear, except that about a third noticed when it said their own name. Wood and Cowan replicated this in 1995 with a similar result, about one person in three.
case: In 2001 Andrew Conway, Nelson Cowan and Michael Bunting repeated Moray's classic test, inserting each participant's own name into the message they were supposed to ignore while repeating aloud a message in the other ear. The twist was that they first measured each person's working memory capacity, their ability to keep focus under distraction. About two-thirds of people with low working memory capacity noticed their name, compared with about a fifth of those with high capacity. So hearing your name "across the room" isn't a sign of a superior brain; it often reflects attention leaking away from the task at hand. The nuance: the same leakiness that makes some people easily distracted also makes them more likely to catch something important in the background, a trade-off rather than a simple flaw.
proof: Selective listening is solidly established. The "own name" breakthrough replicates, but only for a minority of listeners, often those with less working memory to spare.
spot: Notice when a word you care about (your name, your city, your company) pops out of background chatter, or when a child who "can't hear" you hears the word "ice cream".
use: In a busy place today, focus on one conversation for a minute, then on another. Notice what slips through the filter, and which words grab you.
guard: Being able to hear doesn't mean you're listening. If someone needs your full attention, stop doing the other thing.
predict: When people ignored a message in one ear while repeating another, how many noticed their own name in the ignored ear?
- Nobody; attention blocks the ignored ear completely
* About a third
- Almost everyone
scenario: In a crowded office Vikram hears nothing of the meeting next to him until someone there says the name of his project, and suddenly he's listening.
source: Cherry, E. C. (1953). Journal of the Acoustical Society of America; Moray, N. (1959). Quarterly Journal of Experimental Psychology; Wood, N., & Cowan, N. (1995). Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition.
related: inattentional-blindness

## change-blindness
title: Change blindness
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Swap the person someone is talking to mid-conversation and about half won't notice.
what: We feel we see a rich, detailed picture of the world, but we store very little of it from one moment to the next. If a change happens during a blink, a cut or a brief interruption, we often miss it entirely, because we were never holding the detail in the first place.
study: In 1998 Daniel Simons and Daniel Levin had an experimenter stop strangers on a campus to ask directions. Mid-conversation, two workers carried a door between them, and a different experimenter swapped in to continue the chat. Only about half of the people noticed they were now talking to a different person.
case: In 2000 Daniel Levin and colleagues described the door experiment and other change-blindness studies to students, then asked whether they would notice such changes. About 83% predicted they would spot the swap of the person they were talking to, though in the real studies only about half did. The researchers called this "change blindness blindness": we overrate how much detail we store. The real-world stakes are clearest where people monitor screens or scenes: film editors miss continuity errors seen by millions; air traffic controllers and radiologists comparing images over time are trained with deliberate side-by-side comparison and flicker techniques because unaided comparison fails. The nuance: noticing changes isn't a skill you can simply try harder at; it depends on whether the changed detail was attended to and stored in the first place.
proof: Replicated in many forms, from film continuity errors to photo flicker tasks.
spot: Notice continuity errors in films, or a friend's new haircut nobody mentions for a week.
use: Look closely at a room you know well and try to find three things that changed recently. Then ask someone else to try.
guard: Don't trust "I would have noticed". For things that matter, compare before and after side by side, or keep a record.
predict: When the person asking for directions was swapped behind a passing door, how many people noticed?
- Almost all of them, straight away
* About half
- Almost none
scenario: Riya rearranges the photos on the fridge. Her flatmate uses the fridge ten times over the next three days and never notices.
source: Simons, D. J., & Levin, D. T. (1998). Failure to detect changes to people during a real-world interaction. Psychonomic Bulletin & Review.
related: inattentional-blindness, choice-blindness

## learning-styles
title: The learning styles myth
category: memory
evidence: busted
hook: Most teachers believe students learn best in their "style", visual or auditory. When tested properly, it doesn't hold up.
what: The idea is that each person is a visual, auditory or kinaesthetic learner and learns best when teaching matches that style. People do have preferences, but matching teaching to them doesn't improve learning. What matters is matching the method to the material: diagrams for maps, practice for skills, recall for facts.
study: In 2008 Harold Pashler, Mark McDaniel, Doug Rohrer and Robert Bjork reviewed the evidence for the "meshing" hypothesis. They found almost no studies with the design needed to test it, and the few that had one contradicted it. Later experiments found the same: people who called themselves visual learners did no better with pictures. Yet surveys of teachers in several countries find most still believe in learning styles.
case: Medical anatomy courses are a natural test because students spend hundreds of hours studying. In 2019 Polly Husmann and Valerie Dean O'Loughlin had anatomy students take the popular VARK learning-styles questionnaire, then tracked how they actually studied and how they scored. Most students did not study in the way their "style" suggested, and those who did got no benefit in their grades. What predicted scores were specific strategies, such as using the virtual microscope and self-testing, regardless of style. Earlier, Beth Rogowsky and colleagues (2015) gave adults who identified as visual or auditory learners the same text either as an audiobook or as print, and found no match between style and comprehension. The nuance: preferences are real and can affect motivation, but learning depends on matching the method to the content, and labelling yourself a "type" can steer you away from methods that work.
proof: Busted: proper tests find no benefit from matching instruction to a person's preferred style.
spot: Listen for "I'm a visual learner, so..." as a reason to avoid reading, practising or testing yourself.
use: Learn one small thing today in the way that suits the material rather than your preference: say a phone number aloud, sketch a route, or practise a movement.
guard: Be wary of courses or tests that sort you into a type and promise faster learning. Effort, spacing and self-testing work for everyone.
predict: When researchers matched teaching to people's "learning style", what happened?
- Learning improved by about a third
- Learning improved only for visual learners
* Learning didn't improve
scenario: A coaching centre charges extra to test each child's "learning type" so lessons can be tailored, though the tailoring has never been shown to help.
source: Pashler, H., McDaniel, M., Rohrer, D., & Bjork, R. (2008). Learning styles: Concepts and evidence. Psychological Science in the Public Interest.
related: testing-effect

## pretesting-effect
title: The pretesting effect
aka: Why this app asks you to predict first
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Guessing an answer before you learn it, even wrongly, makes the real answer stick better.
what: When you try to answer a question before you've been taught, your mind builds a slot for the answer. When the real answer arrives, it lands in that slot and is remembered better, even if your guess was wrong. That's why every concept in this app asks you to predict first.
study: In 2009 Lindsey Richland, Nate Kornell and Liche Kao had students attempt questions about a passage before reading it. Even though they mostly got them wrong, they later remembered the tested information better than students who simply spent extra time studying. Meta-analyses published in 2023 pooled dozens of such studies and found a reliable, moderate benefit for the pre-asked material.
case: In 2009 Nate Kornell, Matthew Hays and Robert Bjork had people learn pairs of weakly related words. In one condition they first had to guess the second word from the cue for a few seconds, which they almost always got wrong, and were then shown the answer. In the other condition they simply studied the full pair for the whole time. Despite the failed guesses, and despite having less time to study the correct pair, people remembered the pairs better after guessing. The effect has since been shown with prose, videos and real university lectures, where questions posed before a topic improve memory for that material. The nuance: it works when the right answer follows quickly and when the guess is about meaning. It also tends to help only the specific information the questions targeted, not the whole lesson.
proof: Replicated widely with adults and children, in labs and classrooms; the benefit is clearest for the exact material that was pre-questioned.
spot: Notice when a good teacher, podcast or talk opens with a question before giving the answer.
use: Before you look something up today, a word, a fact, a price, write down your best guess first. Notice whether the answer sticks.
guard: The guess only helps if you get the correct answer soon after. Don't leave wrong guesses uncorrected.
predict: Students attempted questions before reading a passage and mostly got them wrong. Later, how did they do on that material?
- Worse; their wrong guesses got in the way
- About the same as students who just studied
* Better than students who spent the same time studying
scenario: Before reading a chapter on monsoons, Tanvi's teacher asks the class to guess why Chennai gets most of its rain in winter. Nobody knows, but everyone remembers the answer afterwards.
source: Richland, L. E., Kornell, N., & Kao, L. S. (2009). The pretesting effect. Journal of Experimental Psychology: Applied.
related: testing-effect, spacing-effect

## false-memories
title: False memories
category: memory
evidence: good
hook: With a little suggestion, about a quarter of people come to remember a childhood event that never happened.
what: Memories can be implanted, not just distorted. A plausible story from a trusted source, repeated and imagined, can grow into a memory that feels like your own, complete with details and emotions.
study: In 1995 Elizabeth Loftus and Jacqueline Pickrell gave adults short stories of childhood events supposedly told by their relatives. One was invented: getting lost in a shopping mall. After two interviews, about a quarter of participants remembered the made-up event, wholly or in part. A 2017 pooled analysis of implanted-memory studies estimated that around 30% of participants form some false memory.
case: In 2015 Julia Shaw and Stephen Porter reported that, after three interviews using suggestive techniques, 70% of students came to "remember" committing a crime as a teenager, such as an assault, that had never happened. The study made headlines about false confessions. In 2018 Kimberley Wade, Maryanne Garry and Kathy Pezdek re-analysed it: Shaw and Porter had counted people who came to believe the event as having false memories. Using stricter coding that requires a person to describe actually remembering it, only about 26 to 30% qualified. The nuance: both numbers matter. A quarter of people forming false recollections of a crime is alarming for police interrogation; but the difference between believing and remembering shows how much the headline rate depends on definitions. It's a model of reading research critically: ask how the key outcome was measured.
proof: Implanted memories are well documented, but how often they occur depends heavily on how a "memory" is counted, so treat the exact percentages loosely.
spot: Notice family stories you "remember" mainly from photos or retellings, or memories that grew more detailed every time they were told.
use: Pick a vivid childhood memory and ask a relative how they remember it. Note where your versions differ.
guard: Confidence and vividness don't prove a memory is accurate. Be careful about "recovering" memories through suggestion, imagination or repeated questioning.
predict: When adults were given a made-up story about getting lost in a mall as a child, how many came to remember it?
- Almost none; people know their own childhood
* About a quarter
- About three quarters
scenario: Aditya has a crisp memory of falling off a swing at age four. His mother later reveals that it was his cousin who fell, and he only heard about it.
source: Loftus, E. F., & Pickrell, J. E. (1995). The formation of false memories. Psychiatric Annals; Scoboria, A., et al. (2017). Memory.
related: misinformation-effect

## serial-position-effect
title: The serial position effect
aka: Primacy and recency
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: In any list, people remember the first and last items best. The middle disappears.
what: Early items get the most rehearsal, so they get into long-term memory (primacy). The last items are still fresh in short-term memory (recency). Whatever sits in the middle gets the least of both, whether it's a shopping list, a presentation or a row of job candidates.
study: In 1962 Bennet Murdock had people recall word lists of different lengths and found the U-shaped curve: good at the start, poor in the middle, good at the end. In 1966 Murray Glanzer and Anita Cunitz showed the two ends have different causes: adding a 30-second delay before recall wiped out the recency advantage but left primacy intact.
case: In judged competitions, people often hear or see options one after another, and position quietly shapes results. Psychologist Wändi Bruine de Bruin analysed the Eurovision Song Contest and international figure-skating championships and found that scores rose with performance order: later performers tended to score higher, even though the order was set by random draw. Studies of elections where ballot order is randomised, such as Daniel Ho and Kosuke Imai's analysis of California, found that being listed first gives a small boost, especially to lesser-known candidates and in primary elections. The nuance: whether first or last wins depends on the setting. When options are presented one at a time and judged at the end, recency tends to help; when people choose from a list with limited attention, the top of the list wins.
proof: One of the most reliable findings in memory research, reproduced countless times.
spot: Notice what people remember from a meeting, a menu or a list of options: usually the first and the last.
use: If you're presenting or explaining something today, put your most important point first and repeat it at the end.
guard: When comparing many options (candidates, flats, products), the middle ones get short-changed. Take notes on each or compare in a different order.
predict: People heard a list of words and recalled them after a 30-second delay. What happened to their memory for the last few words?
- It got stronger; they had more time to think
* The advantage for the last words disappeared
- Nothing; the curve stayed the same
scenario: After hearing twelve specials read out by the waiter, Neha can only remember the first dish and the last one.
source: Murdock, B. B. (1962). Journal of Experimental Psychology; Glanzer, M., & Cunitz, A. R. (1966). Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior.
related: peak-end-rule

## spacing-effect
title: The spacing effect
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Study something in three short sessions spread over a week and you'll keep it longer than one long cram.
what: Practice spread out over time beats the same amount of practice crammed together. Each gap lets the memory fade a little, so the next session has to work harder to retrieve it, and that effort is what strengthens it. This app brings concepts back at growing intervals for that reason.
study: Hermann Ebbinghaus documented the effect on himself in 1885. In 2006 Nicholas Cepeda and colleagues analysed over 300 experiments and found spaced practice reliably beat massed practice, and that the best gap grows with how long you need to remember: for a test in a year, review sessions weeks apart work better than days apart.
case: Psychologist Harry Bahrick turned his own family into a nine-year experiment. In a 1993 study, Bahrick, his wife and two adult children each learned hundreds of foreign-language words, practising some in 13 sessions and others in 26 sessions, with gaps of 14, 28 or 56 days between sessions. Five years after training ended, they were tested. Words practised 13 times at 56-day intervals were remembered about as well as words practised 26 times at 14-day intervals. In other words, wider spacing halved the practice needed for the same long-term retention. The nuance: the widely spaced sessions felt harder, because more had been forgotten between them, and that struggle is part of why they worked. Short gaps feel efficient and fool you into thinking you've mastered the material.
proof: One of the oldest and most robust findings in psychology, confirmed in labs, classrooms and skills training.
spot: Notice cramming: the all-nighter, the one long practice session before the match, the language app binge after weeks off.
use: Review something you learned a few days ago (a recipe, a word, a concept from this app) without looking at it first.
guard: Massed practice feels better in the moment because performance rises quickly. Don't judge a study method by how it feels on the day.
predict: For remembering something a year from now, what review schedule works best?
- Several sessions on the same day
- Daily sessions for one week
* Sessions spread weeks apart
scenario: Instead of one four-hour revision session, Karan does twenty minutes on Monday, Thursday and the following Tuesday, and remembers far more in the exam.
source: Cepeda, N. J., et al. (2006). Distributed practice in verbal recall tasks. Psychological Bulletin.
related: testing-effect, pretesting-effect

## self-reference-effect
title: The self-reference effect
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Ask "does this describe me?" about a word and you'll remember it better than if you study its meaning.
what: Information linked to yourself gets remembered better, because "me" is the most richly connected structure in memory. New ideas that attach to your own experiences have many hooks to hang on. That's why this app asks how today's concept showed up in your life.
study: In 1977 Tim Rogers, Nicholas Kuiper and William Kirker showed people adjectives and asked different questions about them: is it in capitals, does it rhyme, what does it mean, does it describe you? "Does it describe you" produced the best recall, better even than thinking about meaning. A 1997 meta-analysis of 129 studies by Cynthia Symons and Blair Johnson confirmed the advantage.
case: In 2008 Sheila Cunningham and colleagues ran a shopping game in which two people sorted cards picturing items into their own basket or the other person's. Afterwards, participants remembered the items they had "owned" much better than the ones placed in the other person's basket, even though ownership was arbitrary and temporary. In classrooms, Candace Walkington (2013) personalised algebra word problems to students' own interests, such as sports, music or games, while keeping the maths identical; students solved the personalised problems more successfully, with the biggest gains for students who were struggling. The nuance: the effect comes from connecting new information to the rich web of knowledge you have about yourself, so a vague "this relates to me" helps less than a specific link to a real memory.
proof: Confirmed by meta-analysis; it holds across ages and many kinds of material.
spot: Notice which parts of a talk or book you remember: usually the ones where you thought "that's exactly like me" or "that happened to me".
use: Tonight, connect today's concept to a specific moment from your own life and write one sentence about it.
guard: Self-relevance also makes things feel truer than they are (see the Barnum effect). Remembering a horoscope doesn't make it accurate.
predict: Which question about a word produced the best memory for it later?
- "What does this word mean?"
- "Does this word rhyme with another?"
* "Does this word describe you?"
scenario: Sana forgets most of a lecture on attachment theory, except the part she related to how she behaves with her own mother.
source: Rogers, T. B., Kuiper, N. A., & Kirker, W. S. (1977). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Symons, C. S., & Johnson, B. T. (1997). Psychological Bulletin.
related: testing-effect, barnum-effect

## google-effect
title: The Google effect
aka: Digital amnesia
category: memory
evidence: debated
hook: When we know information is saved somewhere, we may remember where it is rather than what it says.
what: The idea is that we treat the internet like a friend who remembers things for us: when information is easy to look up, we invest less in storing it, and remember the location instead. Offloading memory isn't new (diaries, address books), but how much it changes memory is still argued over.
study: In 2011 Betsy Sparrow, Jenny Liu and Daniel Wegner reported that people who believed typed facts would be saved remembered them less well, and were better at recalling the folder they were saved in than the facts. In 2018 a large project replicating social science papers from Nature and Science re-ran one of their experiments and found no effect.
case: In 2015 Matthew Fisher, Mariel Goddu and Frank Keil asked people to answer questions such as "How does a zip work?", either using internet search or without it. Afterwards, those who had searched rated their own ability to explain unrelated topics more highly, as if the internet's knowledge had become their own. In 2017 Benjamin Storm and colleagues found that people who had used Google to answer hard trivia questions were later more likely to reach for Google even on easy questions they could have answered from memory. The nuance: offloading memory to devices isn't new or always bad (Storm's own work shows saving one file can free memory for the next), but these studies suggest a subtler cost: we may lose track of where our knowledge ends and the search engine's begins.
proof: The broad idea (we offload memory to tools) is plausible and partly supported, but a high-profile replication of the original study failed.
spot: Notice when you can't remember a phone number, a route or a fact, but know exactly where to find it.
use: Choose one thing you always look up (a frequently used number, a route, a formula) and learn it by heart today.
guard: Offloading is fine for trivia, but you can't think with knowledge you don't have in your head. Keep the core facts of your work in memory.
predict: What happened when a large replication project re-ran one of the original "Google effect" experiments?
- It found a bigger effect than the original
- It found the same effect
* It found no effect
scenario: Rahul can't recall a single fact from an article he read yesterday, but he knows it's in his "Read later" folder under "Health".
source: Sparrow, B., Liu, J., & Wegner, D. M. (2011). Science; Camerer, C. F., et al. (2018). Nature Human Behaviour.
related: testing-effect

## mozart-effect
title: The Mozart effect
category: memory
evidence: busted
hook: Ten minutes of Mozart was said to make you smarter. The tiny, short-lived bump comes from any music you enjoy.
what: A 1993 study reported that listening to a Mozart sonata briefly improved spatial reasoning. It turned into a craze: CDs for babies, and a US governor proposing a classical CD for every newborn. Later research found the effect was small, lasted minutes, and came from being in a good, alert mood, which any enjoyable music (or story) can produce.
study: In 1993 Frances Rauscher, Gordon Shaw and Katherine Ky reported in Nature that students did better on a spatial task after 10 minutes of Mozart than after silence, for about 10 to 15 minutes. In 2001 William Thompson, Glenn Schellenberg and Gabriela Husain found the benefit appeared only when music lifted mood and arousal; a slow, sad piece didn't help. A 2010 meta-analysis of about 40 studies by Jakob Pietschnig and colleagues found little evidence for a specific Mozart effect.
case: In 1998 the governor of Georgia, Zell Miller, proposed spending about $105,000 to give every newborn in the state a classical music CD, citing the "Mozart effect"; Tennessee followed, and a "Baby Einstein" industry boomed. When researchers checked whether baby media helped development, the results pointed the other way. In 2007 Frederick Zimmerman, Dimitri Christakis and Andrew Meltzoff surveyed about a thousand parents and found that, among infants aged 8 to 16 months, each daily hour of baby DVDs was associated with knowing 6 to 8 fewer words. The study was correlational, but it helped push Disney to offer refunds for Baby Einstein videos. The nuance: music isn't harmful and it does lift mood; but a tiny, short-lived lab effect in college students was scaled up into public policy for infants that it never tested.
proof: Busted: there's no special effect of Mozart, and no lasting gain in intelligence from listening.
spot: Notice products promising brain boosts from passive listening, watching or playing: "brain music", baby genius videos, IQ apps.
use: Before a focused task today, play a piece of music that genuinely lifts your mood for a few minutes, then switch it off and notice your energy.
guard: Passive exposure doesn't build intelligence. Active practice, sleep and learning an instrument have better evidence.
predict: What explained the brief boost after listening to Mozart?
- Mozart's mathematical structure
* Being in a better, more alert mood
- Classical music in general, but not other genres
scenario: Expecting a smarter baby, Pooja plays classical "brain development" playlists to her newborn for hours every day.
source: Rauscher, F. H., Shaw, G. L., & Ky, K. N. (1993). Nature; Thompson, W. F., Schellenberg, E. G., & Husain, G. (2001). Psychological Science; Pietschnig, J., Voracek, M., & Formann, A. K. (2010). Intelligence.
related: learning-styles, replication-crisis

## reminiscence-bump
title: The reminiscence bump
category: memory
evidence: solid
hook: Ask older people for their most vivid memories and they cluster between ages 10 and 30. Your teens and twenties write your story.
what: When people look back over their lives, they recall a disproportionate number of memories from adolescence and early adulthood. It's the period of firsts (first love, first job, first home) and the time when identity forms, so those memories become the core of the life story.
study: In 1986 David Rubin, Scott Wetzler and Robert Nebes analysed autobiographical memories from older adults and found a bump between roughly 10 and 30, above what simple forgetting would predict. The bump appears across cultures, and for favourite music, books and films too: the songs you loved at around 20 tend to stay your favourites.
case: Music researchers Carol Lynne Krumhansl and Justin Zupnick (2013) played popular songs from across six decades to college students and asked which they knew, liked and associated with memories. As expected, students knew and loved the music of their own late teens. But there was a second, "cascading" bump: they also had strong memories and feelings for music from the 1960s and 1980s, the years when their parents were young, absorbed through family life. Earlier, Morris Holbrook and Robert Schindler found that musical tastes peak for songs popular when people were about 23. The bump is used in dementia care: music and objects from someone's late teens and twenties are the cues most likely to unlock memories. The nuance: the bump isn't only about youth being exciting; it is when identity forms, so those memories become the backbone of the life story.
proof: Replicated across many countries and methods.
spot: Notice how older relatives' stories return again and again to the same years, or how "music was better back then" usually means "music was better when I was 18".
use: Ask an older person today about their most vivid memory. Note what age they were.
guard: If you're past 30, remember that new firsts still make memories: travel somewhere new, learn something hard, meet new people. Routine years blur together.
predict: When older adults list their most vivid memories, which period is overrepresented?
- The most recent ten years
* Roughly ages 10 to 30
- Early childhood, before 7
scenario: Whenever the family gathers, Dadaji's stories all come from the years he was at college and starting his first job in Kolkata.
source: Rubin, D. C., Wetzler, S. E., & Nebes, R. D. (1986). Autobiographical memory across the lifespan. In D. C. Rubin (Ed.), Autobiographical Memory.
related: self-reference-effect

## focal-is-causal
title: What's focal seems causal
category: memory
evidence: good
hook: Watch a conversation over one person's shoulder and you'll judge the other person as the one driving it.
what: Whoever or whatever holds our attention gets more of the credit and the blame. The person we can see clearly, the louder voice, the one in the spotlight seems to be causing what happens, while influences outside the frame fade. It is one perceptual root of the fundamental attribution error: the actor is in focus, the situation is background.
study: In 1975 Shelley Taylor and Susan Fiske seated observers around two people having a scripted conversation. Some sat behind one speaker, facing the other; some sat to the side and could see both equally. Observers judged the person they faced as having set the tone and steered the conversation more. Those who could see both rated them about equally, though everyone heard exactly the same conversation.
case: Police often record interrogations with the camera on the suspect alone. Since the 1980s Daniel Lassiter and colleagues have shown mock jurors the same interrogation filmed three ways: focused on the suspect, on the detective, or on both equally. Suspect-focused confessions were judged more voluntary and the suspect more likely guilty. In a 2007 study, 21 judges and 24 police officers showed the same bias, so expertise didn't protect them. Filming both people equally removed the effect, and New Zealand made equal-focus recording national policy after the first study. The nuance: you can't simply decide not to be affected, because the bias starts in perception. The fix is structural: change what's in the frame.
proof: The salience effect has been replicated in many settings, and the camera-perspective bias has been shown repeatedly, including with real interrogation footage, judges and police officers.
spot: Notice who is on camera, at the front of the meeting or quoted in the headline, and how they collect the credit or blame for outcomes that had many causes.
use: In one disagreement or news story today, deliberately shift your attention to the person or factor outside the frame and ask what part they played.
guard: When you judge responsibility (a team failure, a dispute, a video clip), make sure you've seen every party equally well before deciding who caused what.
predict: Judges watched the same confession filmed with the camera on the suspect, or on both suspect and detective. What happened?
- They were unaffected; legal training corrects for camera angles
* They judged the suspect-only version as more voluntary
- They judged the suspect-only version as less voluntary, suspecting pressure
scenario: Watching a clip of a shouting match filmed over one man's shoulder, Kabir decides the woman facing the camera clearly started it, though he never saw the man's face.
source: Taylor, S. E., & Fiske, S. T. (1975). Point of view and perceptions of causality. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Lassiter, G. D., et al. (2007). Evaluating videotaped confessions: Expertise provides no defense against the camera-perspective effect. Psychological Science.
related: fundamental-attribution-error, inattentional-blindness

# =====================================================================================
# THE SELF
# =====================================================================================

## spotlight-effect
title: The spotlight effect
category: self
evidence: good
hook: You think everyone noticed your embarrassing T-shirt. Fewer than half as many did.
what: Because we're the centre of our own experience, we assume we're more noticed than we are. Your bad hair day, your stumble in a presentation, your new glasses: most people barely register them, because they're busy being the centre of their own spotlight.
study: In 2000 Thomas Gilovich, Victoria Medvec and Kenneth Savitsky had students put on a T-shirt with a large picture of the singer Barry Manilow (deeply unfashionable for them) and walk into a room of peers. The wearers estimated that about half the room had noticed the shirt. In fact about 23% had.
case: In 2001 Kenneth Savitsky, Nicholas Epley and Thomas Gilovich had people do something embarrassing in front of observers, such as failing a set of easy-looking anagrams, and then predict how harshly the observers would judge them. People expected to be judged far more harshly than they were. The observers were thinking about the anagrams being hard, about their own performance, or about the situation; the person who failed was thinking about the failure. The same pattern appears in social anxiety research: people overestimate how visible their nervousness, stains, stumbles and awkward pauses are. The nuance: it cuts both ways. Your shining moments also get less attention than you assume, so if a contribution matters, say it clearly rather than counting on people to notice.
proof: Replicated in variations (on appearance, mistakes and contributions to group discussion), though the size varies by situation.
spot: Notice when you or someone else avoids something ("I can't go, I've got a spot on my face") out of fear of being looked at.
use: Wear or do one slightly unusual thing today: mismatched socks, a bright colour, a different hairstyle. Count how many people actually mention it.
guard: The flip side: people also notice your good moments less than you think. Don't rely on being noticed; tell people about things that matter.
predict: Students wore an embarrassing T-shirt into a room and guessed half the room noticed it. How many actually did?
- About 60%
- About 45%
* About 23%
scenario: Priya spent the whole wedding worrying that everyone could see the stain on her kurta. Afterwards, not one photo or guest mentions it.
source: Gilovich, T., Medvec, V. H., & Savitsky, K. (2000). The spotlight effect in social judgment. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: illusion-of-transparency

## bias-blind-spot
title: The bias blind spot
category: self
evidence: solid
hook: Most people think they're less biased than average. Being smarter doesn't help; it can make it worse.
what: We can see biases in others easily, but when we look inward we find only our reasons, which feel objective. So we conclude that others are swayed by bias while we see things as they are. Learning about biases can even make it worse, because now we can name everyone else's.
study: In 2002 Emily Pronin, Daniel Lin and Lee Ross asked people how susceptible they and the average American were to a list of biases. People rated themselves as less biased than average, even right after reading descriptions of the biases. In 2012 Richard West, Russell Meserve and Keith Stanovich found that people with higher cognitive ability didn't have a smaller blind spot; if anything, it was slightly larger.
case: Medicine offers a sharp real-world example. In 2001 Michael Steinman, Michael Shlipak and Stephen McPhee surveyed internal medicine residents about gifts and meals from pharmaceutical companies. 61% said such promotions did not influence their own prescribing, but only 16% believed other doctors were similarly unaffected. Yet studies of prescribing data repeatedly find that even small industry payments, like a single sponsored meal, are followed by more prescriptions of the promoted drug. The nuance: this is not about bad people. The doctors could find no influence when they looked inward, because influence isn't something you can feel. That's why medical bodies moved from "use your judgement" to disclosure rules and gift bans: external rules work where introspection can't.
proof: Replicated across many samples, and with a validated scale measuring individual differences.
spot: Listen for "I'm just being objective" or "they're biased", especially in an argument where both sides say it.
use: Pick a recent disagreement. Write down one way your own view might have been shaped by a bias you'd easily spot in the other person.
guard: Since you can't see your own biases by looking inward, rely on outside checks: ask for disagreement, use clear criteria decided in advance, get blind reviews.
predict: Did people with higher cognitive ability have a smaller bias blind spot?
- Yes, much smaller
- Yes, slightly smaller
* No; if anything it was larger
scenario: Kabir carefully explains how his colleague's view on the project is coloured by her loyalty to her old team, and is sure his own view is purely based on data.
source: Pronin, E., Lin, D. Y., & Ross, L. (2002). Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin; West, R. F., Meserve, R. J., & Stanovich, K. E. (2012). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: confirmation-bias, better-than-average-effect, fundamental-attribution-error

## illusion-of-transparency
title: The illusion of transparency
category: self
evidence: good
hook: You feel your nerves are obvious to everyone. They're mostly invisible, and knowing that makes you calmer.
what: We know what we're feeling so vividly that we assume it leaks out: the nervousness in a speech, the lie, the irritation at a meeting. In fact others read much less of our inner state than we think.
study: In 1998 Thomas Gilovich, Kenneth Savitsky and Victoria Medvec found that people telling lies overestimated how many observers would catch them, and nervous speakers overestimated how nervous they looked. In 2003 Savitsky and Gilovich told some speakers about this illusion before they spoke. Those speakers felt less anxious, and observers rated their speeches as better and more composed.
case: In negotiations, the illusion is costly. In 2003 Leaf Van Boven, Thomas Gilovich and Victoria Medvec had pairs negotiate deals with several issues of different importance to each side. Afterwards, negotiators overestimated how well their counterparts had picked up on their priorities and preferences. Feeling that "it was obvious what I cared about", they didn't spell it out, and left value on the table that a clearer exchange of priorities would have captured. In everyday life the same thing happens with hints: a partner who thinks their disappointment was plain, a manager who believes the team "knows" they're unhappy. The nuance: the solution isn't to hide feelings better, but to make important ones explicit: say what you want and why.
proof: Replicated in a range of settings; the "telling people about it helps" study is a small but encouraging result.
spot: Notice when someone says "Was it obvious I was nervous?" Almost always, the answer is no.
use: Before something nerve-racking today (a call, a meeting, asking for something), remind yourself: they can't see most of what I'm feeling. Then notice whether anyone comments.
guard: The reverse also holds: people don't read your hints. If you're upset or need something, say it in words.
predict: Speakers were told about the illusion of transparency before giving a speech. What happened?
- Nothing changed; knowing about a bias doesn't help
* They felt calmer and their speeches were rated better
- They became more self-conscious and did worse
scenario: Ishaan's heart was pounding through his whole interview. Afterwards the interviewer compliments him on how calm and confident he seemed.
source: Gilovich, T., Savitsky, K., & Medvec, V. H. (1998). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Savitsky, K., & Gilovich, T. (2003). Journal of Experimental Social Psychology.
related: spotlight-effect, curse-of-knowledge

## barnum-effect
title: The Barnum effect
aka: The Forer effect
category: self
evidence: solid
hook: A generic personality description feels uncannily accurate when you think it was written about you.
what: Statements like "You have a great need for others to like you, yet you tend to be critical of yourself" fit almost everyone. When we believe a description is personal, we search our memory for confirming examples and find them. That's how horoscopes, palm readings and many online personality quizzes feel so accurate.
study: In 1949 psychologist Bertram Forer gave his students a personality test, then handed each a "personal" profile. All received the same text, assembled largely from a newsstand astrology book. Rated from 0 to 5 for accuracy, it scored an average of 4.26.
case: In 1985 physicist Shawn Carlson ran a double-blind test of astrology, published in Nature. Astrologers who had agreed to the test design in advance were given a person's birth chart and three personality profiles, one belonging to that person, drawn from a standard psychological questionnaire. They matched charts to the right profile no better than chance. Meanwhile, participants given their own astrological reading picked it out of three no better than chance either, even though readings "feel" accurate. A related case is the popular Myers-Briggs Type Indicator: studies of test-retest reliability have found that many people are assigned a different four-letter type when retested a few weeks later. The nuance: a description feeling right isn't evidence. Useful personality measures make specific predictions that can be checked.
proof: Replicated many times, including with astrology and graphology readings.
spot: Watch for descriptions with both sides covered ("you can be outgoing, but sometimes you need time alone") or flattering vagueness.
use: Read today's horoscope for a different sign than yours. Notice how much of it fits you anyway.
guard: Before trusting a personality test, ask: would this description fit most of my friends too? Good tests make risky, specific predictions.
predict: Students rated a "personal" profile that was actually identical for everyone. On a 0 to 5 scale, how accurate did they rate it?
- About 2 (they saw through it)
- About 3
* Over 4
scenario: After a palm reader tells Simran she is "independent but values deep connections, and has untapped potential", she tells her friends it was scarily accurate.
source: Forer, B. R. (1949). The fallacy of personal validation. Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology.
related: confirmation-bias, self-reference-effect

## power-posing
title: Power posing
category: self
evidence: busted
hook: Standing like a superhero for two minutes was supposed to change your hormones. It doesn't.
what: A famous 2010 study claimed that two minutes of "high-power" poses (hands on hips, feet apart) raised testosterone, lowered cortisol and made people take more risks. The TED talk became one of the most watched ever. Larger studies didn't find the hormone or behaviour changes. What remains is small: people may feel a little more powerful.
study: In 2015 Eva Ranehill and colleagues repeated the experiment with 200 people (about five times the original sample) and found no effect on hormones or risk-taking, only a small rise in self-reported feelings of power. In 2016 Dana Carney, the original study's lead author, publicly stated she no longer believed the effects were real.
case: After the 2012 TED talk, power poses entered corporate training, interview coaching and school programmes. In 2017 Quentin Gronau and colleagues pooled six preregistered studies testing power poses. They found good evidence for one thing only: people who adopted expansive poses reported feeling slightly more powerful. There was no reliable evidence for hormone changes or changes in risk-taking or performance. In 2016 Dana Carney, who ran the original study, published a statement explaining the flexible analyses used in the original paper and saying she no longer believed the effects were real. The nuance: a small, subjective effect on feelings may remain, but the promise of changing your biology in two minutes did not survive careful testing. It is a lesson in how quickly an appealing finding can be scaled up before it has been replicated.
proof: Busted for hormones and behaviour; a modest effect on how powerful people say they feel may remain.
spot: Notice confidence advice that promises physical or hormonal changes from a quick trick.
use: Before a stressful moment today, skip the pose and try something with better evidence: name the feeling, or tell yourself "I'm excited" rather than "I'm calm".
guard: Being moved by a great talk isn't evidence. Ask whether a finding has been repeated by independent researchers with larger samples.
predict: When the power-posing study was repeated with five times as many people, what was found?
- Bigger hormone changes than the original
* No hormone or risk-taking changes, only slightly more "felt power"
- The effect only worked for men
scenario: Before her pitch, Devika locks herself in a bathroom stall and stands with hands on hips for two minutes, sure it will raise her testosterone.
source: Carney, D. R., Cuddy, A. J. C., & Yap, A. J. (2010). Psychological Science; Ranehill, E., et al. (2015). Psychological Science.
related: replication-crisis, facial-feedback

## better-than-average-effect
title: The better-than-average effect
category: self
evidence: solid
hook: 93% of American drivers in one study rated themselves above the median. That's not mathematically possible.
what: On most positive traits (driving, kindness, honesty, intelligence) most people rate themselves above average. We know our own good intentions and the reasons behind our failures, and we define "good" by the skills we happen to have.
study: In 1981 Ola Svenson asked students in the US and Sweden to compare their driving skill with that of others. 93% of the Americans and 69% of the Swedes put themselves in the top half. A 2020 meta-analysis by Ethan Zell and colleagues found the effect across hundreds of studies; it's strongest for traits that are vague and easy to define flatteringly.
case: In 1977 K. Patricia Cross surveyed faculty at the University of Nebraska: 94% rated themselves above-average teachers, and 68% placed themselves in the top quarter. In a 1976 College Board survey of about a million US high-school seniors, 70% rated their leadership ability above average, while only 2% rated it below average; for "ability to get along with others", essentially none rated themselves below average, and a quarter placed themselves in the top 1%. The nuance: later research shows the effect is biggest for vague, flattering traits where each person can define "good" by their own strengths (a "good driver" is careful, or fast, or confident), and smaller for concrete, measurable skills. That is why clear criteria and objective feedback are the antidote.
proof: Highly robust across countries and traits; smaller for specific, measurable skills.
spot: Listen for "most people can't do X, but I'm pretty good at it", or a team where everyone believes they're doing more than their fair share.
use: Pick one skill you think you're above average at. Find an objective measure (a test, a timer, data) and check.
guard: When splitting credit or blame, assume your sense of "my share" is inflated. Couples asked separately what percentage of chores they do often add up to well over 100%.
predict: What share of American students in Svenson's study rated their driving as better than the median driver?
- 50%, as you'd expect
- 70%
* 93%
scenario: In a team survey, nine out of ten engineers say they write cleaner code than the average engineer on the team.
source: Svenson, O. (1981). Are we all less risky and more skillful than our fellow drivers? Acta Psychologica; Zell, E., et al. (2020). Psychological Bulletin.
related: dunning-kruger, self-serving-bias, overconfidence

## self-serving-bias
title: The self-serving bias
category: self
evidence: solid
hook: When you win, it was skill. When you lose, it was the umpire.
what: We tend to credit our successes to ourselves (effort, talent) and blame our failures on circumstances (luck, other people, the situation). It protects self-esteem, but it also stops us learning from failure and makes us unfair to others.
study: A 2004 meta-analysis by Amy Mezulis and colleagues pooled 266 studies and found a large, consistent self-serving bias across ages, though it was stronger in the US and weaker in some East Asian samples, and smaller in people with depression.
case: In 1983 James Bettman and Barton Weitz analysed the letters to shareholders in US corporate annual reports. When results were good, companies credited internal causes such as strategy and management; when results were bad, they blamed external causes such as the economy, the weather or government regulation. Sports reporting shows the same pattern: Richard Lau and Dan Russell (1980) found that players and coaches explained wins mostly with their own skill and effort, and losses more often with luck, refereeing or circumstances. The nuance: in organisations, this bias corrupts learning. If failures are always "the market", nothing changes; if successes are always "our strategy", luck is mistaken for skill. Good post-mortems ask the same questions about wins and losses.
proof: Robust across hundreds of studies; its size varies by culture and mental state.
spot: Listen to how people explain results: "I got the promotion because I worked hard; he got it because he's the boss's favourite."
use: Take one recent success and one failure. For the success, write one way luck helped. For the failure, write one thing you controlled.
guard: In reviews and post-mortems, separate decisions from outcomes: was it a good decision with bad luck, or a bad decision with good luck?
predict: Across 266 studies, where was the self-serving bias weakest?
- In older adults
* In people with depression and in some East Asian samples
- In athletes and business leaders
scenario: Akash says he aced the maths test because he's naturally good with numbers, and failed the history test because the questions were unfair.
source: Mezulis, A. H., Abramson, L. Y., Hyde, J. S., & Hankin, B. L. (2004). Is there a universal positivity bias in attributions? Psychological Bulletin.
related: fundamental-attribution-error, better-than-average-effect, hindsight-bias

## choice-blindness
title: Choice blindness
category: self
evidence: good
hook: Secretly swap the photo someone picked as more attractive, and most will explain why they chose the one they didn't.
what: We believe we know why we made our choices. But when a choice is secretly reversed, most people don't notice, and they happily give detailed reasons for the option they rejected. Our explanations are often reconstructions after the fact, not readouts of what drove us.
study: In 2005 Petter Johansson, Lars Hall and colleagues showed people pairs of faces and asked which was more attractive. On some trials, a sleight of hand swapped the photos and people were asked to explain the choice they'd "made". At most about a quarter of swaps were noticed, and people explained choices they never made, citing features like earrings that only the other face had. Later studies found the same with jam, tea and political opinions.
case: Choice blindness reaches beyond faces. In 2012 Lars Hall, Petter Johansson and Thomas Strandberg asked people in a Swedish park to rate their agreement with moral statements on a clipboard survey. A hidden sticky layer secretly reversed some statements to their opposite, while keeping the participant's rating. When asked to explain their answers, many argued fluently for the reversed position: about 69% accepted at least one of the reversals. A later study did the same with political views before the Swedish election and found that people often defended manipulated answers and some shifted their stated voting intentions after reflecting on them. The nuance: this doesn't mean people have no real attitudes. It shows that our explanations are partly constructed in the moment, which makes attitudes more flexible, and more open to manipulation, than we assume.
proof: Replicated across faces, tastes, smells and moral and political attitudes, by several labs.
spot: Notice how quickly people produce reasons for choices, including ones they made on autopilot.
use: Today, after one choice (food, a purchase, a route), ask yourself: do I know why, or am I making up a reason now?
guard: When asked "why did you do that?" try "I'm not sure" before inventing a tidy story. Your felt reasons can be wrong.
predict: When the face people chose was secretly swapped, how many of the swaps did they notice?
- Nearly all of them
- About two thirds
* About a quarter at most
scenario: At a tasting stall, Aarav picks the spicier chutney. The vendor secretly hands him the mild one, and Aarav explains that he chose it because he likes some heat.
source: Johansson, P., Hall, L., Sikström, S., & Olsson, A. (2005). Failure to detect mismatches between intention and outcome in a simple decision task. Science.
related: change-blindness, cognitive-dissonance, misinformation-effect

## impostor-phenomenon
title: The impostor phenomenon
category: self
evidence: good
hook: Feeling like a fraud who'll soon be found out is common, especially among high achievers.
what: Impostor feelings are the sense that your success is due to luck or charm, not ability, and that you'll eventually be exposed. They're common in people who are in fact competent, especially in new roles or in groups where they feel different. It's a pattern of thinking, not a diagnosis.
study: Psychologists Pauline Clance and Suzanne Imes described it in 1978 among high-achieving women. A 2020 systematic review by Dena Bravata and colleagues found it in men and women across professions, with reported rates ranging from 9% to 82% depending on how it was measured, and linked to anxiety, depression and burnout.
case: In 2022 Basima Tewfik of MIT studied physicians in training. Those who reported more frequent impostor thoughts were evaluated as more interpersonally effective by standardised patients (actors trained to rate doctors), while their diagnostic accuracy was no worse than others'. In other workplaces she found people with impostor thoughts were rated as better at interpersonal skills by colleagues. Her interpretation: feeling that others overestimate you can push people to be more other-oriented: listening more, asking more questions. The nuance: this does not make impostor feelings pleasant or healthy; they are linked to anxiety, burnout and avoiding opportunities. But it complicates the idea that they are purely a defect to be eliminated, and suggests treating them as a signal to keep learning rather than as proof of fraud.
proof: Clearly real and common, but measured inconsistently, so the precise prevalence is uncertain.
spot: Listen for "I just got lucky", "they'll realise I don't know what I'm doing", or deflecting every compliment.
use: Write down three pieces of evidence for your competence, things that luck can't explain, and keep the list somewhere you'll see it.
guard: Talking about it helps: you'll usually find that people you admire feel the same. Don't let the feeling stop you applying or speaking up.
predict: How common did a 2020 review find impostor feelings to be?
- Rare; under 5% of people
* Anywhere from 9% to 82% depending on the measure
- Universal; every high achiever has them
scenario: Despite topping her batch and being promoted twice, Nandini is sure her manager will soon discover she has no idea what she's doing.
source: Clance, P. R., & Imes, S. A. (1978). Psychotherapy: Theory, Research & Practice; Bravata, D. M., et al. (2020). Journal of General Internal Medicine.
related: dunning-kruger, self-handicapping

## dunning-kruger
title: The Dunning-Kruger effect
category: self
evidence: debated
hook: The least skilled people overestimate themselves the most. But the famous "Mount Stupid" curve isn't what the data show.
what: In the original studies, people who scored worst on tests of logic, grammar and humour greatly overestimated their performance, while top scorers slightly underestimated theirs. The pop version, where beginners feel more confident than experts, goes further than the data: confidence still rises with skill, just much more slowly than skill does.
study: In 1999 Justin Kruger and David Dunning found that people scoring in the bottom quarter, around the 12th percentile, estimated they were at about the 62nd. Later researchers showed that part of this pattern appears even with random numbers, through statistical artefacts like regression to the mean, though some studies still find a real effect after correcting for it.
case: Medicine is where self-assessment matters most. In 2006 David Davis and colleagues reviewed studies in JAMA that compared physicians' self-assessments with external measures of their performance. In most comparisons there was little or no relationship, and the worst accuracy was found among the least skilled physicians and those who were most confident. This is one reason why medicine moved towards external audit, checklists and simulation-based assessment rather than relying on doctors to know their own weak spots. The nuance: even skilled people misjudge themselves (top performers often underestimate how unusual their skills are), and some of the classic pattern is statistical. The practical lesson stands either way: self-assessment needs external data.
proof: Low performers do overestimate themselves, but how much of the pattern is psychology and how much is statistics is actively debated.
spot: Notice confident opinions from people who've just started something, and also experts who assume everyone knows what they know.
use: Rate yourself from 1 to 10 at something you can test today (a quiz, a game, an estimate), then test it. Note the gap.
guard: The cure is feedback: get scored, compare your work with experts', ask for specific criticism.
predict: In the original study, people who scored around the 12th percentile estimated they were at about...
- The 15th percentile
- The 35th percentile
* The 62nd percentile
scenario: After two weeks of chess videos, Rohan tells his uncle, a club player, that he's probably better than most club players.
source: Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Unskilled and unaware of it. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Gignac, G. E., & Zajenkowski, M. (2020). Intelligence.
related: better-than-average-effect, overconfidence, impostor-phenomenon, regression-to-the-mean

## self-handicapping
title: Self-handicapping
category: self
evidence: good
hook: Partying the night before an exam gives you a ready excuse if you fail. People do it on purpose, without admitting it.
what: When we fear failing at something that reflects on our ability, we sometimes create obstacles in advance: not practising, staying up late, procrastinating. If we fail, we blame the obstacle; if we succeed, we look extra talented. It protects our self-image while making failure more likely.
study: In 1978 Steven Berglas and Edward Jones gave students either solvable or unsolvable puzzles, then told everyone they'd scored well. Before a second test, they could choose a drug that supposedly improved or impaired performance. Men whose earlier "success" felt unearned were more likely to choose the impairing drug. Later studies found that habitual self-handicappers get worse grades and feel worse over time.
case: In 1984 Frederick Rhodewalt and colleagues studied competitive swimmers and golfers. Athletes high in the tendency to self-handicap practised less before important competitions than before minor ones, the opposite of what you'd expect if they were trying to win, while low self-handicappers practised more when it mattered. A longitudinal study of students by Miron Zuckerman, Suzanne Kieffer and Raymond Knee (1998) found that self-handicapping predicted lower grades and worse adjustment over the semester, and that poor outcomes led to more self-handicapping: a vicious cycle. The nuance: self-handicapping works in the short term, protecting self-esteem, and observers do sometimes discount failure when there was an excuse. The cost is paid later, in worse performance and in never finding out what you could have done.
proof: Well documented in many settings; it reliably predicts poorer outcomes over time.
spot: Listen for excuses offered in advance: "I barely studied", "I'm so tired", "I didn't really try".
use: Name one task you've been avoiding preparing for. Do 20 minutes of real preparation today and notice what fear comes up.
guard: Ask yourself: "If I fail, do I want an excuse or a lesson?" Excuses protect your ego now and cost you later.
predict: People whose earlier success felt unearned were offered a performance-boosting or performance-impairing drug before another test. Which did more of them choose?
- The boosting drug
* The impairing drug
- They refused both
scenario: The night before his driving test, Yash stays out till 3 a.m. When he fails, he tells everyone it was because he was exhausted.
source: Berglas, S., & Jones, E. E. (1978). Drug choice as a self-handicapping strategy. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: impostor-phenomenon, self-serving-bias

## cognitive-dissonance
title: Cognitive dissonance
category: self
evidence: debated
hook: Do something that clashes with your beliefs and you may change the belief to match. The classic proof just failed a big replication.
what: Holding two conflicting thoughts, like "I'm a good person" and "I just lied", is uncomfortable, so we reduce the tension, often by changing an attitude to fit what we did. The idea is one of psychology's most famous, and explains a lot of everyday rationalising, but the experiment it's best known for has not held up well.
study: In 1959 Leon Festinger and James Carlsmith paid students $1 or $20 to tell the next participant that a dull task was fun. Those paid $1, with little external justification, later rated the task as more enjoyable. In 2024 a registered replication by David Vaidis and colleagues, run in dozens of labs with thousands of participants, did not reproduce the key effect of choice on attitude change.
case: In 1968 Robert Knox and James Inkster interviewed bettors at a Vancouver racetrack, either just before or just after they placed a $2 bet. On a seven-point scale, those who had just bet rated their horse's chances as better (about 4.8) than those about to bet (about 3.5), even though nothing about the horse had changed: having committed, they believed in their choice more. Festinger's earlier fieldwork with a group predicting the end of the world (described in When Prophecy Fails, 1956) reported that some members became more committed after the prophecy failed. The nuance: the effect of decisions on confidence is well documented, but the classic lab paradigm failed a large 2024 replication, and other explanations (such as self-perception and impression management) fit some of the evidence. Treat dissonance as a powerful description of rationalising, with the mechanism still debated.
proof: Debated: the theory is influential and other forms of it have support, but its classic paradigm failed a large preregistered replication.
spot: Notice post-decision rationalising: "I didn't want that job anyway", "this expensive phone is actually great value".
use: Recall a decision you defended hard recently. Ask: did I believe this before I made the choice, or did I start believing it after?
guard: When you catch yourself praising a choice you've already made, look for the downsides you might be skipping over.
predict: In 2024, dozens of labs repeated the classic "paid to lie" dissonance experiment. What did they find?
- The same effect, even stronger
* No reliable effect of choice on attitude change
- The effect only in men
scenario: After failing to get into the university he wanted, Sameer starts telling everyone it was overrated and full of snobs anyway.
source: Festinger, L., & Carlsmith, J. M. (1959). Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology; Vaidis, D. C., et al. (2024). Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science.
related: self-perception, choice-blindness, sunk-cost-fallacy

## self-perception
title: Self-perception
category: self
evidence: good
hook: Sometimes we work out what we feel by watching what we do. "I keep going to the gym, so I must be a gym person."
what: Daryl Bem proposed that when our inner states are vague, we infer them the way an observer would: from our behaviour. If you find yourself volunteering, you conclude you care about the cause. This means small actions can reshape identity: act first, and the self-image often follows.
