hook: Telling people to "be unbiased" does almost nothing. Asking "what if the opposite were true?" does far more.
what: Many biases come from considering only the possibility already in mind: the first number, the favoured explanation, the expected outcome. Deliberately asking how the opposite could be true brings evidence for other possibilities to mind, loosening the grip of the first one. It is one of the few debiasing strategies with consistent experimental support, and it beats good intentions.
study: In 1984 Charles Lord, Mark Lepper and Elizabeth Preston gave supporters and opponents of the death penalty mixed evidence on whether it deters crime. Each side usually rates the evidence for its own view as stronger. Instructions to be "as objective and unbiased as possible" didn't reduce this. Asking people how they would have rated the same study had it found the opposite result did. In 2000 Thomas Mussweiler, Fritz Strack and Tim Pfeiffer found that listing reasons why an anchor might be wrong also reduced anchoring.
case: Doctors face the same trap. In 2010 Sílvia Mamede and colleagues had internal-medicine residents review some cases of particular diseases, then diagnose new written cases. Second-year residents misread look-alike cases as the disease they had just seen: their accuracy on those cases was 1.55 out of 4, against 2.19 on the other cases. Then the residents went back over the cases deliberately, listing for each possible diagnosis the findings that supported it, the findings that argued against it and the findings they'd expect but couldn't see. Their accuracy on the look-alike cases rose to 2.03. The nuance: good intentions aren't the active ingredient. Telling yourself to be careful changes little; a concrete routine that makes you argue for the alternatives does.
proof: Supported across research on biased reading of evidence, anchoring, overconfidence and medical diagnosis. Most tests are short experiments, so how much it helps in everyday life is less certain.
spot: Notice moments of instant certainty: a story that explains everything, a first estimate you're comfortable with, a person you've already labelled.
use: Pick one belief you held firmly today and write down two concrete reasons it might be wrong.
guard: When a decision matters, ask "If the opposite turned out to be true, what would be the most likely reason?" before you commit, and have each person in a group do it separately.
predict: Supporters and opponents of the death penalty rated mixed evidence. What reduced their bias toward their own side?
- Being told to be as objective and unbiased as possible
* Being asked how they'd rate the same study if it had found the opposite
- Being told that experts would check their reasoning
scenario: Before signing the lease, Farah makes herself write down two ways the cheap flat could turn out to be a bad deal. One of them, the long commute in monsoon traffic, changes her mind.
source: Lord, C. G., Lepper, M. R., & Preston, E. (1984). Considering the opposite: A corrective strategy for social judgment. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Mussweiler, T., Strack, F., & Pfeiffer, T. (2000). Overcoming the inevitable anchoring effect. Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin; Mamede, S., et al. (2010). Effect of availability bias and reflective reasoning on diagnostic accuracy among internal medicine residents. JAMA.
related: confirmation-bias, anchoring, hindsight-bias, premortem, availability-heuristic

# =====================================================================================
# INFLUENCE & PERSUASION
# =====================================================================================

## reciprocity
title: Reciprocity
category: influence
evidence: solid
hook: Give someone a small, unasked-for gift and they feel pulled to give back, often far more than you gave.
what: Every human culture has a rule: we repay what others give us. It underpins trust and cooperation, which is why it's so powerful, and why it can be exploited: free samples, "complimentary" consultations and small favours create a sense of debt that makes it hard to say no.
study: In 1971 Dennis Regan had an assistant either buy participants a Coke unasked or not. Later, the assistant asked them to buy raffle tickets. Those who'd received the Coke bought about twice as many tickets, even if they didn't particularly like him. In 2002 David Strohmetz and colleagues found that waiters who left one mint with the bill got about 3% higher tips, two mints 14%, and one mint followed by a personal "for you, here's another" 23%.
case: In 2007 economist Armin Falk ran a field experiment with a charity in Zurich that funds schools in Bangladesh. About 10,000 potential donors received a mailing asking for a donation. A third got no gift, a third got one postcard drawn by a child from the project, and a third got four such postcards. Compared with no gift, the small gift raised the share of people who donated by about 17%, and the large gift by about 75%. Because more people gave, total donations rose, even though the average gift per donor was slightly smaller. The nuance: the gift had to feel personal and tied to the cause, and the effect raises ethical questions in other settings, which is why many medical and government bodies restrict gifts from companies that want something back.
proof: The norm of reciprocity is one of the most robust findings in social science; individual tipping studies are small but consistent.
spot: Notice free samples, "small gifts" from salespeople, or a favour followed quickly by a request.
use: Give something small and genuine today, with no request attached: help, a compliment, a snack. Notice how people respond.
guard: When given something unexpected, ask: is this a gift or a sales technique? You can accept a gift without owing a purchase.
predict: Waiters left one mint, then turned back and gave a second "just for you". How much did tips rise?
- About 3%
- About 10%
* About 23%
scenario: After the salesman insists on giving Pooja a free shade card and a cup of chai, she feels too guilty to leave the shop without buying the paint.
source: Regan, D. T. (1971). Effects of a favor and liking on compliance. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology; Strohmetz, D. B., et al. (2002). Journal of Applied Social Psychology.
related: door-in-the-face, even-a-penny

## foot-in-the-door
title: Foot in the door
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: Get someone to agree to something tiny, and they become more likely to agree to something big.
what: Once we've agreed to a small request, we start seeing ourselves as the kind of person who supports that cause, and saying yes to a bigger request keeps us consistent with that image. Charities, petitions and sales funnels all start small for this reason.
study: In 1966 Jonathan Freedman and Scott Fraser asked homeowners to put a large, ugly "Drive Carefully" sign in their front yards. About 17% agreed. But among homeowners who had, two weeks earlier, agreed to display a tiny 3-inch "Be a safe driver" sticker, 76% agreed. A 1999 review by Jerry Burger found the technique works reliably, though usually with smaller effects.
case: In Freedman and Fraser's first 1966 experiment, researchers phoned housewives in California, posing as a consumer group. Some were first asked to answer a few short questions about the soap products they used. Three days later, they were asked a much bigger favour: to let a team of five or six men into their home for two hours to catalogue all their household products. About 53% of those who had answered the questions agreed, compared with about 22% of women asked only the big request. Later research found the effect is reliable but usually smaller, and works best when the first request is actually carried out, when it's about the same cause, and when people see themselves as the kind of person who helps. The nuance: the first step works through self-image, so it fails when the first request is so tiny it says nothing about who you are.
proof: Supported by decades of studies; real but usually more modest than the famous example.
spot: Watch for a sequence of escalating asks: "Just sign this", then "Could you share it?", then "Would you donate?"
use: If you want to build a habit, start with an absurdly small version today (one push-up, one page) and notice how it changes your sense of yourself.
guard: When a request follows a small one you agreed to, judge it on its own: "Would I agree to this if it were the first thing asked?"
predict: Homeowners had earlier agreed to show a tiny safe-driving sticker. What share then agreed to a large, ugly yard sign?
- About 17%, the same as everyone else
- About 40%
* About 76%
scenario: First the gym asks Rahul to fill in a short fitness survey, then to take a free trial session, and by the end of the trial he signs a one-year contract.
source: Freedman, J. L., & Fraser, S. C. (1966). Compliance without pressure. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Burger, J. M. (1999). Personality and Social Psychology Review.
related: low-ball, self-perception

## social-proof
title: Social proof
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: When unsure what to do, we copy what others like us are doing. Hotel guests reuse towels when told others do.
what: In uncertain situations, other people's behaviour feels like information about what's right. It's why crowded restaurants seem better, reviews matter, and "bestseller" labels sell. The more similar the others are to us, the stronger the pull.
study: In 2008 Noah Goldstein, Robert Cialdini and Vladas Griskevicius tested signs in hotel bathrooms. A standard environmental message got about 35% of guests to reuse their towels. "Most guests at this hotel reuse their towels" raised it to about 44%, and "most guests who stayed in this room" to about 49%. Some later replications found smaller or no effects, but social norm messages generally work.
case: In 2017 Michael Hallsworth and colleagues worked with the UK tax authority, HMRC, on letters to people who hadn't paid their income tax on time. Standard reminders were compared with versions adding social norms such as "Nine out of ten people in the UK pay their tax on time. You are currently in the very small minority of people who have not paid us yet." The norm messages raised the share of people who paid within 23 days by several percentage points, worth millions of pounds in earlier revenue. The most effective versions were the most specific: norms about people in the recipient's own area, or framing non-payment as being in a small minority. The nuance: social proof works best when the norm is true, local and about people like the reader; saying "lots of people don't pay" would have normalised the opposite behaviour.
proof: Social norm effects are well supported; individual studies like the towel one vary in how well they replicate.
spot: Look for "most popular", "4.8 stars from 20,000 people", "trending", or queues outside a shop.
use: If you want people to do something today (pay on time, recycle, reply), mention that most people similar to them already do it.
guard: Ask: are these others really like me, and do they actually know better? A crowd can be wrong, and fake reviews are cheap.
predict: Which hotel sign got the most guests to reuse towels?
- "Help save the environment"
- "Most guests at this hotel reuse their towels"
* "Most guests who stayed in this room reuse their towels"
scenario: Choosing between two identical dhabas on the highway, Gaurav picks the one with ten trucks parked outside.
source: Goldstein, N. J., Cialdini, R. B., & Griskevicius, V. (2008). A room with a viewpoint. Journal of Consumer Research.
related: norm-boomerang, asch-conformity, pluralistic-ignorance

## because-justification
title: The power of "because"
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: "Can I cut in line because I have to make copies?" worked almost as well as a real reason.
what: Requests with a reason work better, and for small requests, almost any reason will do. Hearing "because" triggers a mental shortcut: a reason exists, so it's probably fine. For bigger requests, people actually listen to the reason, and a meaningless one stops working.
study: In 1978 Ellen Langer, Arthur Blank and Benzion Chanowitz had a person ask to cut in line at a photocopier. For a small job, 60% agreed with no reason, 94% with a real reason ("because I'm in a rush") and 93% with an empty one ("because I have to make copies"). For a bigger job, the empty reason did no better than none (24%), while the real reason still helped (42%).
case: In 1985 Valerie Folkes repeated Langer's photocopier study with different settings and found a more cautious picture. Real reasons generally worked better than empty ones, and whether people complied "mindlessly" depended on how much the request cost them and how much attention they were paying. Later field studies of requests in shops and on the street also found that giving a reason helps, but that people often do notice whether it's a good one. The nuance: the classic study is real, but the popular version, that "because" works like a magic word, overstates it. Reasons work best when they're genuine; empty reasons only slip through for small, low-cost requests made to distracted people, and they can backfire when noticed.
proof: Well known and partially replicated; the important nuance is that empty reasons only work for small requests.
spot: Notice requests or ads where the "because" adds no real information: "Buy now because you deserve it."
use: Add a genuine "because" to one request today, and make it a real reason.
guard: For anything that costs you, stop at "because" and ask: is that actually a reason?
predict: For a big request (20 pages to copy), how did an empty reason ("because I have to make copies") do?
- As well as a real reason
* No better than giving no reason
- Worse than giving no reason
scenario: At the bank, a man asks Lata, "Can I go ahead of you? Because I need to deposit this cheque." She steps aside before realising everyone in the queue is there to deposit cheques.
source: Langer, E., Blank, A., & Chanowitz, B. (1978). The mindlessness of ostensibly thoughtful action. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: authority

## door-in-the-face
title: Door in the face
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: Ask for something huge, get refused, then ask for what you really wanted. People feel they should meet you halfway.
what: When someone retreats from a large request to a smaller one, it feels like a concession, and reciprocity says concessions should be returned. The smaller request also looks modest by comparison. Negotiators and fundraisers use it constantly.
study: In 1975 Robert Cialdini and colleagues asked students to chaperone a group of juvenile offenders on a two-hour zoo trip. Only about 17% agreed. But if they first asked students to volunteer two hours a week for two years (everyone refused), then asked about the zoo trip, 50% said yes. A later meta-analysis found the technique reliably increases agreement, but has less effect on whether people actually follow through.
case: In 1976 Robert Cialdini and Karen Ascani applied the technique to blood donation on a university campus. Some students were first asked to commit to donating blood every six weeks for at least three years. When they refused, they were asked to donate just once, the next day. Others were asked only for the single donation. Students who had refused the large request first were more likely to agree to donate once, and also more likely to actually turn up and give blood. A 2012 meta-analysis by Thomas Feeley and colleagues found that the technique reliably increases agreement, but has a much weaker effect on whether people follow through. The nuance: it works best when the same person makes both requests, the request is for a good cause, and the second request follows straight after the first. Delays and obvious manipulation weaken it.
proof: Reliable for getting a "yes"; weaker for actual follow-through.
spot: Watch for a big ask quickly followed by a "well, how about just..." offer.
use: Next time you negotiate today, notice whether the first offer is designed to be refused, and respond to the second on its merits.
guard: Judge the second request on its own. Ask: "Would I have agreed to this if it were the first thing asked?"
predict: Students first refused to volunteer for two years, then were asked to chaperone a zoo trip. How many agreed?
- About 17%, the same as when asked directly
* About 50%
- About 90%
scenario: Rohan asks his dad for a new laptop for ₹1.2 lakh. When his dad refuses, Rohan "settles" for the ₹25,000 headphones he wanted all along.
source: Cialdini, R. B., et al. (1975). Reciprocal concessions procedure for inducing compliance. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: reciprocity, contrast-principle, anchoring

## scarcity
title: Scarcity
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: Cookies from a nearly empty jar taste better than the same cookies from a full one.
what: Things seem more valuable when they're rare or running out, partly because scarcity signals that others want them, and partly because we hate losing options. "Only 2 left", "offer ends tonight" and "limited edition" all work this way.
study: In 1975 Stephen Worchel, Jerry Lee and Akanbi Adewole gave people a cookie from a jar holding either 10 or 2 cookies. The same cookies were rated more desirable from the jar of 2. They were rated highest of all when the jar went from 10 to 2 because other people had wanted them.
case: Retailers use limits such as "maximum 4 per customer" and "today only". In 1997 Jeffrey Inman, Anil Peter and Priya Raghubir found that deals with restrictions, such as purchase limits or time limits, were seen as more valuable and increased purchases of the deal, especially for shoppers who weren't actively searching for deals. Restrictions signal that the deal is good enough to need rationing. Another classic, often-cited example comes from a beef importer described by Robert Cialdini: customers told about an impending shortage ordered more, and those told the information was exclusive ordered even more. That example came from a student's unpublished dissertation, so treat the size of the effect with caution. The nuance: scarcity cues work best when they seem to reflect real demand; obviously fake countdown timers can damage trust.
proof: Scarcity effects are well supported, especially when scarcity comes from demand; the cookie study itself is small.
spot: Look for "only 3 rooms left at this price", countdown timers and "last day of the sale" banners, especially ones that reset.
use: The next time you feel urgency to buy today, wait 24 hours. Note whether you still want it.
guard: Ask: would I want this if there were plenty of it? Check whether the scarcity is real (refresh the page, check tomorrow).
predict: When were identical cookies rated most desirable?
- From a full jar of 10
- From a jar that always had 2
* From a jar that went from 10 to 2 because others wanted them
scenario: The booking site says "Only 1 room left! 14 people are looking at this hotel." Aditi books instantly, and later finds plenty of rooms at the same price.
source: Worchel, S., Lee, J., & Adewole, A. (1975). Effects of supply and demand on ratings of object value. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: loss-aversion, reactance

## authority
title: Authority
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: 21 of 22 nurses started to give a dangerous overdose because a "doctor" phoned. Context changed that dramatically.
what: We defer to experts, titles and uniforms, usually sensibly, because they often know more. But the symbols of authority (a white coat, "Dr", a confident voice) can trigger obedience even when the request is wrong or the authority is fake.
study: In 1966 Charles Hofling and colleagues had an unknown "doctor" phone nurses and order a dose of an unfamiliar drug at twice the maximum on the label. 21 of 22 nurses began to give it before being stopped. But in 1977 Rank and Jacobson repeated it with a familiar drug and let nurses consult colleagues: only 2 of 18 complied. Authority is strong, but knowledge and the chance to check weaken it a lot.
case: In 1977, at Tenerife airport, a KLM jumbo jet captained by the airline's chief flight instructor began its take-off run in fog without clearance and collided with a Pan Am jet on the runway, killing 583 people: the deadliest accident in aviation history. The cockpit voice recorder showed the flight engineer asked whether the Pan Am plane was clear of the runway; the captain emphatically said it was, and the take-off continued. Investigators concluded that deference to a highly senior captain played a part. The industry responded with Crew Resource Management training, which teaches junior crew to speak up with specific, escalating language and captains to invite challenge. The nuance: obedience isn't only about bad commands from bad people; it also suppresses accurate doubts in front of trusted experts.
proof: The pull of authority is well documented; how strong it is depends heavily on context, as the replication shows.
spot: Notice "doctor recommended", "as seen on TV", uniforms in ads, and people who drop their titles in arguments.
use: When someone with authority tells you something today, ask one polite checking question: "What's the reasoning?" or "Where can I read about that?"
guard: Ask: is this person an authority on this specific topic? Is the authority real? Can I check with someone else before I act?
predict: When nurses were given a familiar drug and could consult colleagues, how many followed an unsafe phone order?
- Most of them, as in the original
- About half
* Only 2 out of 18
scenario: A caller says he's from the "bank's fraud department" and needs Sunil's OTP to block a suspicious transaction. His confident, official tone makes Sunil almost read it out.
source: Hofling, C. K., et al. (1966). An experimental study in nurse-physician relationships. Journal of Nervous and Mental Disease; Rank, S. G., & Jacobson, C. K. (1977). Journal of Health and Social Behavior.
related: milgram-obedience, because-justification

## low-ball
title: The low-ball technique
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: Get a "yes" first, then reveal the real cost. Most people stick with their decision.
what: Once we've committed to a choice, we generate reasons why it's good. If the price then rises, those reasons stay, and backing out feels inconsistent. Car dealers, airlines with fees revealed at checkout and "drip pricing" all use it.
study: In 1978 Robert Cialdini and colleagues invited students to a study. When the 7 a.m. start time was mentioned upfront, only 24% turned up. When students were asked first and told about the 7 a.m. start only after agreeing (with the option to back out), 53% turned up. Later studies confirmed the effect works best when the initial commitment is freely made.
case: In 1981 Jerry Burger and Richard Petty tested why the low-ball works. Students agreed to take part in an experiment in exchange for extra course credit, and were then told the credit was no longer available. If the same experimenter made the new request, many still went ahead. If a different person made the request, the effect largely disappeared. Their conclusion: people feel an obligation to the person they made a commitment to, not just to the decision itself. In real sales, the technique shows up as prices that rise after agreement, and consumer-protection regulators in several countries have targeted "drip pricing", where extra fees appear at checkout, for this reason. The nuance: once you've said yes, backing out feels like breaking a promise to a person, so a useful defence is to notice that the deal you agreed to no longer exists.
proof: Replicated in several studies and supported by later meta-analysis.
spot: Watch for prices that grow at checkout ("convenience fee", "platform fee"), or terms that change after you've agreed.
use: When booking anything today, check the total price at the very end and ask: would I have said yes to this price at the start?
guard: Treat a changed deal as a new deal. It's fine to walk away, even after saying yes.
predict: Students agreed to a study, then learned it started at 7 a.m. How many turned up, compared with 24% told upfront?
- About 24%, the same
* About 53%
- About 85%
scenario: The flight looked like ₹3,200, but by checkout seat selection, a convenience fee and baggage push it to ₹5,600. Having already chosen it, Hemant pays.
source: Cialdini, R. B., Cacioppo, J. T., Bassett, R., & Miller, J. A. (1978). Low-ball procedure for producing compliance. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: foot-in-the-door, sunk-cost-fallacy

## norm-boomerang
title: When social proof backfires
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: "Many visitors steal petrified wood" more than quadrupled the theft. Telling people a bad thing is common makes it normal.
what: Messages meant to alarm people about a bad behaviour often say, by accident, that lots of people do it. That makes it feel normal. And telling people they're better than average can make them slip back towards the average. A sign of approval can prevent that.
study: In 2003 Robert Cialdini and colleagues placed signs in Arizona's Petrified Forest National Park. A sign saying many past visitors had removed wood led to 7.92% of planted pieces being stolen, compared with 1.67% under a sign that simply asked people not to. In 2007 Wesley Schultz and colleagues showed households their energy use compared with neighbours: low users increased their use, unless the report added a smiley face.
case: Opower turned the Schultz study into a business. Starting in 2008, US utilities sent millions of households Home Energy Reports comparing their electricity use with that of similar neighbours, plus smiley faces and tips. In 2011 economist Hunt Allcott analysed randomised trials with about 600,000 households and found the reports cut energy use by about 2% on average, an effect similar to a short-term electricity price increase of 11 to 20%, at very low cost. The effects were larger for heavy users and persisted as long as reports continued. The nuance: the "boomerang" for already-efficient households was dampened by the approval signal, and the effect decayed between reports, then bounced back when the next report arrived. Social comparison works, but it needs constant reminders.
proof: Supported by field experiments; the general principle (descriptive norms can backfire) is well established.
spot: Look for messages like "Everyone cheats on their taxes" or "Millions don't vote", which normalise what they want to stop.
use: Next time you want someone to do the right thing today, point out how common it is, not how common the wrong thing is.
guard: When a public message says "everyone does X", ask whether that's meant to persuade you towards X.
predict: In the Petrified Forest, how did a sign saying many visitors took wood affect theft, compared with a simple "please don't"?
- It halved theft
- No difference
* Theft was more than four times higher
scenario: A notice in Divya's office says, "Most employees leave their monitors on overnight, wasting power!" The next week, even more monitors are left on.
source: Cialdini, R. B. (2003). Crafting normative messages to protect the environment. Current Directions in Psychological Science; Schultz, P. W., et al. (2007). Psychological Science.
related: social-proof, pluralistic-ignorance

## but-you-are-free
title: "But you are free"
category: influence
evidence: debated
hook: Adding "you're free to say no" to a request was said to make people far more likely to say yes. The evidence turned out to be much shakier.
what: The idea: explicitly reminding people they can refuse lowers the sense of pressure, so they feel less need to resist and reward the respect with cooperation. Early studies reported big effects, especially face to face. But most of the research comes from small studies, much of it from one research group, and the most careful studies show little or no effect.
study: In 2000 Nicolas Guéguen and Alexandre Pascual had a researcher ask strangers for money for a bus fare. Adding "but you are free to accept or refuse" raised the share who gave from 10% to 47.5%. A 2013 meta-analysis by Christopher Carpenter of 42 studies found the technique reliably increases agreement, especially when people decide on the spot.
case: Christopher Carpenter's 2013 meta-analysis pooled 42 studies, many of them by Guéguen and Pascual, and found that a reminder of freedom reliably raised compliance, most of all for face-to-face requests decided on the spot. Ten years later a re-examination, co-written by Alexandre Pascual, who ran the original study, pooled 74 results and again found a sizeable average. But most of the studies were small, there were strong signs that unsuccessful ones had gone unpublished, and some had been publicly questioned for possible data problems; several of Guéguen's papers have since been retracted. The seven studies judged at low risk of bias showed no clear effect. The nuance: telling people they're free to refuse is respectful and costs nothing, so it's still worth saying, but don't count on it to raise your yes rate.
proof: Debated: a 2013 meta-analysis found a reliable effect, but a 2023 re-examination found strong signs of selective publishing, and the few studies at low risk of bias showed no clear effect.
spot: Notice persuasive messages that emphasise your choice ("no pressure, decide for yourself") and whether you feel more inclined as a result.
use: When asking someone for something today, add an honest "and it's completely fine to say no".
guard: Being told you're free doesn't make a request good. Decide based on the request, not the respectful framing.
predict: Asking strangers for bus money, how did adding "but you are free to accept or refuse" change the share who gave?
- From 10% to 12%
- From 10% to 25%
* From 10% to about 47%
scenario: Asking colleagues to join the charity run, Simran ends each message with "totally your call though, no pressure", and is convinced that's why twice as many sign up as last year.
source: Guéguen, N., & Pascual, A. (2000). Evocation of freedom and compliance. Current Research in Social Psychology; Carpenter, C. J. (2013). A meta-analysis of the effectiveness of the "but you are free" compliance-gaining technique. Communication Studies; Fillon, A. A., Souchet, L., Pascual, A., & Girandola, F. (2023). The effectiveness of the "but-you-are-free" technique: Meta-analysis and re-examination of the technique. Meta-Psychology.
related: reactance

## thats-not-all
title: "That's not all!"
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: A deal sweetened while you're still deciding sells better than the same deal offered upfront.
what: When a seller adds a bonus or drops the price before you've answered ("wait, I'll throw in this too"), it feels like a concession, triggering reciprocity, and the deal looks better compared with the first offer. TV shopping channels are built on it.
study: In 1986 Jerry Burger sold cupcakes at a campus bake sale. When customers were told upfront that a cupcake and two cookies cost 75 cents, 40% bought. When they were told a cupcake cost 75 cents, and then, before they could reply, that two cookies came with it, 73% bought.
case: In 1999 Jerry Burger and colleagues explored the limits of the technique. When the starting price was reasonable, adding a bonus or lowering the price during the pitch increased sales. But when the starting price was unreasonably high, the that's-not-all approach didn't help, and could even reduce sales: shoppers who had already dismissed the offer didn't reconsider. Burger's explanation is that the technique works when people are still undecided and thinking casually; a sweetener feels like a concession worth returning. The nuance: TV shopping channels pair it with countdown clocks and limited stock, combining several techniques. Its effect fades when people are paying close attention, so a defence is to slow down and evaluate the final package as if it had been the first offer.
proof: Replicated by Burger and others; it works less well when the starting price is absurdly high.
spot: Look for "but wait, there's more", free gifts added during a pitch, and discounts that appear while you hesitate.
use: Watch a shopping channel or sales video for five minutes today. Count the "that's not all" moments.
guard: When an offer changes while you're deciding, decide on the final package alone: would you buy this at this price if it had been offered this way from the start?
predict: Same cupcake, same cookies, same price. How many bought when the cookies were added after the price was given?
- 40%, the same as upfront
- 55%
* 73%
scenario: As Vinod hesitates over the mixer-grinder, the salesman says, "OK, for you I'll add a free jar and two years' extra warranty." Vinod buys it.
source: Burger, J. M. (1986). Increasing compliance by improving the deal. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: door-in-the-face, reciprocity, contrast-principle

## reactance
title: Reactance
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: Ban something and people want it more. Tell people not to do it and some will do it just to prove they're free.
what: When we feel our freedom is threatened, we push back: we want the forbidden thing more and resist the pressure. Heavy-handed rules, strict parents and pushy salespeople can all produce the opposite of what they want.
study: Jack Brehm described reactance in 1966. When Miami banned phosphate laundry detergents in the 1970s, Michael Mazis found Miami residents rated them as more effective than people in a city without a ban, and some smuggled them in. In 1976 James Pennebaker and Deborah Sanders found more graffiti under a "Do not write on these walls under any circumstances" sign than under a mild "Please don't write on these walls".
case: In 1999 the tobacco company Philip Morris launched a youth anti-smoking campaign, "Think. Don't Smoke". At about the same time, the American Legacy Foundation launched "truth", which portrayed tobacco companies as manipulating teenagers and invited young people to rebel against them. In 2002 Matthew Farrelly and colleagues surveyed teenagers and found that exposure to "truth" ads was linked to less intention to smoke, while exposure to the Philip Morris ads was linked to more openness to smoking. Telling teenagers what not to do triggered resistance, while giving them a reason to resist someone else's manipulation recruited reactance for health. The nuance: the studies were observational and the campaigns differed in many ways. Still, it's a vivid example of how messages that threaten autonomy can backfire, and how framing can redirect the same rebellious energy.
proof: Reactance is well documented, though the size depends on how strongly freedom is threatened.
spot: Notice "you can't have it" making you want it, or teenagers doing exactly what they're told not to.
use: When you need someone to do something today, offer two acceptable options instead of one order: "Would you rather do it now or after lunch?"
guard: If you suddenly want something because it's forbidden or restricted, notice the push. You may want your freedom more than the thing itself.
predict: Which sign attracted more graffiti?
- "Please don't write on these walls"
* "Do not write on these walls under any circumstances"
- Both the same
scenario: When the school bans a particular brand of sticker, every student in Class 7 suddenly wants one.
source: Brehm, J. W. (1966). A Theory of Psychological Reactance; Mazis, M. B. (1975). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Pennebaker, J. W., & Sanders, D. Y. (1976). Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin.
related: scarcity, but-you-are-free, ironic-process

## even-a-penny
title: "Even a penny will help"
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: Making the smallest contribution acceptable gets more people to give, and they rarely give the minimum.
what: Many people refuse requests because they feel they can't give enough, or have a ready excuse. Saying that even a tiny contribution counts removes the excuse, and once people decide to give, they give a normal amount.
study: In 1976 Robert Cialdini and David Schroeder went door to door collecting for the American Cancer Society. With the standard request, about 29% gave. Adding "even a penny will help" raised it to 50%, and the average donation was about the same, so total money raised went up.
case: A 2008 meta-analysis by Kyle Andrews and colleagues pooled studies of "legitimising paltry contributions", messages like "even a penny will help". They found it reliably increased the number of people who gave, with little effect on the average size of each gift. Crowdfunding and charity pages now often show low suggested amounts to lower the barrier. But follow-up research shows complications: showing a very low anchor can sometimes pull average donations down, and the technique works best for face-to-face, one-off appeals where people would otherwise refuse out of embarrassment at giving little. The nuance: the technique removes an excuse ("I can't give enough to matter") rather than changing what people value, so it works best when combined with a clear explanation of what the money achieves.
proof: Replicated in several later studies and supported by a meta-analysis.
spot: Notice "even ₹10 helps", "just 5 minutes of your time", or "even one rupee counts" on donation boxes and forms.
use: If you're asking for help today, make the smallest step explicit: "Even five minutes of feedback would help."
guard: Small asks are fine, but notice when "just a little" is the first step in a bigger plan (see foot-in-the-door).
predict: When fundraisers added "even a penny will help", what happened to the average donation from those who gave?
- It dropped to almost nothing
* It stayed about the same
- It doubled
scenario: The crowdfunding page for a stray-animal shelter says, "Even ₹10 feeds a dog for a day." Arvind, who'd scrolled past many appeals, gives ₹500.
source: Cialdini, R. B., & Schroeder, D. A. (1976). Increasing compliance by legitimizing paltry contributions. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: foot-in-the-door, reciprocity

## contrast-principle
title: The contrast principle
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: A ₹2,000 tie looks cheap after a ₹40,000 suit. Everything is judged against what came just before.
what: We judge things by comparison, not in absolute terms. A lukewarm bath feels hot after cold water; a flat looks spacious after a cramped one. Salespeople sell the expensive item first, estate agents show a bad flat before the good one, and menus place an absurdly priced dish to make the rest look reasonable.
study: Perceptual contrast is one of the oldest findings in psychology, and experiments on decisions show the same pattern. In 1992 Itamar Simonson and Amos Tversky found that people's choices between two options shift depending on which other options are shown alongside, including ones nobody would choose.
case: In 1992 Itamar Simonson and Amos Tversky asked people to choose between microwave ovens. One group chose between a basic Emerson model and a mid-range Panasonic. Another group chose among the same two plus a more expensive, higher-end Panasonic. Adding the expensive option, which few people chose, increased the share choosing the mid-range Panasonic: it now looked like a sensible compromise rather than the pricey option. This "compromise effect" is why menus and product lines often include a premium option mainly to make the middle one look reasonable. The nuance: contrast effects are strongest when you don't have a clear idea of what you need or how much things should cost. Deciding your requirements and budget before seeing the options is the best defence.
proof: Contrast effects are well established in perception and choice; specific sales-floor tactics are mostly documented observationally.
spot: Notice the first price you're shown in a shop or on a site, and whether it makes the next feel cheaper.
use: When choosing something today, compare each option with your budget or needs instead of with the option next to it.
guard: Reset your reference point: ask "what would I think of this price if it were the only option?"
predict: In choice experiments, what happens when an extra option that nobody picks is added to a pair?
- Nothing; people ignore irrelevant options
* Choices between the original two shift
- People refuse to choose
scenario: The broker first shows Kavita two cramped flats at ₹30,000, then a merely average one for ₹28,000, which suddenly feels like a great find.
source: Simonson, I., & Tversky, A. (1992). Choice in context. Journal of Marketing Research; Cialdini, R. B. (2001). Influence: Science and Practice.
related: anchoring, decoy-effect, door-in-the-face, thats-not-all

## inoculation
title: Inoculation against misinformation
aka: Prebunking
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: A weakened dose of a manipulation trick, explained in advance, helps people resist the real thing.
what: Like a vaccine, exposure to a weakened form of a persuasion technique, plus an explanation of how it works, builds resistance to later attacks. "Prebunking" teaches people to recognise tactics like fake experts, scapegoating and emotional manipulation, before they meet them.
study: William McGuire proposed inoculation theory in the 1960s. In 2019 Jon Roozenbeek and Sander van der Linden found that playing "Bad News", a game where you create fake news, made people better at spotting manipulative headlines. In 2022 they showed short prebunking videos as YouTube ads to millions of users and found viewers were better at identifying manipulation techniques.
case: In 2022 Jon Roozenbeek, Sander van der Linden and colleagues worked with Google's Jigsaw unit to test 90-second "prebunking" videos explaining common manipulation techniques, such as emotional language, false dichotomies and scapegoating, using examples from popular culture rather than real political claims. In lab studies, the videos improved people's ability to spot those techniques. They then ran the videos as ads on YouTube in the US, and viewers were better than a control group at recognising the techniques in a follow-up question within the following day. Google later ran prebunking campaigns about refugees in several Central European countries. The nuance: the effects were modest and faded over time without "booster" doses, and recognising a technique doesn't always change what people believe or share. But prebunking scales cheaply and avoids arguing about specific facts.
proof: Supported by many experiments and field trials, though effects are modest and fade without boosters.
spot: Notice when you can name a manipulation tactic in a message: false choice, fake expert, outrage, scapegoat.
use: Teach one manipulation trick to a family member today (for example, "they use fake experts in white coats") and ask them to spot it in a forward.
guard: Inoculation fades. Refresh your knowledge, and watch for new tactics, especially in health and money scams.
predict: What happened when people played a game where they created fake news?
- They believed fake news more
* They got better at spotting manipulative headlines
- No measurable effect
scenario: After watching a short video about "fake urgency" scams, Shweta immediately recognises a message saying her electricity will be cut "in 2 hours" unless she clicks a link.
source: McGuire, W. J. (1961). Sociometry; Roozenbeek, J., & van der Linden, S. (2019). Palgrave Communications; Roozenbeek, J., et al. (2022). Science Advances.
related: illusory-truth-effect, backfire-effect

## identifiable-victim
title: The identifiable victim effect
category: influence
evidence: debated
hook: One named child moves us more than millions of statistics, and adding the statistics can reduce giving.
what: A single, identifiable person with a face and a name triggers emotion; large numbers feel abstract. Charities therefore feature one child's story. Bizarrely, adding statistics to the story can make people give less, as if it switches on calculating mode.
study: In 2007 Deborah Small, George Loewenstein and Paul Slovic gave people a chance to donate after reading about Rokia, a seven-year-old girl in Mali, or about statistics on millions of people facing hunger. They gave on average $2.38 to Rokia and $1.14 for the statistics. When Rokia's story came with the statistics, giving fell to $1.43. A 2016 meta-analysis found the effect is real but small and inconsistent across studies.
case: In 1987, 18-month-old Jessica McClure fell into a well in Texas. As the 58-hour rescue was broadcast live, Americans sent hundreds of thousands of dollars in donations to her family. Meanwhile, appeals for statistical victims, such as thousands of children dying each day from preventable diseases, struggled to raise money. In the Rokia study, Deborah Small and colleagues also tested whether teaching people about this bias would help. It didn't increase giving to statistical victims; it reduced giving to the identifiable one. The nuance: the effect in controlled studies is small and inconsistent, but it shapes fundraising and media coverage. Some charities now combine a single named person with clear information about scale, to keep both the emotional connection and the sense of impact.
proof: Debated: the effect exists but is smaller and more variable than the famous study suggests.
spot: Notice campaigns built around one face and name, and how your feelings change between a story and a statistic.
use: When you read about a big problem today, find one real person's story within it, then look up the number. Notice the difference in how each feels.
guard: Don't let a single vivid story override the numbers when deciding where help does the most good.
predict: How did adding statistics to the story of one girl affect donations?
- Donations went up
* Donations went down
- No change
scenario: After seeing a video about one injured street dog, Megha donates ₹2,000, though she had ignored a report about thousands of animals in need the day before.
source: Small, D. A., Loewenstein, G., & Slovic, P. (2007). Sympathy and callousness. Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes; Lee, S., & Feeley, T. H. (2016). Social Influence.
related: availability-heuristic

## behavioural-priming
title: Behavioural priming (the Florida effect)
aka: Social priming
category: influence
evidence: busted
hook: Reading words like "Florida" and "bingo" was said to make people walk more slowly. The famous finding didn't survive replication.
what: Behavioural priming was the idea that subtle cues, such as words about old age, a picture of money or a briefcase on the table, change what people do without their noticing: walking slower, acting more selfishly, competing harder. It featured in bestsellers including Thinking, Fast and Slow and Pre-Suasion. Large replications have mostly failed. Priming in the narrow sense, where seeing one word makes a related word quicker to recognise, is real; dramatic effects on behaviour are not established.
study: In 1996 John Bargh, Mark Chen and Lara Burrows reported that students who unscrambled sentences containing words linked to old age, such as "Florida" and "bingo", walked more slowly down a corridor afterwards. Each experiment had about 30 participants. In 2012 Stéphane Doyen and colleagues timed walking with infrared sensors and found no effect, except when the experimenters themselves expected participants to walk slowly, which suggests the original result may have come from the experimenters, not the words.
case: Daniel Kahneman devoted a section of Thinking, Fast and Slow to "the marvels of priming" and told readers that "disbelief is not an option". In 2012, as replications failed, Kahneman wrote an open letter warning priming researchers of "a train wreck looming" and urging them to replicate each other's work. In 2017, responding to an analysis showing that the studies in that chapter were badly underpowered, Kahneman admitted to having "placed too much faith in underpowered studies" and called the evidence "significantly weaker than I believed". Money priming also failed in the large Many Labs replication. The nuance: environments do matter. Visible norms, defaults and reminders change behaviour in field experiments. What failed is the claim that tiny, unnoticed cues reliably steer what people do.
proof: Busted: the flagship studies were small, and large replications of elderly priming, money priming and similar effects have mostly found nothing.
spot: Watch for claims that a word, image, scent or object you never consciously noticed will change what you buy, how hard you work or how kind you are.
use: Find one "a subtle cue changes behaviour" claim today, in an ad, a book or a post, and check whether anyone has independently replicated it.
guard: Be most sceptical of findings that are surprising, cheap to apply and based on small studies. That is exactly the profile of the results that failed.
predict: Researchers re-ran the "old-age words make you walk slowly" study with automatic timing. When did they find slower walking?
- Every time; the effect replicated
- Never, in any condition
* Only when the experimenters expected participants to walk slowly
scenario: A sales trainer tells Aman that a framed photo of a briefcase on his desk will make clients bargain harder without noticing, so he should hide it before every negotiation.
source: Bargh, J. A., Chen, M., & Burrows, L. (1996). Automaticity of social behavior. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Doyen, S., Klein, O., Pichon, C.-L., & Cleeremans, A. (2012). Behavioral priming: It's all in the mind, but whose mind? PLoS ONE; Klein, R. A., et al. (2014). Investigating variation in replicability. Social Psychology.
related: replication-crisis, facial-feedback, power-posing

## underestimating-compliance
title: People say yes more than you think
category: influence
evidence: good
hook: People asked to get five strangers to fill in a survey expected to ask about twenty. They needed about ten.
what: When we ask for something, we picture the cost to the other person of saying yes and forget the social cost of saying no, which is awkward and feels unkind. So we underestimate how often people agree, and stay too shy to ask for help. The same pressure has a darker side: people also go along with questionable requests far more than requesters expect, because refusing face to face is hard.
study: In 2008 Francis Flynn and Vanessa Lake had participants ask strangers for small favours, such as filling in a questionnaire or lending a phone. Participants predicted they would need to ask about twice as many people as they actually did: to get five people to fill in a questionnaire, they needed to ask about ten. Across studies, people underestimated compliance by as much as 50%.
case: In 2014 Vanessa Bohns, Mahdi Roghanizad and Amy Xu tested requests people should refuse. Participants approached strangers on a university campus and asked them to write the word "pickle" in pen in what was presented as a library book. Requesters predicted that about 28% would agree. In fact more than 64% did, many while saying they felt uncomfortable. The nuance: the pressure that makes people kinder than we expect also makes them easier to talk into things. That cuts both ways. Ask for help more boldly, and when someone keeps pushing you to agree, remember that the urge to say yes is social, not a sign that yes is right.
proof: Replicated across many studies and kinds of request, and reviewed by Bohns in 2016; the effect is strongest for direct, face-to-face requests.
spot: Notice when you don't ask for help because "they'll say no", and when someone agrees to something while looking reluctant.
use: Ask one person today for something small that you'd normally talk yourself out of asking for.
guard: If someone keeps pressing after you've hesitated, treat the persistence as information about them, not about the request. You can say no without giving a reason.
predict: Requesters asked strangers to write "pickle" in a library book and predicted 28% would agree. How many did?
- About 10%
- About 30%, as predicted
* More than 64%
scenario: Tanvi needs someone to cover her Saturday shift but doesn't ask, sure everyone will refuse. When her manager finally asks around for her, the first colleague says yes straight away.
source: Flynn, F. J., & Lake, V. K. B. (2008). If you need help, just ask: Underestimating compliance with direct requests for help. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Bohns, V. K., Roghanizad, M. M., & Xu, A. Z. (2014). Underestimating our influence over others' unethical behavior and decisions. Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin; Bohns, V. K. (2016). (Mis)understanding our influence over others: A review of the underestimation-of-compliance effect. Current Directions in Psychological Science.
related: reaching-out, compliments, foot-in-the-door

## forced-teaming
title: Forced teaming and other warning signs
aka: Pre-incident indicators
category: influence
evidence: debated
hook: "We've got a hungry cat up there." A stranger who insists you're a team, puts you in their debt or won't hear no may be steering you.
what: The Gift of Fear, by security consultant Gavin de Becker, lists tactics that, according to the book, predators use to gain control: forced teaming (talking as if you're in it together), loan sharking (unasked-for help that creates a debt), charm, too many details, the unsolicited promise, typecasting ("you're probably too proud to accept help") and, above all, ignoring "no". Each rides on a well-studied influence effect such as similarity, reciprocity or consistency. What isn't established is that the list reliably picks out dangerous people.
study: The building blocks are solid. In 2004 Jerry Burger and colleagues found that when students believed they shared a birthday with a stranger, about 62% agreed to read and critique the stranger's long essay, against about 34% when the birthdays didn't match: a trivial "we" nearly doubled compliance. Reciprocity and foot-in-the-door effects are among the best replicated in social psychology. But the list comes from the author's casework, not from studies testing whether these signs predict harm in advance.
case: Hindsight makes warning signs look obvious. In 2016 Georgia Winters and Elizabeth Jeglic gave 525 students a story describing an adult's behaviour towards a child that included common grooming tactics, such as special attention, gifts and time alone. Some were told the adult went on to abuse the child; others weren't. Knowing the outcome made people overestimate how likely they were to have predicted it, while the grooming behaviours themselves often went unrecognised. The researchers later developed a validated list of grooming behaviours precisely because they are so hard to spot as they happen. The nuance: harmless salespeople and suitors use these tactics every day too. A single sign means little; the clearest signal is someone who won't accept "no".
proof: Debated: each tactic matches a well-replicated influence effect, but the claim that spotting them predicts violence rests on case histories, which are vulnerable to hindsight bias, not on studies that follow people forward.
spot: Notice unasked-for help that leaves you owing something, "we" from someone you've just met, a promise nobody asked for, and requests that start again after you've said no.
use: Today, notice one moment when someone (a salesperson, an app, an acquaintance) frames you as a team or gives you something first, and ask what they want next.
guard: You don't need proof of bad intent to decline. If someone ignores a clear "no", stop negotiating: repeat it or leave. That refusal to hear no is the one sign worth acting on.
predict: Students who thought they shared a birthday with a stranger were asked to critique the stranger's essay. How many agreed, compared with about 34% otherwise?
- About the same, 34%
- About 45%
* About 62%
scenario: In the lift, a stranger offers to carry Pooja's shopping, says "we'd better hurry, we're both running late" and promises he'll leave as soon as the bags are inside. She hears herself agreeing before she has decided anything.
source: de Becker, G. (1997). The Gift of Fear. Little, Brown; Burger, J. M., et al. (2004). What a coincidence! The effects of incidental similarity on compliance. Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin; Winters, G. M., & Jeglic, E. L. (2016). I knew it all along: The sexual grooming behaviors of child molesters and the hindsight bias. Journal of Child Sexual Abuse.
related: similarity, reciprocity, hindsight-bias

# =====================================================================================
# EMOTIONS & WELLBEING
# =====================================================================================

## peak-end-rule
title: The peak-end rule
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: We remember an experience by its most intense moment and its end, and almost ignore how long it lasted.
what: Memory doesn't average an experience. It keeps snapshots of the peak (best or worst moment) and the ending. So a holiday with a lovely final day is remembered fondly, and a painful procedure that ends gently is remembered as less bad, even if it lasted longer.
study: In 1993 Daniel Kahneman and colleagues had people hold a hand in 14°C water for 60 seconds. In another trial, they did the same, then kept their hand in for 30 more seconds while the water warmed slightly. Asked which trial to repeat, about 69% chose the longer one: more total pain, but a better ending. In 2003 a randomised trial with 682 colonoscopy patients found that adding a gentler final minute made people rate the procedure as less unpleasant and more willing to come back.
case: Donald Redelmeier and Daniel Kahneman first tested the rule in hospitals. In 1996 they had 154 colonoscopy patients and 133 kidney-stone (lithotripsy) patients rate their pain every 60 seconds during the procedure, then give an overall rating afterwards. Procedures ranged from a few minutes to over an hour, yet length barely mattered: the remembered pain tracked the worst moment and the final few minutes. That set up a test with real stakes. In a 2003 trial with 682 patients, half had the colonoscope left still in place for a short extra interval at the end, which added mild discomfort on purpose. They endured more discomfort in total, but rated the whole procedure as less unpleasant and were more likely to come back for a recommended follow-up. The nuance: what patients remember, not what they lived through minute by minute, decides whether they return for cancer screening. It also raises an uncomfortable ethical question: is it right to add discomfort to improve a memory?
proof: Well supported; later work shows peaks and ends matter most but are not the whole story.
spot: Notice how you describe a trip or a meeting afterwards: usually by its high point and how it finished.
use: Design a good ending today: finish a meeting on a positive note, end your workout with your favourite exercise, or close a call warmly.
guard: For big decisions (repeating a holiday, a job, a relationship), look at the whole experience, not just how it ended.
predict: Given a choice of which cold-water trial to repeat, what did most people pick?
- The shorter trial, with less total pain
* The longer trial, which ended slightly less painfully
- They refused to repeat either
scenario: Lakshmi's beach holiday had three days of rain, but the last evening was a stunning sunset. Months later she tells everyone it was perfect.
source: Kahneman, D., et al. (1993). When more pain is preferred to less. Psychological Science; Redelmeier, D. A., Katz, J., & Kahneman, D. (2003). Pain.
related: serial-position-effect, impact-bias

## affect-labeling
title: Affect labelling
aka: Name it to tame it
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: Putting a feeling into words, "I'm anxious", turns down the emotional alarm.
what: Naming an emotion precisely seems to engage the brain's regulatory systems and dampen the reaction. It's different from venting or reasoning yourself out of it: you simply describe what you feel. People rarely expect it to help, which is part of why it's underused.
study: In 2007 Matthew Lieberman and colleagues found that labelling the emotion in an angry or fearful face reduced activity in the amygdala, a brain region involved in threat responses. In 2012 Katharina Kircanski and colleagues had people with spider fear describe their feelings while facing a spider. A week later, they showed smaller physiological fear responses than those who tried to reframe or distract themselves.
case: In 2019 Rui Fan, Onur Varol and colleagues looked for affect labelling outside the lab, in the tweets of 74,487 Twitter users. They found moments when people explicitly named a feeling, as in "I feel so sad" or "I feel happy", and tracked the emotional tone of the same person's tweets minute by minute around it. Emotional intensity climbed in the period before the label and then dropped quickly after it, especially for negative feelings, the pattern lab studies of putting feelings into words would predict. The nuance: this is real behaviour from tens of thousands of people, but it is observational. People may simply tweet "I feel..." at the peak of an emotion that was about to fade anyway. It is the lab experiments, where people are randomly asked to label or not, that show the labelling itself helps; the Twitter data shows the pattern holds in everyday life.
proof: Supported by brain imaging and behavioural studies; the effects are moderate.
spot: Notice moments when someone says "I'm fine" while clearly not, or when naming a feeling ("I'm just tired and cranky") suddenly lowers the tension.
use: The next time you feel a strong emotion today, name it as precisely as you can ("frustrated and a bit embarrassed", not just "bad").
guard: Naming isn't dwelling. Say it once, clearly, then decide what to do. Endlessly analysing the feeling is rumination, which works the other way.
predict: People with spider fear described their feelings while facing a spider. A week later, how did their fear responses compare?
* Smaller than those who tried to reframe or distract
- Larger; describing the fear made it stronger
- About the same
scenario: Mid-argument with her brother, Tara stops and says, "I think I'm actually just hurt that you didn't call." The heat goes out of the conversation.
source: Lieberman, M. D., et al. (2007). Putting feelings into words. Psychological Science; Kircanski, K., Lieberman, M. D., & Craske, M. G. (2012). Psychological Science.
related: reappraisal, self-distancing, expressive-writing

## impact-bias
title: The impact bias
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: We expect bad news to crush us for years and good news to thrill us for months. Both fade faster than we predict.
what: When we imagine future events, we overestimate how intensely and how long they'll affect us. We forget that life goes on, that we'll focus on other things, and that we're good at making sense of setbacks: a "psychological immune system".
study: In 1998 Daniel Gilbert and colleagues asked assistant professors how happy they'd be in the years after getting or being denied tenure. They predicted being much less happy if denied. People who had actually been denied tenure were about as happy as those who got it. Similar gaps appear for elections, break-ups and exam results.
case: Huntington's disease is a fatal inherited brain disorder; a child of someone with it has a 50% chance of carrying the gene. In the early 1990s a Canadian study led by Sandi Wiggins followed people who took a newly available predictive test, many of whom dreaded learning they carried the gene. A year later, those told their risk had increased showed no lasting rise in distress or depression; over the year their distress even declined slightly. Those whose risk dropped felt better too. The group that fared worst was the one whose result was uninformative and who stayed uncertain: at 12 months, people with bad news and people with good news both reported higher well-being than those still not knowing. The nuance: we overestimate how long bad news will hurt and underestimate how much uncertainty hurts. The people who chose to be tested were self-selected, though, and may have expected to cope better than those who stayed away.
proof: Widely replicated; some debate over how much is a forecasting error versus how questions are asked.
spot: Listen for "if this doesn't happen, I'll never get over it" or "once I get this, I'll be set".
use: Think of something you're dreading or longing for. Write how you think you'll feel about it a month later. Check in a month.
guard: Before a big decision based on how you'll feel, ask people who've actually been through it how they feel now.
predict: How happy were professors who had been denied tenure, compared with those who got it, years later?
- Much less happy, as they predicted
* About as happy
- Happier
scenario: Before the results, Rhea was sure missing the IIT cut-off would ruin her life. A year into a course she enjoys, she rarely thinks about it.
source: Gilbert, D. T., et al. (1998). Immune neglect. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Wilson, T. D., & Gilbert, D. T. (2005). Current Directions in Psychological Science.
related: hedonic-adaptation, focusing-illusion, end-of-history-illusion

## reappraisal
title: Reappraisal
category: feelings
evidence: solid
hook: Telling yourself "I'm excited" instead of "I'm anxious" can improve exam scores. How you interpret a feeling changes it.
what: Reappraisal means changing how you interpret a situation or a bodily feeling, so that the emotion itself changes. It tends to work better than suppressing the emotion, which hides it on the outside but keeps it running inside and makes conversations worse.
study: In 1998 James Gross showed that suppressing the expression of disgust left physiological arousal high, while reappraising reduced the experience. In 2010 Jeremy Jamieson and colleagues told some students before a practice GRE that anxiety arousal can improve performance. Those students scored higher on the maths section, and later on the actual GRE.
case: In 2008, in the middle of the financial crisis, Alia Crum, Peter Salovey and Shawn Achor ran a study inside UBS, a global bank where layoffs and losses were daily news. Employees were randomly assigned to watch three short videos portraying stress either as debilitating (causing illness and mistakes) or as enhancing (sharpening focus and building resilience), or to a control group with no videos. Afterwards, those who saw the enhancing videos had shifted towards seeing stress as useful, and reported fewer health symptoms and better work performance than those who saw the debilitating version. The bank and its pressures hadn't changed; how people read their own racing hearts had. The nuance: this isn't "stress is good" or "think positive". Reappraisal changes what an arousal response means rather than denying it. The outcomes were self-reported and the effects modest, about what you would expect from a few minutes of video.
proof: Reappraisal is one of the best-supported emotion regulation strategies; meta-analyses show moderate effects.
spot: Notice how people talk about stress: "this is a disaster" versus "this is a challenge".
use: Before something stressful today, try saying "I'm excited" or "my body is getting ready" instead of "calm down".
guard: Reappraisal isn't pretending. It works best when the new interpretation is believable. In truly bad situations, changing the situation matters more.
predict: Students were told anxiety arousal can help performance before a practice GRE. What happened to their maths scores?
* They improved
- They dropped; the message raised their anxiety
- No change
scenario: Before her first stand-up set, Ananya feels her heart racing. Instead of fighting it, she tells herself "this is my body getting ready to perform".
source: Gross, J. J. (1998). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Jamieson, J. P., et al. (2010). Turning the knots in your stomach into bows. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology.
related: affect-labeling, self-distancing

## self-distancing
title: Self-distancing
aka: Solomon's paradox
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: We give wiser advice to friends than to ourselves. Talking to yourself by name brings that wisdom home.
what: When we're inside a problem, we're flooded with emotion and see narrowly. When advising a friend, we step back and see the bigger picture. You can borrow that distance by thinking about your situation in the third person ("Why is Rahul so upset?") or as if advising a friend.
study: In 2014 Ethan Kross and colleagues found that people who used their own name or "you" instead of "I" in self-talk before a speech felt less anxious and gave better speeches. Also in 2014, Igor Grossmann and Ethan Kross found that people reasoned more wisely about a friend's relationship problem than their own, but the gap disappeared when they considered their own problem in the third person.
case: In October 2014, Ebola had just been diagnosed in the United States and anxiety was spreading far faster than the virus. Ethan Kross and colleagues asked 1,257 Americans to think through their feelings about Ebola, either in the first person ("Why am I worried?") or using their own name. Among people who started out highly worried, those who used their own name generated more fact-based reasons not to worry, such as how the disease actually spreads, and that predicted lower worry and a lower sense of personal risk afterwards. People who weren't very anxious to begin with changed little. The nuance: self-distancing didn't make people careless or blindly reassured; it seemed to let anxious people reason with facts they already had. It is a tool for when emotion is crowding out thinking, not a general mood booster.
proof: Supported by several studies; effects are moderate and best for emotional, personal situations.
spot: Notice how easily you advise others on problems that would floor you if they were your own.
use: When something bothers you today, ask about it in the third person: "What does [your name] need right now?"
guard: Distance helps you think; it doesn't replace dealing with the problem or talking to someone.
predict: When people thought about their own relationship problem in the third person, how wisely did they reason?
- Less wisely; it felt artificial
* About as wisely as about a friend's problem
- No change from thinking in the first person
scenario: Stuck over whether to quit his job, Nikhil asks himself, "If my best friend were in this exact situation, what would I tell him?" The answer is suddenly clear.
source: Kross, E., et al. (2014). Self-talk as a regulatory mechanism. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Grossmann, I., & Kross, E. (2014). Psychological Science.
related: affect-labeling, reappraisal

## hedonic-adaptation
title: Hedonic adaptation
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: The new phone, the raise, the bigger flat: we get used to good things, and the glow fades.
what: After changes in our circumstances, our happiness tends to drift back towards where it was. It's protective after bad events, and disappointing after good ones. Adaptation isn't complete for everything, though: things like chronic pain, unemployment, noise and long commutes keep costing happiness.
study: In 1978 Philip Brickman and colleagues compared 22 lottery winners with people who hadn't won and found the winners were not much happier, a small and often over-cited study. Later research with large panels, such as Richard Lucas's 2007 analyses, showed adaptation is real but often incomplete: disability and unemployment leave lasting dips.
case: Swedish lotteries allowed a far cleaner test than the famous 1978 study of 22 winners. In 2020 Erik Lindqvist, Robert Östling and David Cesarini surveyed about 3,000 Swedish lottery players 5 to 22 years after a win. Because prize sizes were random, differences in well-being between big and small winners could be traced to the money itself. Big winners were more satisfied with their lives, and the effect showed no sign of fading over those years: an after-tax prize of about $100,000 raised life satisfaction by a small but lasting amount. But on measures closer to day-to-day feeling, happiness and mental health, the effects were small and not clearly different from zero. The nuance: adaptation depends on what you measure. People adapt in how they feel moment to moment far more than in how they judge their life as a whole, which partly reflects lasting financial security. "Winners aren't happier" was too simple; so is "money buys happiness".
proof: Adaptation is real and well documented, but not total; the lottery study is small.
spot: Notice the thing you were thrilled about six months ago that you now barely notice.
use: Slow adaptation down today: take one thing you enjoy (your coffee, your home, a person) and notice it as if for the first time.
guard: Don't count on adapting to everything: long commutes, noise and loneliness keep hurting. Weigh them seriously in big choices.
predict: Large studies following people over years found that after becoming unemployed, life satisfaction...
- Fully recovered within a year
* Often stayed lower for years
- Rose above where it was before
scenario: For the first month, Pranav marvelled at his new car every morning. Six months later, it's just how he gets to work.
source: Brickman, P., Coates, D., & Janoff-Bulman, R. (1978). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Lucas, R. E. (2007). Current Directions in Psychological Science.
related: impact-bias, savoring, focusing-illusion

## mind-wandering
title: A wandering mind is an unhappy mind
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: Our minds wander almost half the time, and we're less happy when they do, even to pleasant thoughts.
what: Mind wandering is the default. We spend a huge share of waking life thinking about something other than what we're doing. While it supports planning and creativity, it's linked to lower mood in the moment. What you're doing matters less to how you feel than whether your mind is on it.
study: In 2010 Matthew Killingsworth and Daniel Gilbert used an iPhone app to ask about 2,250 people, at random moments, what they were doing, what they were thinking about and how they felt. Minds were wandering 46.9% of the time. People were less happy when their minds wandered, and even pleasant wanderings didn't make them happier than staying on task.
case: Between 2010 and 2011, Cédric Galéra and colleagues interviewed 955 drivers injured in road crashes as they arrived at the emergency department of the university hospital in Bordeaux, France. Each was asked what they had been thinking about just before the crash, and how disturbing or distracting the thought had been; the researchers separately determined who was responsible for each crash. Drivers whose minds had been taken over by an intense, disturbing thought, often a worry or a recent upsetting event, made up 17% of those responsible for their crash but only 9% of those who weren't. After accounting for alcohol, fatigue and other factors, that kind of mind wandering roughly doubled the odds of being responsible. The nuance: mild mind wandering was common and wasn't the danger; it was absorbing, emotionally loaded thought. Recall of thoughts just before a crash is imperfect, but the study takes Killingsworth's "wandering mind" out of the lab and onto the road.
proof: A large, well-known study; later work suggests the direction of cause runs both ways (low mood also causes wandering).
spot: Notice how often you're replaying a conversation or planning tomorrow while eating, walking or talking.
use: Pick one routine activity today (brushing teeth, a walk, chai) and keep your attention on it from start to finish.
guard: Don't fight every wandering thought. Notice it, then return. That return is the skill.
predict: How often were people's minds wandering in the large phone-sampling study?
- About 10% of the time
- About 25% of the time
* About 47% of the time
scenario: On the scenic train ride to Shimla, Mansi spends most of the journey mentally rewriting an email to her boss, and arrives feeling flat.
source: Killingsworth, M. A., & Gilbert, D. T. (2010). A wandering mind is an unhappy mind. Science.
related: savoring

## negativity-bias
title: The negativity bias
category: feelings
evidence: solid
hook: One criticism can outweigh ten compliments. Bad is stronger than good.
what: Negative events, emotions and information have more impact than positive ones of the same size. We notice threats faster, remember insults longer and weigh losses more heavily. It kept our ancestors alive; today it can make one bad review feel bigger than many good ones.
study: In 2001 Roy Baumeister and colleagues reviewed evidence across close relationships, emotions, learning, health and impression formation in a paper titled "Bad is stronger than good". Across almost every domain, bad events had bigger and longer-lasting effects than good ones. Paul Rozin and Edward Royzman reached a similar conclusion the same year.
case: Upworthy, a US news site, routinely tested several headlines for the same story on random slices of its readers, keeping whichever drew the most clicks. In 2023 Claire Robertson and colleagues analysed about 105,000 of these headline variations, which together had been seen over 370 million times. Because readers were randomly assigned, the study could isolate the effect of the wording itself. For a headline of average length, each additional negative word raised the click-through rate by about 2.3%, while positive words slightly lowered it. The nuance: this is how the negativity bias scales up. Each reader makes a small, reasonable choice to look at the threat first; summed across millions of clicks, it gives editors a steady financial reason to make the news gloomier than the world it describes.
proof: Supported by a broad range of findings, though the size varies by domain.
spot: Notice what you remember from a performance review, or which comment you replay from a day that was mostly good.
use: Tonight, write down three things that went well today, specifically, to balance the scales.
guard: Before deciding based on one bad experience or review, ask how many good ones there were.
predict: According to "Bad is stronger than good", what happens when good and bad events of similar size are compared?
* Bad events have bigger, longer-lasting effects in almost every domain
- Good events win in close relationships
- They roughly cancel out
scenario: Shruti's talk gets twenty warm comments and one critical one. She spends the evening thinking only about the critical one.
source: Baumeister, R. F., Bratslavsky, E., Finkenauer, C., & Vohs, K. D. (2001). Bad is stronger than good. Review of General Psychology.
related: loss-aversion

## focusing-illusion
title: The focusing illusion
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: Nothing in life is as important as you think it is while you are thinking about it.
