what: When we focus on one factor (weather, income, a new house), we overestimate how much it affects overall happiness, because in daily life we're rarely thinking about it. People in sunny places aren't much happier; they just get used to the sun.
study: In 1998 David Schkade and Daniel Kahneman surveyed students in California and the US Midwest. Both groups were about equally satisfied with life. But both predicted Californians would be happier, mostly because of the weather, which in reality plays little part in overall life satisfaction.
case: When people choose where to live and work, they focus on what is easy to compare: salary, rent, the size of the flat. In 2008 Alois Stutzer and Bruno Frey analysed the German Socio-Economic Panel, which follows the same people's lives and life satisfaction for years. People with longer commutes were consistently less satisfied with their lives, and, contrary to what standard economics predicts, they were not compensated by higher pay or better housing. By one widely quoted estimate from this work, someone with a one-hour commute would need about 40% more income to be as satisfied as someone with none. The nuance: commuting is the classic cost that is small in any single moment and so gets underweighted when deciding, yet is paid twice a day, every working day. A bigger home is noticed when you move in; the commute is noticed every time you are stuck in it, which is far more often.
proof: The original study is well known and the principle has been supported in related research; the classic example is a single study.
spot: Listen for "if only I lived in a bigger city / earned more / had that car, I'd be happy".
use: Take one thing you think would make you much happier. List everything else that would stay the same about your daily life if you got it.
guard: When weighing a big change, imagine an ordinary Tuesday after the change, not the moment it happens.
predict: Were students in California happier with life than students in the Midwest?
- Yes, much happier
* No; about equally satisfied, though both groups predicted they would be
- No; Midwesterners were happier
scenario: Vivek is sure moving to Goa would make him happy. He hasn't thought about the same work deadlines, the same commute to his laptop, and missing his friends.
source: Schkade, D. A., & Kahneman, D. (1998). Does living in California make people happy? Psychological Science.
related: impact-bias, hedonic-adaptation

## facial-feedback
title: The facial feedback hypothesis
category: feelings
evidence: debated
hook: Does smiling make you happier? A tiny bit, according to a big 19-country test. The famous pen trick didn't work.
what: The idea is that facial expressions don't just show emotions; they feed back into them. In the classic version, holding a pen in your teeth (forcing a smile) made cartoons funnier. That version failed a big replication. A later multi-lab study found posed smiles do slightly lift mood.
study: In 1988 Fritz Strack and colleagues reported the pen-in-teeth effect. In 2016 a registered replication across 17 labs found no effect. Then in 2022 the Many Smiles Collaboration, 3,878 people in 19 countries, found that mimicking smiling faces or pulling the corners of the mouth up modestly increased happiness, but the pen method still didn't.
case: Botox paralyses the muscles that pull the brows into a frown. If expressions feed back into emotion, blocking the frown might ease depression. In 2014 Eric Finzi and Norman Rosenthal ran a randomised, double-blind trial with 85 people with major depression: half received a single round of botulinum toxin injections between the eyebrows, half received saline. Six weeks later, 52% of the Botox group had improved substantially (at least halving their symptom score), compared with 15% of the placebo group, and smaller trials found similar results. The nuance: it is twofold. True blinding is hard, because people can feel they can't frown and may guess which group they are in. And nobody is sure the effect runs through emotional feedback rather than, for example, how others respond to a less troubled-looking face. It hasn't become standard treatment, but it is the boldest real-world test of the idea that the face talks back to the mind.
proof: Debated: a small effect of posed smiles seems real; the famous method and big claims are not.
spot: Notice advice like "just smile and you'll feel better". There may be a small effect, but it won't fix a bad day.
use: Try a genuine smile for 10 seconds before a call today. Notice if anything shifts, and don't expect miracles.
guard: Forced smiles at work all day ("service with a smile") are linked to exhaustion. A small boost isn't a reason to hide real feelings.
predict: In the 19-country Many Smiles study, which method increased happiness?
- Holding a pen between the teeth
* Mimicking smiling faces or pulling the mouth up
- None of them
scenario: Feeling low before a family dinner, Raj deliberately smiles in the mirror for a while, and notices a slight lift, though not much.
source: Strack, F., Martin, L. L., & Stepper, S. (1988). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Coles, N. A., et al. (2022). A multi-lab test of the facial feedback hypothesis. Nature Human Behaviour.
related: power-posing, replication-crisis

## behavioral-activation
title: Behavioural activation
aka: Action before motivation
category: feelings
evidence: solid
hook: Waiting to feel motivated before acting gets it backwards. Doing comes first; the mood often follows.
what: When we feel low, we withdraw, which removes the activities that give us pleasure and mastery, which makes us feel lower. Behavioural activation breaks the loop by scheduling small, valued activities regardless of mood. It's one of the most effective treatments for depression.
study: In 1996 Neil Jacobson and colleagues found that the behavioural activation part of cognitive behavioural therapy worked about as well as the full therapy. In 2016 the COBRA trial, published in The Lancet, randomised 440 adults with depression and found behavioural activation delivered by junior mental health workers was as effective as CBT delivered by experienced therapists, and cheaper.
case: In Goa, India, the PREMIUM trial tested whether behavioural activation could be delivered by people with no professional training at all. Vikram Patel and colleagues trained lay counsellors, local people without mental health degrees, to deliver a brief programme called the Healthy Activity Program in primary care clinics. 495 adults with moderate to severe depression were randomly assigned to it or to enhanced usual care. Over six to eight sessions, counsellors helped each person identify activities that had once given them pleasure or a sense of mastery, schedule them, and solve the problems that got in the way. Three months later, 64% of the HAP group had remitted, compared with 39% of those receiving usual care, and the programme was cost-effective. Published in The Lancet in 2017, it is one of the clearest demonstrations that the core of behavioural activation is simple enough to scale. The nuance: "simple" doesn't mean "just keep busy". Activities were chosen for each person's values and adjusted when they didn't help.
proof: Supported by large randomised trials and meta-analyses.
spot: Listen for "I'll do it when I feel like it" or "I'm not in the mood". Moods often follow action, not the other way round.
use: Pick one small thing you've been avoiding and do just five minutes of it today, mood or no mood.
guard: Start very small and specific ("walk to the end of the road") rather than big ("get fit"). If low mood lasts for weeks, talk to a professional.
predict: In the COBRA trial, how did behavioural activation by junior workers compare with CBT by experienced therapists?
- Much less effective
* About as effective, and cheaper
- Only effective for mild cases
scenario: Feeling flat all week, Leena forces herself to water her plants and call a friend. An hour later she notices she feels a little lighter.
source: Jacobson, N. S., et al. (1996). Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology; Richards, D. A., et al. (2016). The Lancet.
related: self-perception

## savoring
title: Savouring
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: Good moments give more joy when you deliberately slow down and notice them.
what: Savouring is paying attention to good experiences: anticipating them, enjoying them as they happen, and recalling them afterwards. It's the positive counterpart of coping. People who savour get more happiness from the same events.
study: Psychologists Fred Bryant and Joseph Veroff set out a model of savouring in 2007. Bryant's studies found that people who habitually savour get more positive mood from good events. In a later intervention, people asked to take a daily 20-minute walk while deliberately noticing pleasant things reported more happiness after a week than those who walked normally.
case: In 2010 Jordi Quoidbach, Elizabeth Dunn and colleagues surveyed adult employees of a Belgian university, from cleaners to senior administrators. Wealthier employees reported a weaker ability to savour everyday pleasures, and that weaker savouring cancelled out part of the happiness their money otherwise brought. In a second study, participants ate a piece of chocolate after seeing either a photo of a stack of money or a neutral photo. Those reminded of money spent less time savouring the chocolate and appeared to enjoy it less. The nuance: access to the best things may dull the small ones, because abundance makes any single pleasure feel ordinary. Treat the chocolate experiment cautiously, since many "money reminder" effects have failed to replicate, but the survey finding fits a wider pattern: savouring is a skill and a habit, not a by-product of having nice things.
proof: Supported by several studies; intervention effects are modest.
spot: Notice when you rush through something enjoyable (a meal, a view, a hug) while thinking about the next thing.
use: Pick one pleasant moment today and stretch it: slow down, notice three details, and tell someone about it later.
guard: Savouring is about noticing, not clinging. Trying to make a moment perfect can spoil it.
predict: People who took a daily walk while deliberately noticing pleasant things, compared with normal walkers, reported...
- No difference after a week
* More happiness after a week
- More anxiety, from the pressure to enjoy it
scenario: Instead of scrolling through her first mango of the season, Sonal eats it slowly on the balcony, noticing the smell and the sticky sweetness.
source: Bryant, F. B., & Veroff, J. (2007). Savoring: A New Model of Positive Experience.
related: mind-wandering, hedonic-adaptation, peak-end-rule

## hot-cold-empathy-gap
title: The hot-cold empathy gap
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: When you're calm, you can't imagine how differently you'll act when hungry, angry or tired.
what: In a "cold" state, we underestimate how much "hot" states (hunger, craving, anger, fear, arousal) will drive our behaviour, and in a hot state, we can't imagine feeling differently. So we make promises we can't keep and judge others harshly.
study: George Loewenstein described the gap in 2005. In 2003 Leaf Van Boven and Loewenstein had people predict how hikers lost without food and water would feel. Those who had just exercised (and were thirsty) were more likely to say the hikers would be bothered by thirst than hunger. Their current state coloured their predictions about others.
case: In 2008 Michael Sayette, George Loewenstein and colleagues studied 98 smokers. Some predicted, while in a "cold" state with little craving, how much they would value smoking during a later session in which they would be craving; others made the same prediction while already craving. In the later session, everyone was put in a craving state and their actual valuation of a cigarette was measured. Smokers who had predicted from the cold state underestimated how much they would want to smoke; those who predicted while craving were much more accurate. The nuance: when calm, people know about cravings in the abstract without feeling their force. That is why plans like "I'll just have one at the party" fail so often, and why relapse plans work better when made with the craving in mind, or by removing the temptation in advance rather than relying on resisting it in the moment.
proof: Supported by several lines of research on predicting feelings and behaviour across states.
spot: Notice grocery shopping when hungry, texting when angry, or deciding on dessert before a meal.
use: Notice one decision today you're making in a "hot" state (tired, hungry, irritated). Postpone it by an hour if you can.
guard: Make important commitments in a cold state and protect them for hot ones: no shopping when hungry, a 24-hour rule for angry emails.
predict: People who had just exercised predicted how lost hikers would feel. What did they think would bother the hikers most?
* Thirst
- Hunger
- Cold
scenario: Every morning, Arun swears he'll skip the evening samosa. Every evening, starving after work, he buys two.
source: Loewenstein, G. (2005). Hot-cold empathy gaps and medical decision making. Health Psychology; Van Boven, L., & Loewenstein, G. (2003). Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin.
related: present-bias, end-of-history-illusion

## expressive-writing
title: Expressive writing
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: Writing about your deepest feelings for 15 minutes, a few days in a row, can help you process them.
what: Putting difficult experiences into words, privately, seems to help people organise and make sense of them. The effect is modest on average, but writing costs nothing, and it helps most when people move from raw emotion towards understanding.
study: In 1986 James Pennebaker and Sandra Beall had students write about traumatic experiences for 15 minutes on four days. In the following months, they visited the health centre less often than students who wrote about trivial topics. A 2006 meta-analysis of 146 studies by Joanne Frattaroli found a small but real average benefit.
case: In 1994 Stefanie Spera, Eric Buhrfeind and James Pennebaker worked with 63 professionals, mostly engineers over 50, who had been laid off by a Dallas computer company after many years of service and who were angry and anxious about finding work again. One group wrote on five consecutive days about their deepest thoughts and feelings about losing their jobs; a second wrote about how they spent their time; a third didn't write. Eight months later, 53% of the expressive writers had found new full-time jobs, compared with 24% of the time-management writers and 14% of those who didn't write. Strikingly, the expressive writers hadn't sent more applications or gone to more interviews. The nuance: writing didn't make them try harder. It seems to have changed how they came across, letting them work through anger that might otherwise have leaked into interviews. The sample was small, and most expressive-writing studies find much smaller effects.
proof: Replicated many times; average effects are small, larger for some people and outcomes.
spot: Notice whether you feel clearer after writing something down than after just thinking about it.
use: Tonight, write for 10 minutes about something that's been bothering you, without editing. Then close the notebook.
guard: If writing makes you feel much worse or brings up trauma, stop and talk to someone. Aim to understand, not to relive it.
predict: Across 146 studies, how large is the average benefit of expressive writing?
- Large; it often replaces therapy
* Small but real
- Zero
scenario: After a painful break-up, Ishita writes about it for fifteen minutes each night for four nights. By the fourth night, she's writing more about what she's learned than what she lost.
source: Pennebaker, J. W., & Beall, S. K. (1986). Journal of Abnormal Psychology; Frattaroli, J. (2006). Psychological Bulletin.
related: affect-labeling

## awe
title: Awe
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: A minute of looking up at tall trees made people more helpful. Awe shrinks the self, in a good way.
what: Awe is what we feel in front of something vast that we can't easily take in: mountains, the night sky, a great performance. It makes our own concerns feel smaller and connects us to something bigger, which seems to make us kinder and less self-focused.
study: In 2015 Paul Piff and colleagues had people stare up at towering eucalyptus trees or at a tall building for one minute. Then an experimenter "accidentally" dropped some pens. The tree-gazers picked up more pens and reported feeling less entitled. In 2022 Virginia Sturm and colleagues found that older adults who took weekly "awe walks" for eight weeks reported more joy, and their selfies shifted to show less of themselves and more of the scenery.
case: In 2018 Craig Anderson, Maria Monroy and Dacher Keltner took 72 military veterans and 52 teenagers from under-resourced Bay Area communities whitewater rafting on the American River in California, on one- or two-day trips; a dozen more veterans joined a four-day trip in Utah. Participants reported their emotions during the trip and their well-being and stress symptoms before and a week after. The veterans' PTSD symptoms fell by about 29%, and stress dropped in both groups. Crucially, how much awe each person felt on the river predicted their improvements a week later better than joy, pride, gratitude, contentment or amusement did. The nuance: this wasn't a randomised trial, so the trips might have helped for many reasons, from exercise to companionship. What the design does show is that, among all the good feelings the river produced, awe was the one that tracked healing.
proof: Supported by a growing body of experiments, though many are small.
spot: Notice the moment you pause in front of a view, a storm or a stadium crowd, and how your worries feel smaller.
use: Take a five-minute walk today looking for something vast or intricate: the sky, a big tree, the detail in a leaf.
guard: Awe can also be overwhelming or used to sway crowds (rallies, spectacles). Notice who might be using it.
predict: People who gazed at tall trees for a minute, versus a building, when an experimenter dropped pens...
* Picked up more pens
- Picked up fewer pens
- Picked up about the same number
scenario: After watching the sun rise over the Himalayas, Ritesh finds his office rivalry suddenly seems small and a bit silly.
source: Piff, P. K., et al. (2015). Awe, the small self, and prosocial behavior. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Sturm, V. E., et al. (2022). Emotion.
related: savoring

## prosocial-spending
title: Prosocial spending
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: Spending a little money on someone else can lift your mood more than spending it on yourself.
what: Giving, whether it's buying a friend lunch or donating, seems to boost the giver's happiness, especially when you can see the impact and feel connected to the person. Big replications show the effect is real but smaller than first reported.
study: In 2008 Elizabeth Dunn, Lara Aknin and Michael Norton gave people $5 or $20 to spend by the end of the day, either on themselves or on others. Those who spent it on others reported more happiness that evening. That study was small; larger preregistered studies since then find a reliable but more modest boost.
case: In 2013 Lalin Anik, Lara Aknin, Michael Norton, Elizabeth Dunn and Jordi Quoidbach tested "prosocial bonuses" in real organisations. In Belgian pharmaceutical sales teams, some members received money to spend on themselves and others received the same amount to spend on a teammate; the researchers then tracked team sales. Teams whose members spent on each other went on to sell more, by more than the cost of the bonuses, while money spent on oneself produced no measurable return. Sports teams in Canada showed the same pattern, and at an Australian bank, employees who gave part of a bonus to charity reported more happiness and job satisfaction. The nuance: the benefit doesn't come from "being nice" in the abstract. It appears strongest when giving builds a relationship you are part of and the giver can see the impact, which a teammate's thanks makes visible in a way a cheque to a distant cause often doesn't.
proof: Real but more modest than the original; clearer when the giving is personal and its impact visible.
spot: Notice how you feel after treating someone compared with buying something for yourself.
use: Spend a small amount on someone else today: a coffee, a snack, a tip. Notice how you feel an hour later.
guard: Giving out of guilt or pressure doesn't seem to bring the same lift. Choose people and causes you care about.
predict: What did larger preregistered studies find about the happiness boost from spending on others?
- It doesn't exist
* It's real but smaller than first reported
- It's even bigger than first reported
scenario: Instead of buying himself another T-shirt, Abhay uses the money to buy his building's watchman a warm jacket, and thinks about it happily all week.
source: Dunn, E. W., Aknin, L. B., & Norton, M. I. (2008). Spending money on others promotes happiness. Science; Aknin, L. B., Dunn, E. W., & Whillans, A. V. (2022). Current Directions in Psychological Science.
related: experiential-purchases, buying-time, gratitude-letters

## misattribution-of-arousal
title: Misattribution of arousal
aka: The shaky bridge study
category: feelings
evidence: debated
hook: Men on a scary suspension bridge were more likely to call the woman they met there. Was it fear, or attraction?
what: A racing heart doesn't come labelled. We look around for an explanation, and can mistake arousal from one source (fear, exercise, caffeine) for another (attraction, anger). It's why first dates at thrilling places can feel electric.
study: In 1974 Donald Dutton and Arthur Aron had an attractive woman approach men on either a swaying suspension bridge over the Capilano Canyon or a low, stable bridge, and give them her phone number. 9 of 18 men on the scary bridge called her, compared with 2 of 16 on the stable bridge. The samples were tiny and later replications have been mixed.
case: In 2003 Cindy Meston and Penny Frohlich took the bridge study to amusement parks. They approached 300 people either queuing for a roller coaster or just getting off it, showed them a photo of an averagely attractive person of the opposite sex, and asked how attractive and dateable that person seemed. Among people riding with a friend, those coming off the ride rated the photo as more attractive than those about to get on, as if some of the ride's pounding heart had been credited to the face. But among people riding with their romantic partner, the effect vanished: ratings were the same before and after. The nuance: arousal doesn't automatically become attraction. People look for a reason for their racing heart, and when an obvious candidate is already sitting beside them, the arousal goes there, not to a stranger in a photo. That kind of condition-dependence is part of why the original bridge study is debated.
proof: Debated: misattribution can happen, but the classic study is small and the effect inconsistent.
spot: Notice when stress from one thing (traffic, a deadline) spills into irritation at someone who did nothing.
use: The next time you feel your heart racing today, pause and ask: what's really causing this?
guard: Before reacting to a strong feeling, check its source. Are you angry at your partner, or still wound up from the drive?
predict: How many of the men on the scary bridge called the woman, compared with 2 of 16 on the stable bridge?
- 3 of 18
* 9 of 18
- 17 of 18
scenario: After an intense horror movie, Kunal finds himself unusually drawn to his date, and can't tell if it's her or the adrenaline.
source: Dutton, D. G., & Aron, A. P. (1974). Some evidence for heightened sexual attraction under conditions of high anxiety. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: reappraisal

## worries-rarely-happen
title: Most worries never happen
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: People with anxiety logged their worries, then checked later what actually happened. About 91% of the worries never came true.
what: Worry feels like useful preparation, so we keep doing it. But most of what we worry about doesn't happen, and when it does it's usually less bad than we imagined. Worry may persist partly because it seems to work: when the feared event doesn't come, the worrying gets the credit. It can also keep us braced so that bad news won't feel like a sudden drop, at the price of feeling bad all the time.
study: In 2020 Lucas LaFreniere and Michelle Newman had people with generalised anxiety disorder record their worries over several days and later note whether each one had come true. On average 91.4% of the worries did not come true, and for many participants none did. Emotionally, people had felt their worries were more likely than they logically believed. Those who found more of their worries untrue tended to improve more in treatment.
case: If worrying gives false comfort, can you postpone it? In "worry postponement", people notice a worry, jot it down and save it for a fixed half-hour "worry period" later in the day, in the same place each time. In 2013 Sandra McGowan and Evelyn Behar randomly assigned 53 students with high levels of worry either to this technique or to worrying as they normally would. The postponement group reported less worry and anxiety, and better sleep. A 2023 meta-analysis of seven randomised trials with 999 participants found that postponement modestly reduced how often and how long people worried. The nuance: worry isn't the same as planning. If a worry points to a problem you can act on, act; it's the circling, unanswerable "what ifs" that these techniques shrink.
proof: Good: the "most worries don't come true" finding comes from one careful study of people with anxiety; worry postponement is supported by randomised trials and a meta-analysis, with modest effects.
spot: Notice "what if" thoughts that circle without leading to any action, and how often yesterday's worries quietly didn't happen.
use: Write down one worry today with a date to check it. When the date comes, record whether it happened and how bad it really was.
guard: When a worry won't let go, ask: "Is there anything I can do about this now?" If yes, do it or schedule it; if not, park it for a set worry time later.
predict: People with anxiety recorded their worries and later checked them. What share of the worries did not come true?
- About 30%
- About half
* About 91%
scenario: Every night Devika rehearses how her presentation could go wrong. This month she keeps a log, and when she reads it back she finds that none of the eleven disasters she imagined happened.
source: LaFreniere, L. S., & Newman, M. G. (2020). Exposing worry's deceit: Percentage of untrue worries in generalized anxiety disorder treatment. Behavior Therapy; McGowan, S. K., & Behar, E. (2013). A preliminary investigation of stimulus control training for worry. Behavior Modification; Dippel, N., et al. (2023). Effects of worry postponement on daily worry: A meta-analysis. International Journal of Cognitive Therapy.
related: ironic-process, affect-labeling, self-distancing

## money-and-happiness
title: Money and happiness, revised
category: feelings
evidence: good
hook: A famous study said happiness stops rising at $75,000 a year. One of its authors teamed up with a critic and found it keeps rising for most people.
what: In 2010 Daniel Kahneman and Angus Deaton reported that people's day-to-day emotional well-being rose with income up to about $75,000 a year and then flattened, while their overall evaluation of their lives kept rising. The plateau became a widely quoted rule. Larger data later found no plateau for most people. Both turned out to be partly right: for the unhappiest fifth of people, extra income stops helping beyond a point; for most others, feelings keep improving as income rises, though each extra rupee matters less.
study: In 2021 Matthew Killingsworth analysed over 1.7 million real-time mood reports from 33,391 employed US adults and found emotional well-being rising steadily with income, with no plateau. Rather than argue in print, Kahneman and Killingsworth worked together, with Barbara Mellers as arbiter, in an "adversarial collaboration". Their 2023 reanalysis found that well-being rises with the logarithm of income for most people, but flattens above about $100,000 a year for the least happy 20%.
case: Money matters most where people have least. In 2016 Johannes Haushofer and Jeremy Shapiro evaluated GiveDirectly, a charity that gave unconditional cash transfers to randomly chosen poor households in rural Kenya. Months later, compared with households that hadn't received money, recipients reported more happiness and life satisfaction, and less stress and depression. Their overall index of psychological well-being rose by about a quarter of a standard deviation. The nuance: "money can't buy happiness" is wrong, and so is "more money always helps the same amount". A fixed sum helps a poor household far more than a rich one, and when unhappiness comes from grief, illness or depression, income can't fix what's wrong.
proof: Good: the link between income and well-being is supported by very large datasets and an adversarial collaboration, with continuing debate about cause and exact shape.
spot: Notice claims like "money doesn't make you happier above X" or "a raise will fix everything", and who they're being said about.
use: Look at your last big discretionary purchase and ask honestly how much it changed how you feel day to day. Compare it with money you spent on time or on someone else.
guard: Treat income as one input to well-being among several. Before trading time, health or relationships for more money, ask what the extra money would actually change.
predict: In the 2023 adversarial collaboration, for whom did emotional well-being stop rising with income above about $100,000?
- For everyone; the plateau is universal
* Only for the unhappiest 20% of people
- For no one; it rises in every group at every income
scenario: Kunal turned down a promotion because he'd read that happiness stops rising above a certain salary. His cousin, behind on her rent, says that rule clearly wasn't written about people like her.
source: Kahneman, D., & Deaton, A. (2010). High income improves evaluation of life but not emotional well-being. PNAS; Killingsworth, M. A. (2021). Experienced well-being rises with income, even above $75,000 per year. PNAS; Killingsworth, M. A., Kahneman, D., & Mellers, B. (2023). Income and emotional well-being: A conflict resolved. PNAS; Haushofer, J., & Shapiro, J. (2016). The short-term impact of unconditional cash transfers to the poor. Quarterly Journal of Economics.
related: hedonic-adaptation, focusing-illusion, prosocial-spending

# RELATIONSHIPS & CONNECTION

## liking-gap
title: The liking gap
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: After a conversation with someone new, they liked you more than you think.
what: After talking with a stranger, we replay our awkward moments and assume the other person saw them too. Meanwhile they were busy worrying about their own. The result is a consistent gap: people underestimate how much their conversation partner liked them and enjoyed their company.
study: In 2018 Erica Boothby, Gus Cooney, Gillian Sandstrom and Margaret Clark had strangers chat, then rate how much they liked their partner and how much they thought their partner liked them. People consistently underestimated how much they were liked. The gap appeared in lab conversations, in a public workshop, and among first-year university roommates, where it lasted for months.
case: In 2021 Adam Mastroianni, Gus Cooney, Erica Boothby and Andrew Reece took the liking gap to work. They studied people in small groups and in real workplace teams. Again and again, people believed their teammates liked them less than they actually did, and many believed they were the least-liked person in the group, which can't be true for everyone. It mattered for work: how much employees thought a colleague liked them predicted whether they were willing to ask that colleague for help, give them honest feedback, and collaborate with them again. The nuance: the liking gap isn't just a private insecurity. In teams it quietly reduces the help-seeking and feedback that good work runs on, so a manager who says "people enjoy working with you" may be correcting a real distortion, not just being kind.
proof: Replicated across several settings and studies in the original paper and later work.
spot: Notice your post-conversation thoughts: "I talked too much", "they were just being polite".
use: After a conversation today, assume you were liked more than you think, and follow up: send the article you mentioned, or suggest meeting again.
guard: The gap can stop you building friendships. When in doubt, take the next small step; the odds are better than your gut says.
predict: How long did the liking gap last among new university roommates?
- It disappeared after the first week
* It persisted for months
- It reversed after a month
scenario: Walking home from a dinner party, Megha is sure the new couple found her boring. The next day they message asking when they can meet again.
source: Boothby, E. J., Cooney, G., Sandstrom, G. M., & Clark, M. S. (2018). The liking gap in conversations. Psychological Science.
related: spotlight-effect, beautiful-mess, talking-to-strangers

## beautiful-mess
title: The beautiful mess effect
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: When you show vulnerability, you see weakness. Others see courage.
what: Admitting a mistake, asking for help or confessing feelings feels risky and exposing from the inside. From the outside, the same acts tend to look honest and brave. Because we judge our own vulnerability more harshly than others do, we hide it more than we need to.
study: In 2018 Anna Bruk, Sabine Scholl and Herbert Bless asked people to imagine themselves or others in vulnerable situations, like being the first to confess love, or admitting a serious mistake at work. People consistently rated others' vulnerability more positively than their own. The same act looked like weakness when they did it and like a "beautiful mess" when someone else did.
case: Hospitals long assumed that admitting a mistake to a patient would invite lawsuits. From 2001 the University of Michigan Health System did the opposite: when its own review found that an error had caused harm, it disclosed the mistake, apologised and offered fair compensation; when care had been reasonable, it explained why and defended it. In 2010 Allen Kachalia and colleagues compared the years before and after. The monthly rate of new claims fell from about 7.0 to 4.5 per 100,000 patient visits, lawsuits fell from about 2.1 to 0.75, claims were resolved faster, and liability costs dropped. The nuance: this is the beautiful-mess effect at institutional scale. The people doing the admitting expected it to look like weakness and be punished; the people receiving it often saw honesty. But it was a before-and-after study of one health system during a period of other legal changes, so it shows the fear was overblown more than it proves apology alone caused the drop.
proof: Replicated across several studies in the original paper and supported by related work on vulnerability.
spot: Notice how you admire a friend who admits a mistake openly, while dreading doing the same.
use: Share one small, honest admission today: "I don't know how to do this" or "I was wrong about that."
guard: Vulnerability works best with people who've earned trust, and in moderation. Oversharing with strangers is a different thing.
predict: How did people judge vulnerability in others compared with in themselves?
* More positively in others
- More positively in themselves
- About the same
scenario: Saurabh thinks admitting his error in the client report will make him look incompetent. When he does, his manager thanks him for his honesty and trusts him more.
source: Bruk, A., Scholl, S. G., & Bless, H. (2018). Beautiful mess effect. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: liking-gap, pratfall-effect, illusion-of-transparency

## fundamental-attribution-error
title: The fundamental attribution error
aka: Correspondence bias
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: When someone cuts you off, they're a jerk. When you cut someone off, you're late for something important.
what: When explaining other people's behaviour, we overweight their character and underweight their situation. We see their actions but not the pressures behind them. When we explain our own behaviour, we see the situation clearly. It's one of the main sources of unfair judgements.
study: In 1967 Edward Jones and Victor Harris had people read essays for or against Fidel Castro. Even when told the writer had been assigned which side to argue, readers still judged that the writer personally held that view. Lee Ross named it "the fundamental attribution error" in 1977. Cross-cultural research suggests it's weaker in some East Asian contexts, and a 2006 meta-analysis found the related "actor-observer" difference is much smaller than textbooks claim.
case: In November 1991, two mass shootings happened in the US within two weeks. In Iowa, Gang Lu, a Chinese physics graduate student who had lost out on an academic award, shot his adviser and others before killing himself. In Royal Oak, Michigan, Thomas McIlvane, a postal worker who had been fired, shot his supervisor and others. In 1994 Michael Morris and Kaiping Peng compared coverage in the New York Times with coverage in the World Journal, a Chinese-language US newspaper. The English-language reports leaned on the killers' personal traits: a bad temper, a sinister personality, psychological problems. The Chinese-language reports leaned on situations: isolation, a rivalry, the availability of guns, a recent firing. The same pattern held for both killers. When American and Chinese students were asked to weigh possible causes, the same cultural difference appeared. The nuance: the error isn't a fixed human constant; how strongly we blame character over context is partly learned.
proof: The tendency to over-attribute to character is well replicated; how universal and how large it is are debated.
spot: Notice "he's just lazy", "she's so rude" or "they don't care" about someone whose situation you don't know.
use: The next time someone annoys you today, think of two situational reasons for their behaviour before deciding it's their character.
guard: Ask "what would I have done in their situation?" before concluding what kind of person they are.
predict: Readers were told an essay writer had been assigned their position on Castro. What did readers conclude?
- That the essay said nothing about the writer's views
* That the writer still personally held that view
- That the writer probably held the opposite view
scenario: The delivery executive arrives late and short-tempered. Tanya decides he's rude, not knowing he'd been sent to three wrong addresses in the rain.
source: Jones, E. E., & Harris, V. A. (1967). The attribution of attitudes. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology; Ross, L. (1977). Advances in Experimental Social Psychology.
related: self-serving-bias, bias-blind-spot, just-world

## talking-to-strangers
title: Talking to strangers
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: We avoid chatting with strangers because we expect it to be awkward. It's usually far more pleasant than predicted.
what: People think talking to a stranger on a train or in a queue will be unpleasant, and assume the stranger doesn't want to talk either. So everyone stays silent. When people are nudged to talk, they're happier, and the conversations go better than they predicted.
study: In 2014 Nicholas Epley and Juliana Schroeder asked Chicago commuters to either talk to a stranger, keep to themselves, or commute as usual. Commuters predicted talking would be the worst, but those who talked reported the most pleasant journey. They also underestimated how interested others would be in talking. Later week-long studies found fears of rejection shrank with practice.
case: In 2014 Gillian Sandstrom and Elizabeth Dunn stood outside a busy Starbucks and recruited customers on their way in. Half were asked to have a genuine interaction with the cashier: smile, make eye contact and have a brief conversation. The other half were asked to be as efficient as possible: get their coffee and go, avoiding unnecessary conversation. Afterwards, those who had chatted reported a better mood and a greater sense of belonging than the efficient ones, and the feeling of belonging appeared to explain the boost. The nuance: the "relationship" lasted seconds and was with someone they might never see again. People skip these moments because they seem trivial or because they don't want to bother anyone; the study suggests both the cost and the triviality are overestimated. It was a small study, so treat it as a hint rather than a law, but it fits the larger research on commuters and strangers.
proof: Replicated in several settings and countries; effects are moderate and consistent.
spot: Notice everyone in a lift or waiting room looking at their phones, each probably assuming the others don't want to talk.
use: Start one short conversation with a stranger today: the person next to you in a queue, a shopkeeper, a cab driver.
guard: Read the cues: some people genuinely want quiet. A friendly line and a quick exit if it doesn't land is enough.
predict: Commuters assigned to talk to a stranger, compared with those who kept to themselves, reported their journey as...
- More unpleasant, as they had predicted
- About the same
* More pleasant
scenario: Stuck at the airport for three hours, Neel starts chatting with the man next to him about cricket. The delay suddenly feels much shorter.
source: Epley, N., & Schroeder, J. (2014). Mistakenly seeking solitude. Journal of Experimental Psychology: General.
related: liking-gap, weak-ties

## curse-of-knowledge
title: The curse of knowledge
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: Once you know something, it's hard to imagine not knowing it. It's why experts explain things badly.
what: When we know something, we can't easily simulate what it's like not to know it. So we skip steps, use jargon, and assume our meaning is obvious. Teachers, managers and engineers all suffer from it.
study: In 1990 Elizabeth Newton asked people to tap out the rhythm of well-known songs, like "Happy Birthday", on a table, and predict how many listeners would recognise them. Tappers predicted about 50%. Listeners named only 3 of the 120 songs, 2.5%. The tappers could hear the melody in their heads; the listeners heard only knocking.
case: In 1999 Pamela Hinds asked people to predict how long a complete beginner would take to learn a set of tasks on a mobile phone, exactly the kind of thing explained in shops every day. Her predictors included experts, such as experienced phone salespeople, as well as intermediate users and novices. The experts were the worst at it: they badly underestimated how long beginners would need, and novices took about twice as long as predicted. People with intermediate experience were more accurate. Hinds then tried debiasing techniques meant to make the experts take a beginner's point of view; they barely reduced the underestimation. The nuance: expertise isn't the problem; forgetting what it was like not to know is. That's why instructions are often best written, or at least tested, by someone who learned the task recently rather than by the person who knows it best.
proof: The tapping study is a doctoral dissertation; the broader effect is well supported in economics and education research.
spot: Notice when someone explains something with jargon or skips a step "everyone knows", or when your instructions confuse someone.
use: Explain something from your work to a friend outside your field today. Notice where you need to back up and explain.
guard: Test your explanations on a real beginner, or ask "can you tell me back what you understood?"
predict: Tappers predicted listeners would recognise 50% of tapped songs. What share did they actually recognise?
- About 40%
- About 20%
* About 2.5%
scenario: Raghu sends his mother a "simple" guide to using the banking app. She calls three times, stuck on steps he didn't think needed explaining.
source: Newton, E. L. (1990). Overconfidence in the communication of intent: Heard and unheard melodies. Doctoral dissertation, Stanford University; Camerer, C., Loewenstein, G., & Weber, M. (1989). Journal of Political Economy.
related: illusion-of-transparency, explanatory-depth

## mehrabian-myth
title: The 7-38-55 myth
category: connection
evidence: busted
hook: "Only 7% of communication is words." The study behind this famous claim says nothing of the kind.
what: You may have heard that communication is 7% words, 38% tone and 55% body language. That figure comes from two small 1967 studies where people heard single words spoken in a tone that conflicted with their meaning, and judged how much the speaker liked them. It was never about communication in general, and the researcher has said so.
study: Albert Mehrabian and colleagues' 1967 experiments used single words like "dear" or "terrible" spoken in positive, neutral or negative tones, sometimes alongside photos of faces. When word and tone conflicted, tone influenced judgements of liking more. Mehrabian has repeatedly stated that his findings don't apply to ordinary communication.
case: If 93% of meaning were nonverbal, you would understand someone's emotions far better on a video call than on a phone call. In 2017 Michael Kraus tested something close to this across five experiments with about 1,800 participants. People either interacted with others or watched recordings of strangers interacting, and had to judge what the others were feeling, with voice only, video only, or both. Voice-only listeners were slightly more accurate at reading emotions than those who could also see faces; adding the face didn't improve accuracy. The nuance: Kraus's effect was small, and a later reanalysis argued it was too small to matter in practice. But even the sceptical reading is a problem for the 7-38-55 myth: words and voice alone carry a great deal of emotional meaning, and faces are not the master key that popular training courses claim.
proof: Busted as a general claim: it's a misreading of narrow studies, as the original researcher has said.
spot: Listen for "studies show 93% of communication is non-verbal" in presentations, trainings and social media posts.
use: Try explaining something today entirely by gestures for 30 seconds. Notice how much words actually carry.
guard: Tone and body language matter, especially when they contradict words. But words carry most of the information. Choose them carefully.
predict: What did Mehrabian's 1967 studies actually measure?
- How much information people take from body language in conversations
* How people judged liking from single words in conflicting tones
- How job interviewers rate candidates
scenario: At a corporate training, the trainer says "words don't matter, only 7% of communication is verbal", then hands out 40 pages of written notes.
source: Mehrabian, A., & Wiener, M. (1967). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Mehrabian, A., & Ferris, S. R. (1967). Journal of Consulting Psychology.
related: replication-crisis

## compliments
title: Compliments matter more than you think
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: We hold back compliments because we expect them to be awkward. They land far better than we predict.
what: People worry their compliment will seem insincere or make the other person uncomfortable, and underestimate how happy it will make them. So sincere compliments go unsaid. It's a simple, free way to make someone's day that we use too little.
study: In 2021 Erica Boothby and Vanessa Bohns had people write compliments to friends and strangers and predict the reaction. People underestimated how good the recipient would feel and overestimated how awkward they would feel. The more someone worried about how they'd come across, the more they underestimated the impact.
case: Xuan Zhao and Nicholas Epley asked a practical question: if a compliment feels good once, does it wear thin if you hear one every day? In a 2021 study, people received a different compliment each day for five days and reported how each one felt, while others predicted how recipients would react. The predictors expected diminishing returns: by the fifth day, they thought compliments would feel less positive and less sincere. The recipients didn't adapt; they felt about as good on day five as on day one. The nuance: we treat kind words like a resource that loses value with use, so we ration them. For the person receiving them, they behaved more like a renewable resource. Frequency didn't spoil the compliments; what makes a compliment land is that it is specific and sincere.
proof: Supported by several studies in the original paper and by related work on gratitude and reaching out.
spot: Notice when you think something nice about someone ("great presentation", "your garden looks lovely") but don't say it.
use: Give two sincere, specific compliments today, one to someone you don't know well.
guard: Specific beats general: "the way you explained the budget was so clear" lands better than "nice job".
predict: How did people's predictions about compliments compare with the actual reactions?
* They underestimated how good recipients felt and overestimated the awkwardness
- They were accurate
- They overestimated how good recipients felt
scenario: Ajay thinks the night-shift nurse's care for his father was remarkable, but doesn't say so, worried it would be strange.
source: Boothby, E. J., & Bohns, V. K. (2021). Why a simple act of kindness is not as simple as it seems. Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin.
related: gratitude-letters, reaching-out, liking-gap

## bids-for-connection
title: Bids for connection
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: "Look at that bird!" is a small request for attention. How partners respond to these bids predicts whether they stay together.
what: John Gottman calls small attempts to connect "bids": a comment, a question, a touch, a joke. The other person can turn towards it (engage), turn away (ignore it) or turn against it (respond irritably). These tiny moments add up to the emotional climate of a relationship.
study: In Gottman's lab, newlywed couples spent a day in a flat-like setting while researchers observed their interactions. According to Gottman's research reports, couples still married six years later had turned towards each other's bids about 86% of the time; those who divorced, about 33%. Broader research on "responsiveness" by Harry Reis and others supports the importance of feeling noticed and understood.
case: In 2018 Ryan Dwyer, Kostadin Kushlev and Elizabeth Dunn invited more than 300 people to share a meal at a café with friends or family, in groups of three to five. Groups were randomly told either to keep their phones on the table or to put them away. Those with phones present felt more distracted and enjoyed the meal somewhat less. The nuance: this is Gottman's "turning away" in its most modern form. No one decided to ignore a friend; a buzz or a glance was enough to miss a small bid for attention, like a story half-heard or a joke that got no reply. The effect at any single dinner was modest, which is exactly why it goes unnoticed. Bids are mostly small, and so are the misses, but Gottman's couples suggest they add up over the years.
proof: The importance of responsiveness is well supported; the specific percentages come from the Gottman lab's own reports.
spot: Notice small bids around you: a sigh, "guess what happened today", a shared link, and how people respond.
use: Today, catch one bid from someone close to you and turn fully towards it: put down your phone, look, and respond.
guard: Turning towards doesn't mean agreeing or dropping everything; a warm "tell me in five minutes, I want to hear it" counts.
predict: In Gottman's newlywed study, how often had couples who stayed married turned towards each other's bids?
- About 33% of the time
- About 55% of the time
* About 86% of the time
scenario: Kavya says "the sunset's amazing tonight" while her husband scrolls his phone. He grunts without looking up, and she goes quiet.
source: Driver, J. L., & Gottman, J. M. (2004). Daily marital interactions and positive affect during marital conflict among newlywed couples. Family Process.
related: capitalization

## mere-exposure
title: The mere exposure effect
category: connection
evidence: solid
hook: The more we see something, the more we tend to like it, even if we don't remember seeing it.
what: Repeated exposure makes things familiar, and familiarity feels safe and pleasant. It works on faces, music, words and brands, and is one reason we become friends with neighbours and colleagues. Proximity matters: people who are physically near each other end up liking each other more.
study: Robert Zajonc showed in 1968 that repeated exposure increased liking for nonsense words and faces. In 1992 Richard Moreland and Scott Beach had four similar-looking women attend a lecture course 0, 5, 10 or 15 times, without interacting. At the end, students rated the woman who had attended most often as more attractive and more similar to them. Earlier, in 1950, a study of student housing found friendships formed mostly between people living closest together.
case: Which version of your face do you prefer? In 1977 Theodore Mita, Marshall Dermer and Jeffrey Knight photographed 33 women students and printed each photo two ways: as a true image, the way others see them, and mirror-reversed, the way they see themselves every morning. Each woman, and a close friend she had brought along, chose which print they liked better. The women mostly preferred their mirror image; their friends mostly preferred the true one. Same face, two audiences, each preferring the version they had seen more often. The nuance: this explains a familiar discomfort, why your own photos often look "off" to you while friends insist they're fine. It also shows exposure shaping a judgement people believe they make objectively. Faces aren't perfectly symmetrical, so a mirrored photo really is a slightly different face; familiarity decides which one looks right.
proof: Supported by a meta-analysis of over 200 experiments; the effect weakens with too many exposures.
spot: Notice songs you disliked on first hearing that grew on you, or ads repeated until a brand feels trustworthy.
use: If you want to build a connection, show up regularly: the same gym time, the same café, the same weekly call.
guard: Familiar doesn't mean good. Ask whether you like something on its merits or just because it's familiar (a politician, a product, an old habit).
predict: Women attended a lecture course 0, 5, 10 or 15 times without speaking to anyone. How did students rate them?
- All about the same
* The more she attended, the more attractive she was rated
- The one who never attended was rated highest, as most mysterious
scenario: Ravi barely noticed the woman at the next desk in his first month. By the sixth month of seeing her every day, they've become close friends.
source: Zajonc, R. B. (1968). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Moreland, R. L., & Beach, S. R. (1992). Journal of Experimental Social Psychology; Festinger, L., Schachter, S., & Back, K. (1950). Social Pressures in Informal Groups.
related: illusory-truth-effect, similarity

## capitalization
title: Capitalisation
aka: Active-constructive responding
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: How you respond to good news matters more than how you respond to bad news.
what: Sharing good news ("capitalising") boosts its joy, but only if the listener responds well. An enthusiastic, curious response ("That's amazing! Tell me everything!") builds the relationship. A flat "nice", a change of subject or a "but what about..." quietly erodes it.
study: In 2004 Shelly Gable, Harry Reis, Emily Impett and Evan Asher found that people whose partners typically responded actively and constructively to their good news reported more relationship satisfaction and intimacy. In a 2006 study, responses to good news predicted relationship well-being and break-ups better than responses to bad news.
case: In 2010 Harry Reis, Shannon Smith, Cheryl Carmichael and colleagues tested why sharing good news helps. Across several studies, participants told a listener, sometimes just an experimenter they had never met, about a recent positive event. Depending on the condition, the listener responded with genuine enthusiasm or more neutrally, or the participant simply wrote the event down without sharing it. When the listener responded enthusiastically, people valued the event more, and it seemed more important to them; enthusiastic listeners were also liked and trusted more. The nuance: capitalisation isn't only about the relationship. The listener's response changes how much the good event itself is worth to the person who had it. A lukewarm "nice" to a partner's news costs more than it seems, because it quietly shrinks their good moment.
proof: Supported by several studies with couples and friends.
spot: Notice how people respond when someone shares good news: enthusiasm, a quick "nice", a worry, or a story about themselves.
use: The next time someone shares good news today, ask two follow-up questions before saying anything about yourself.
guard: Don't fake it; genuine curiosity is what matters. And save the "but have you thought about..." for later.
predict: Which response to a partner's good news predicted relationship well-being best?
- Calm, measured responses that point out risks
* Enthusiastic, curious responses
- Quickly changing the subject to keep things balanced
scenario: When Priya announces her promotion, her sister says "Wow! How did they tell you? What will you do first?" rather than "Will you have more work now?"
source: Gable, S. L., Reis, H. T., Impett, E. A., & Asher, E. R. (2004). What do you do when things go right? Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: bids-for-connection

## fast-friends
title: Fast friends
aka: The 36 questions
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: 45 minutes of gradually deeper questions can make strangers feel as close as months of small talk.
what: Closeness grows through mutual, escalating self-disclosure: you share a bit, they share a bit, and you both go a little deeper. A structured set of questions that does this can build surprising closeness quickly. The famous "36 questions to fall in love" come from this research.
study: In 1997 Arthur Aron and colleagues paired strangers to take turns asking each other 36 questions that grew more personal, from "Given the choice of anyone in the world, whom would you want as a dinner guest?" to "When did you last cry in front of another person?" After 45 minutes, they felt much closer than pairs who made small talk. Later studies replicated the effect across cultures.
case: In 2008 Elizabeth Page-Gould, Rodolfo Mendoza-Denton and Linda Tropp used the fast-friends method to build friendships across ethnic lines at Berkeley. White and Latino students were paired with a same-gender partner of either their own or the other ethnicity and met three times, working through increasingly personal questions together. The researchers measured cortisol, a stress hormone, during the meetings. Students with signs of implicit prejudice, or who worried about rejection by the other group, showed high cortisol in their first cross-group meeting, but it fell over the sessions as the friendship formed. Afterwards, in daily diaries, these students sought out more interactions with members of the other group. The nuance: the closeness wasn't produced by talking about prejudice. It came from the ordinary, structured disclosure that builds any friendship, and the people who gained most were those who started out most uncomfortable.
proof: Replicated in several studies; it builds a feeling of closeness, not necessarily lasting relationships.
spot: Notice conversations where both people gradually share more, and how close you feel afterwards compared with small talk.
use: Ask someone one deeper question today instead of "How was your day?", like "What was the best part of your week?"
guard: The key is mutual and gradual. Asking deep questions without sharing yourself, or going too fast, feels like an interrogation.
predict: After 45 minutes of escalating questions, how close did strangers feel compared with those who made small talk?
- About the same
- Less close; the questions felt forced
* Much closer
scenario: Seated next to a stranger at a wedding, Aditi skips the weather talk and asks what he'd do if he didn't need money. They talk for two hours.
source: Aron, A., et al. (1997). The experimental generation of interpersonal closeness. Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin.
related: liking-gap, beautiful-mess

## reaching-out
title: The surprise of reaching out
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: A quick "thinking of you" message to an old friend is appreciated far more than you expect.
what: We hesitate to reach out to people we've lost touch with, worried it will seem odd or unwelcome. In reality people appreciate being contacted much more than we predict, especially when it's a surprise.
study: In 2022 Peggy Liu, SoYon Rim, Lauren Min and Kate Min ran experiments where people sent a short note or a small gift to someone in their social circle they hadn't been in touch with recently. Senders consistently underestimated how much recipients would appreciate it, and the more surprising the contact, the more it was appreciated.
case: In the late 1960s and early 1970s, psychiatrist Jerome Motto followed 843 people in San Francisco who had been hospitalised for depression or suicidal thoughts and had refused ongoing treatment after discharge. Half were randomly assigned to receive short personal letters from a staff member they had met, monthly at first and then less often, over five years. The letters asked nothing; they said, in effect, that the writer was thinking of them and would be glad to hear from them. Over the first two years, the suicide rate among those who received letters was about half that of those who didn't; the difference faded in later years. The nuance: these were people who had turned down help, and a few lines of human contact reached them when treatment hadn't. Caring-contact programmes based on Motto's design are now used by crisis services and militaries, although later trials have had mixed results.
proof: Supported by several experiments in the original paper; consistent with related work on compliments and gratitude.
spot: Notice when you think of someone you haven't spoken to in a while, then don't message them because "it's been too long".
use: Send one short "thought of you today because..." message to someone you haven't spoken to in months.
guard: Keep it light and with no request attached. A first message that asks for a favour feels different.
predict: When people reached out to someone they'd lost touch with, how did their expectations compare with the reaction?
- They expected more appreciation than they got
- About right
* They underestimated how much it was appreciated
scenario: Seeing a photo of the hostel where they shared a room, Anil sends it to his old roommate. His friend replies within a minute, thrilled, and they talk for an hour.
source: Liu, P. J., Rim, S., Min, L., & Min, K. E. (2022). The surprise of reaching out. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: compliments, gratitude-letters

## gratitude-letters
title: Undervaluing gratitude
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: People writing thank-you letters expect the reader to feel awkward. Readers feel moved.
what: Expressing gratitude helps both people, but we hold back because we misjudge the reaction. We worry about our wording and the awkwardness, while recipients focus on the warmth of being appreciated.
study: In 2018 Amit Kumar and Nicholas Epley had people write letters of gratitude to someone who had mattered to them. Writers predicted how surprised, happy and awkward recipients would feel. Recipients were more surprised and happier, and felt much less awkward, than writers expected. Writers also underestimated how warm and competent their letters would seem.
case: In 2005 Martin Seligman, Tracy Steen, Nansook Park and Christopher Peterson ran an online randomised trial comparing five happiness exercises with a placebo exercise (writing about early memories). One was the "gratitude visit": write a letter to someone who had been especially kind to you but whom you had never properly thanked, then deliver it, ideally in person, reading it aloud. It produced the biggest immediate rise in happiness and fall in depressive symptoms of any exercise, lasting about a month. By three months its effects had faded, while two quieter exercises, writing down three good things each day and using a personal strength in a new way, kept working for months. The nuance: a single grand gesture moves you a lot, briefly; a small habit moves you less, for longer. Kumar and Epley's research adds that the recipient benefits too, more than the writer expects.
proof: Replicated across several experiments in the original paper and related studies.
spot: Notice gratitude you feel towards teachers, colleagues or family that you've never actually expressed.
use: Write a short message of specific thanks to someone today. Tell them what they did and how it affected you.
guard: Make it specific and sincere. And don't expect a particular reaction; the value is in the giving.
predict: How awkward did recipients of gratitude letters feel compared with what writers predicted?
* Much less awkward
- About as awkward
- More awkward
scenario: Sneha has always credited her Class 9 maths teacher for her career in engineering, but has never told him, assuming it would embarrass him.
source: Kumar, A., & Epley, N. (2018). Undervaluing gratitude. Psychological Science.
related: compliments, reaching-out, prosocial-spending

## similarity
title: Similarity attracts
category: connection
evidence: solid
hook: Opposites attract in films. In real life, we're drawn to people who are like us.
what: We tend to like people who share our attitudes, values, background and interests. Similarity makes interactions easier, validates our views and predicts understanding. Even small, trivial similarities (a shared birthday, the same hometown) can create a sense of connection.
study: In the 1960s and 1970s Donn Byrne showed that people liked strangers more when they believed the strangers shared their attitudes. A 2008 meta-analysis by Matthew Montoya, Robert Horton and Jeffrey Kirchner of over 300 studies found that actual similarity drives attraction mainly before people get to know each other, while perceived similarity matters more in existing relationships.
case: In 2013 Paul Tidwell, Paul Eastwick and Eli Finkel ran speed-dating events where students first filled in detailed questionnaires about their personalities and attitudes, then had 4-minute dates with around a dozen people. After each date, they rated how attracted they felt and how similar they thought their date was to them. Actual similarity, calculated from the questionnaires, didn't predict attraction. Perceived similarity, the feeling that "we're alike", strongly did. The nuance: this fits the meta-analysis above. Before meeting, people use real overlaps in attitudes as a cue. Once they meet and click, they tend to see similarity in people they already like. "We have so much in common" is often a result of attraction as much as a cause of it.
proof: One of the most consistent findings in relationship science, supported by meta-analysis.
spot: Notice how quickly you warm to someone who shares your hometown, school or favourite team, and how salespeople mention "I'm from there too!"
use: When meeting someone new today, look for one genuine thing you have in common and mention it.
guard: Be aware when similarity is being used on you ("I also love dogs!"), and when it keeps your circle narrow.
predict: According to a meta-analysis, when does actual similarity matter most for attraction?
* Before people know each other well
- Only in long marriages
- Never; opposites attract more
scenario: The new hire mentions she grew up in Indore, and Varun, also from Indore, immediately feels they'll get on well.
source: Byrne, D. (1971). The Attraction Paradigm; Montoya, R. M., Horton, R. S., & Kirchner, J. (2008). Journal of Social and Personal Relationships.
related: mere-exposure, minimal-group

## pratfall-effect
title: The pratfall effect
category: connection
evidence: debated
hook: A highly competent person who spills coffee on themselves becomes more likeable. But only if they seemed competent first.
what: When someone who seems almost too perfect makes a small, human mistake, it can make them more relatable and likeable. But for someone who isn't seen as competent, the same blunder makes things worse. And the effect seems to depend on who's watching.
study: In 1966 Elliot Aronson, Ben Willerman and Joanne Floyd had people listen to a recording of a quiz contestant. When a highly competent contestant spilled coffee over himself, listeners rated him more likeable. When an average contestant did the same, he was rated less likeable. Later studies found the effect varies with the observer's own self-esteem and gender.
case: In 2012 Danit Ein-Gar, Baba Shiv and Zakary Tormala tested a commercial version of the pratfall effect, which they called "blemishing". In lab and field studies, people read glowing descriptions of products. Adding a small negative detail, such as a minor limitation, made people like the product more, but only under specific conditions: when they weren't scrutinising the information closely, and when the negative came after the positives rather than before them. When people paid close attention, or the flaw came first, the blemish simply hurt. The nuance: as with Aronson's quiz contestant, a small flaw helps only when the strengths are already established and the flaw is clearly minor. Then it highlights the positives and makes the description more believable; otherwise it is just a flaw. That conditionality is why the pratfall effect is classed as debated rather than solid.
proof: Debated: the classic study is influential, but later results are mixed and depend on the observer.
spot: Notice leaders or celebrities who share small, harmless flaws, and whether it makes them more appealing.
use: If you're in a position of expertise today, admit one small, harmless gap ("I always forget this formula, let me check").
guard: It only helps after competence is established, and only for small mistakes. A serious error is just a serious error.
predict: When an average-seeming contestant spilled coffee, how did listeners rate him?
- More likeable, like the expert
* Less likeable
- No change
scenario: The intimidatingly polished CEO trips over a cable during her keynote, laughs at herself, and the audience warms to her immediately.
source: Aronson, E., Willerman, B., & Floyd, J. (1966). The effect of a pratfall on increasing interpersonal attractiveness. Psychonomic Science.
related: beautiful-mess

## weak-ties
title: The strength of weak ties
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: Your next job is more likely to come from an acquaintance than from a close friend.
what: Close friends tend to know the same people and hear the same news as you. Acquaintances move in different circles, so they bring new information and opportunities. Weak ties also brighten everyday life: friendly chats with the barista or a neighbour make days happier.
study: In the 1970s Mark Granovetter found that most people who got jobs through contacts heard about them from people they saw only occasionally or rarely. In 2022 a team led by Karthik Rajkumar analysed experiments on LinkedIn involving about 20 million people and found that moderately weak ties were the most helpful for job mobility. In 2014 Gillian Sandstrom and Elizabeth Dunn found people were happier on days they interacted with more weak ties.
case: In 2017 Laura Gee, Jason Jones and Moira Burke used de-identified data on about 6 million Facebook users to revisit Granovetter. They identified people who started working at a company where one of their Facebook friends already worked, and measured how strong each friendship was by how often the two interacted. Two things were true at once. Any single strong tie was more likely to have helped than any single weak tie. But because people have far more weak ties than strong ones, most of these job moves still ran through weak ties. The nuance: Granovetter's finding survives, but for a different reason than the popular version suggests. Weak ties aren't individually more useful; there are simply many more of them, and they carry information your close circle already shares.
proof: Supported by classic research and a very large field experiment; the details vary by industry.
spot: Notice how often useful tips, opportunities or recommendations come from people you barely know.
use: Reconnect with one acquaintance today: comment on their post, ask how their new job is going, or say hi to a neighbour.
guard: Weak ties don't replace close ones for support. You need both.
predict: In the large LinkedIn study, which ties helped job mobility most?
- The closest ties
* Moderately weak ties
- People you've never interacted with
scenario: Harish gets his dream job after a casual chat with a former classmate he hadn't spoken to in years, not through his close friends.
source: Granovetter, M. S. (1973). The strength of weak ties. American Journal of Sociology; Rajkumar, K., et al. (2022). Science; Sandstrom, G. M., & Dunn, E. W. (2014). Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin.
related: talking-to-strangers

## social-connection-health
title: Connection and health
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: Strong social ties are linked to longer life, on a par with well-known health risks like smoking.
what: People with stronger social relationships tend to live longer, recover better from illness and have lower risk of many diseases. Loneliness and isolation are linked to worse health. Most of this evidence is observational, so cause and effect aren't fully pinned down, but the pattern is striking and consistent.
study: In 2010 Julianne Holt-Lunstad, Timothy Smith and Bradley Layton analysed 148 studies involving over 300,000 people. Those with stronger social relationships had about a 50% greater likelihood of survival over the study periods (averaging 7.5 years). The size of the effect was comparable to quitting smoking and larger than many other well-known risk factors.
case: In 2020, during the first COVID-19 lockdowns, Maninder Kahlon and colleagues at Dell Medical School in Texas ran a randomised trial with 240 older adults who received meals from a Meals on Wheels programme. Sixteen young callers were given two hours of training in empathetic listening and phoned participants several times a week for four weeks, letting them steer the conversation to whatever they wanted to talk about. Compared with a control group who received no calls, those who were called reported significantly less loneliness, depression and anxiety after four weeks. The nuance: the callers weren't therapists and the calls weren't treatment. The active ingredient seemed to be someone reliably listening. It was a small, short trial, but it shows that the link between connection and health isn't just something to measure; it can be acted on cheaply.
proof: A large, consistent body of observational evidence; causation is likely but not proven by these studies.
spot: Notice who in your life is becoming isolated: an elderly neighbour, a friend who has moved, a colleague who eats alone.
use: Make one plan today to spend time with someone in person this week.
guard: Quality matters more than quantity. A few supportive relationships beat many shallow ones.
predict: In a review of 148 studies, how much more likely to survive were people with stronger social relationships?
- About 5%
- About 20%
* About 50%
scenario: After his wife passes away, Mr Rao stops going to the temple and his walking group. Within a year his health declines noticeably.
source: Holt-Lunstad, J., Smith, T. B., & Layton, J. B. (2010). Social relationships and mortality risk. PLoS Medicine.
related: weak-ties

## halo-effect
title: The halo effect
category: connection
evidence: solid
hook: Strangers picked which of two candidates looked more competent, from faces alone. The competent-looking one won about 7 in 10 Senate races.
what: One prominent good trait, such as looks, confidence or a polished presentation, casts a glow over everything else we judge about a person or product, including traits we have no information about. It works in reverse too: one bad trait drags down the rest. Because the impression feels like a set of separate judgements, we don't notice it's one feeling repeated.
study: Edward Thorndike named the effect in 1920 after finding that officers' ratings of their soldiers' looks, intelligence, leadership and character were far too closely related to be independent judgements. In 1977 Richard Nisbett and Timothy Wilson showed students a video of a lecturer behaving either warmly or coldly. Students who saw the warm version found the lecturer's appearance, mannerisms and accent appealing; those who saw the cold version found the same features irritating. Both groups denied that the lecturer's manner had affected those ratings.
case: In 2005 Alexander Todorov and colleagues showed people pairs of photos of rival candidates in real US congressional elections and asked who looked more competent. The more competent-looking face belonged to the winner in about 69% of the 2004 Senate races, although the raters knew nothing about the candidates, and a one-second look was enough to form the same judgement. Later studies found the same with glimpses of a tenth of a second, and with Swiss children choosing a "captain" for a boat trip from the faces of real French candidates. The effect on votes is largest among voters who know little about the candidates. The nuance: a competent-looking face says almost nothing about competence in office; the studies show that voters partly vote on the glow.
proof: Among the oldest and most replicated findings in social psychology, including meta-analyses showing that attractive people are judged and treated more favourably across many areas of life.
spot: Notice when one thing you like about someone, such as a confident voice, a famous employer, good looks or a sleek website, makes you assume they're good at things you haven't seen.
use: Pick one person or product you admire. List one specific thing you've actually seen them do well, and one thing you're only assuming.
guard: When evaluating people (hiring, reviewing, choosing a doctor), judge each quality separately against evidence, and decide what matters before you meet them.
