predict: The candidate whose face strangers rated as more competent won what share of the 2004 US Senate races?
- About 50%, no better than chance
* About 69%
- About 95%
scenario: Kavya, the new hire, speaks fluently in meetings and dresses sharply, so her manager Meenal assumes Kavya's spreadsheets must be accurate too. Meenal doesn't check them, and the first one contains a costly error.
source: Thorndike, E. L. (1920). A constant error in psychological ratings. Journal of Applied Psychology; Nisbett, R. E., & Wilson, T. D. (1977). The halo effect: Evidence for unconscious alteration of judgments. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Todorov, A., et al. (2005). Inferences of competence from faces predict election outcomes. Science.
related: affect-heuristic, similarity, mere-exposure

## lie-detection-myth
title: The myth of spotting liars
aka: Reading lies from body language
category: connection
evidence: busted
hook: Most people think liars avoid eye contact. Across 24,483 judges in 206 studies, people spotted lies and truths 54% of the time.
what: The belief that liars give themselves away, by avoiding eye contact, fidgeting, touching their faces or piling on detail, is held around the world. The research says behavioural cues to lying are faint and inconsistent, and people, including police and other professionals, are only slightly better than chance at telling lies from truth. Confidence and accuracy are barely related. Better results come from what people say and how they're questioned, not from watching their bodies.
study: In 2006 Charles Bond and Bella DePaulo combined 206 studies with 24,483 judges: people correctly classified 54% of statements, 47% of lies and 61% of truths. In 2003 DePaulo and colleagues analysed 158 possible cues to deception and found most had little or no link to lying; liars told somewhat less detailed and less compelling stories, the opposite of the popular "too many details" warning. In 2006 a survey across 58 countries found that "liars avoid eye contact" was the most common belief nearly everywhere, though gaze aversion isn't a reliable cue.
case: From 2007 the US Transportation Security Administration paid behaviour detection officers to spot potential terrorists at airports from signs such as fidgeting, sweating and gaze, under a programme called SPOT. In 2013 the Government Accountability Office reviewed it. The TSA had spent about $900 million; its own validation study was flawed; and decades of research suggested that people detect deception from behaviour at about chance level. The report found no evidence that the programme worked and advised Congress to limit its funding. The nuance: lie detection isn't hopeless. Methods that focus on what people say, such as asking for details that can be checked, do better than watching for nerves, because liars tend to avoid giving verifiable details.
proof: Busted as a skill most people have: hundreds of studies and large meta-analyses find accuracy near chance and only weak, inconsistent behavioural cues.
spot: Notice when someone, you included, claims to know a person was lying from their eyes, their posture, their nerves or how much they said.
use: The next time you suspect someone of lying today, ask a specific question whose answer can be checked instead of studying their face.
guard: Don't let a gut sense of dishonesty, or of honesty, settle anything important. Look for facts that can be verified.
predict: Across 206 studies, how often did people correctly tell lies from truths?
- About 75%; most people are decent lie detectors
- About 65%, and much higher for police
* About 54%, barely better than chance
scenario: Ritu is certain her tenant is lying about the broken tap because he kept looking at the floor. The plumber later finds that the pipe had simply corroded.
source: Bond, C. F., & DePaulo, B. M. (2006). Accuracy of deception judgments. Personality and Social Psychology Review; DePaulo, B. M., et al. (2003). Cues to deception. Psychological Bulletin; US Government Accountability Office (2013). Aviation security: TSA should limit future funding for behavior detection activities.
related: mehrabian-myth, illusion-of-transparency, overconfidence

## charm-that-fades
title: Charm that fades
aka: The narcissist's first impression
category: connection
evidence: good
hook: People high in narcissism are liked more than others after a first meeting. Seven weeks later, they're liked less.
what: Confidence, entertaining stories and a flattering self-presentation make a strong first impression, and self-enhancing, narcissistic people are good at all three. The traits that charm early start to grate with repeated contact, as arrogance and a habit of using people show through. So first impressions systematically favour people who often turn out to be worse colleagues, partners and leaders.
study: In 1998 Delroy Paulhus put students into discussion groups that met weekly. After the first meeting, members high in narcissism and self-enhancement were rated as more agreeable, competent and well adjusted than the others. After the seventh meeting, the same people were rated negatively. In 2010 Mitja Back, Stefan Schmukle and Boris Egloff found that narcissistic students were more popular at the very first meeting of a class, thanks to flashier clothes, charming expressions, self-assured movements and humour.
case: The same pattern appears at work. In 2015 Emily Grijalva and colleagues combined studies of narcissism and leadership. More narcissistic people were more likely to emerge as leaders, by being chosen or putting themselves forward, but once in charge they were no more effective when rated by their supervisors, peers or subordinates; only their ratings of themselves were higher. The link with emerging as leader was largely explained by extraversion, and the most effective leaders had moderate levels of narcissism. The nuance: not everyone charming is a narcissist, and some confidence helps. The lesson is to delay judgement: watch how someone behaves once the novelty wears off, especially toward people who can't help them.
proof: Shown in studies following groups over time and supported by meta-analysis, though the effects are moderate and depend on how narcissism is measured.
spot: Notice who dazzles at a first meeting, interview or first date, and check later whether the impression held up.
use: Think of someone whose first impression you loved. Write one thing they did in the first week and one thing they did months later, and compare.
guard: For decisions that last, such as hiring, partnerships or choosing a leader, ask people who've known the person a long time instead of trusting your own first meeting.
predict: In weekly discussion groups, how were people high in narcissism rated after the first meeting compared with the seventh?
- Disliked at first, then liked as people got to know them
* Liked more than others at first, rated negatively by the seventh
- Rated about the same throughout
scenario: At the networking dinner, Vikram is the most entertaining person at the table and everyone wants his number. Six months into working with him, his co-founder finds him exhausting: he talks over people, sulks when challenged and ignores anyone junior.
source: Paulhus, D. L. (1998). Interpersonal and intrapsychic adaptiveness of trait self-enhancement. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Back, M. D., Schmukle, S. C., & Egloff, B. (2010). Why are narcissists so charming at first sight? Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Grijalva, E., et al. (2015). Narcissism and leadership. Personnel Psychology.
related: halo-effect, forced-teaming, better-than-average-effect

## romeo-and-juliet-effect
title: The Romeo and Juliet effect myth
category: connection
evidence: busted
hook: Parents' opposition was supposed to make couples fall harder for each other. A registered replication found the opposite.
what: The Romeo and Juliet effect is the idea that when parents or friends oppose a relationship, the couple's love grows stronger, a romantic version of reactance. It's widely repeated, including in Robert Cialdini's Influence. But the 1972 study behind it hasn't held up: most evidence shows that disapproval from friends and family weakens relationships, which researchers call the social network effect.
study: In 1972 Richard Driscoll, Keith Davis and Milton Lipetz surveyed 140 couples and reported that increases in parental interference went with increases in romantic love over the following months. In 2014 Colleen Sinclair, Kristina Hood and Brittany Wright repeated the study as a registered report using the original measures, following 396 people for three to four months. They found no Romeo and Juliet effect: more interference and less approval from friends and family went with less love, commitment and trust.
case: In 2015 Sinclair, Diane Felmlee, Susan Sprecher and Wright tested the idea three ways: a survey of 858 people, written scenarios in which friends' and parents' reactions to a partner were varied, and a lab dating game in which peers approved or disapproved of a potential date. Across all three, approval increased love and commitment and disapproval reduced them. People with a strong desire to make their own decisions were less swayed by disapproval, but they didn't fall harder because of it. The nuance: forbidden love makes great stories because it's unusual. Your friends' doubts about a partner are, on average, worth hearing, not proof the relationship is special.
proof: Busted: a registered replication with the original measures, and larger studies using several methods, found the opposite of the original result.
spot: Notice films, songs and advice that treat opposition as proof of true love, and couples who cite outsiders' doubts as a reason to commit faster.
use: Think of a relationship, yours or a friend's, that others questioned. Write down what their doubts actually were, separately from how the doubts made you feel.
guard: When people close to you doubt a relationship, treat it as information rather than a challenge to defy. Ask what they've seen that you haven't.
predict: When researchers repeated the 1972 study with the original measures, what did more interference from family and friends go with?
- Stronger love and commitment, as in 1972
* Less love, commitment and trust
- No change in the relationship at all
scenario: When Arnav's parents forbid him to see Tara, he tells his friends the ban proves how special their love is, and that if his parents had approved he'd probably have lost interest by now.
source: Driscoll, R., Davis, K. E., & Lipetz, M. E. (1972). Parental interference and romantic love: The Romeo and Juliet effect. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Sinclair, H. C., Hood, K. B., & Wright, B. L. (2014). Revisiting the Romeo and Juliet effect. Social Psychology; Sinclair, H. C., et al. (2015). Don't tell me who I can't love. Social Psychology Quarterly.
related: reactance, social-connection-health, backfire-effect

# =====================================================================================
# CHOICES & MONEY
# =====================================================================================

## default-effect
title: The default effect
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: Make organ donation the default and nearly everyone is a donor. Make it opt-in and few are.
what: Whatever happens if you do nothing has a huge pull. Defaults feel like recommendations, changing them takes effort, and switching feels like a loss. Pre-ticked boxes, auto-renewals and pre-set options shape millions of decisions.
study: In 2003 Eric Johnson and Daniel Goldstein compared European countries. Where organ donation was opt-in, consent rates ranged from about 4% to 28%. Where it was opt-out, rates ranged from about 86% to nearly 100%. In 2001 Brigitte Madrian and Dennis Shea found that automatically enrolling new employees in a US company's retirement plan raised participation from 37% to 86%. A 2019 meta-analysis confirmed that defaults reliably change choices.
case: In 2015 Felix Ebeling and Sebastian Lotz ran a field experiment on the website of a German energy supplier, with tens of thousands of households signing up for electricity. For some, the box for a slightly more expensive green-electricity tariff was already ticked; for others, it was empty and had to be ticked. When green was the default, about 69% of new customers kept it; when it had to be actively chosen, about 7% did. The extra cost was small but real, and nothing else about the offer changed. The nuance: defaults work partly because people read them as a recommendation and partly because changing them takes effort, which makes them powerful and ethically loaded. Whoever designs the form is, in effect, making the choice for most people. That is why sensible defaults often achieve more than persuasive campaigns, and why a default that serves the company rather than the customer deserves suspicion.
proof: One of the strongest findings in behavioural science, confirmed by meta-analysis.
spot: Look for pre-ticked boxes, "auto-renew" settings, default tips in apps and the pre-selected insurance add-on at checkout.
use: Change one default in your life today to the one you'd actively choose: turn off auto-play, set a savings auto-transfer, or move a snack out of sight.
guard: For every subscription or form, ask: "If this box weren't ticked, would I tick it?"
predict: When new employees were automatically enrolled in a retirement plan, participation went from 37% to...
- 45%
- 60%
* 86%
scenario: The food delivery app pre-selects "Add ₹30 for eco-friendly packaging". Almost nobody unticks it, though few would add it themselves.
source: Johnson, E. J., & Goldstein, D. (2003). Do defaults save lives? Science; Madrian, B. C., & Shea, D. F. (2001). Quarterly Journal of Economics.
related: loss-aversion, endowment-effect

## loss-aversion
title: Loss aversion
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: Losing ₹1,000 hurts more than winning ₹1,000 feels good. Roughly twice as much, for big enough sums.
what: In Prospect Theory, losses loom larger than equivalent gains. It explains why people refuse fair bets, hold onto losing shares, and respond to "don't lose" offers more than "gain" offers. Recent research suggests the effect is real for meaningful amounts but can vanish for small, everyday sums.
study: Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky introduced loss aversion in their 1979 Prospect Theory paper. A typical demonstration: most people refuse a fair coin toss that would win ₹1,500 or lose ₹1,000. In 2018 David Gal and Derek Rucker argued the evidence was weaker than assumed. A 2020 replication across 19 countries largely reproduced Prospect Theory's core results, and a 2024 meta-analysis estimated losses weigh roughly twice as much as gains on average.
case: In 2010-11 Roland Fryer, Steven Levitt, John List and Sally Sadoff ran an experiment with about 150 teachers in Chicago Heights, Illinois. Some were offered a traditional bonus, paid at the end of the year if their students' test scores improved. Others got the same bonus framed as a loss: they were paid a lump sum at the start of the year and agreed to return part of it if their students didn't improve enough. Both groups could end up with the same money for the same results. Students of the "loss" teachers gained between 0.2 and 0.4 standard deviations in maths, a large effect for an education programme; the traditional bonus produced no significant improvement. The nuance: the teachers understood exactly how the scheme worked, so this wasn't a trick of attention. Money already in hand felt like theirs, and the prospect of giving it back motivated more than the prospect of earning it. Whether such schemes are fair to teachers is a separate, live debate.
proof: Supported on average, though debate continues about when and how strongly it applies.
spot: Notice "Don't miss out!" and "You'll lose your discount" offers, or holding onto a falling investment to avoid "booking a loss".
use: Frame one request today as avoiding a loss ("don't lose the early-bird slot") and notice whether it works better.
guard: For a decision about something you own, ask: if I didn't own this, would I buy it at today's price?
predict: On average, how much more do losses weigh than equal gains, according to a 2024 meta-analysis?
- About the same
* About twice as much
- About five times as much
scenario: Siddharth won't sell shares that have fallen 40%, because selling would make the loss "real", even though he'd never buy them today.
source: Kahneman, D., & Tversky, A. (1979). Prospect theory. Econometrica; Ruggeri, K., et al. (2020). Nature Human Behaviour; Brown, A. L., et al. (2024). Journal of Economic Literature.
related: endowment-effect, framing-effect, sunk-cost-fallacy, scarcity, negativity-bias, default-effect

## framing-effect
title: The framing effect
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: "90% survival" and "10% mortality" are the same fact. Doctors and patients choose differently depending on which they hear.
what: The same information, described as a gain or as a loss, leads to different choices. Framed as gains, we prefer certainty; framed as losses, we take risks. Advertisers, politicians and doctors all choose frames, and so do you.
study: In 1981 Tversky and Kahneman described a disease expected to kill 600 people. Framed as lives saved, 72% chose a programme that would surely save 200 over a gamble. Framed as deaths, 78% chose the gamble over a programme where 400 would surely die. The outcomes were identical. In 1982 a study found that surgery was more popular when described by survival rates than by mortality rates, even among doctors. The 2014 Many Labs project replicated the framing effect.
case: In 1988 Irwin Levin and Gary Gaeth gave students samples of ground beef labelled either "75% lean" or "25% fat", which describe exactly the same meat. Before tasting, the "75% lean" beef was rated as better quality, better-tasting and less greasy. Then the meat was cooked and people ate it. The framing effect shrank after tasting but didn't disappear: those who ate the "75% lean" burger still rated it leaner, less greasy and better in quality than those who ate the identical "25% fat" burger. The nuance: experience corrects a frame only partly. The label set an expectation, and the expectation shaped what people perceived in their own mouths. It is why "95% fat-free" outsells "5% fat", and why the same operation sounds different when described as "90% survive" or "10% die".
proof: Highly robust across many replications, including large multi-lab projects.
spot: Notice "95% fat-free" versus "5% fat", or "save ₹500" versus "don't lose ₹500".
use: Describe one choice to yourself in both frames today ("70% chance this works" and "30% chance it fails") before deciding.
guard: For important decisions, rephrase the numbers in the opposite frame and see if your choice changes. If it does, think again.
predict: When outcomes were framed as deaths rather than lives saved, what share chose the risky option?
- About 22%
- About 50%
* About 78%
scenario: Two shops sell the same ghee. One says "98% pure", the other "contains 2% impurities". Most shoppers pick the first.
source: Tversky, A., & Kahneman, D. (1981). The framing of decisions and the psychology of choice. Science; McNeil, B. J., et al. (1982). New England Journal of Medicine.
related: loss-aversion, anchoring

## sunk-cost-fallacy
title: The sunk cost fallacy
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: You've paid for the ticket, so you go to the boring film. The money is gone either way.
what: Money, time or effort already spent can't be recovered, so it shouldn't affect what you do next. Yet we keep going with bad projects, relationships and films because we don't want to "waste" what we've already put in.
study: In 1985 Hal Arkes and Catherine Blumer sold season theatre tickets at full price or at random discounts. In the first half of the season, people who paid full price attended more plays than those who got discounts. The 2014 Many Labs project found a smaller but consistent sunk cost effect in a hypothetical scenario.
case: In 1995 Barry Staw and Ha Hoang analysed players in the NBA, the US professional basketball league. A high draft pick is a costly commitment: top picks get bigger contracts, and executives publicly stake their judgement on them. After controlling for players' actual on-court performance, injuries and position, players drafted higher still got more playing time and were kept longer before being traded or cut. Teams kept investing in the players they had paid most to acquire, beyond what their performance justified. The nuance: a 1999 reanalysis by Colin Camerer and Roberto Weber found the effect smaller than first reported, though still present, and teams may have had other motives, such as hoping a talented player would develop or protecting executives' reputations. Real-world sunk-cost effects are messier than lab ones, but they appear where money and pride are both on the line.
proof: Real but typically smaller than famous examples suggest; strongest when the past spending is salient.
spot: Listen for "we've come this far", "I've already paid for it" or "we can't stop now after spending so much".
use: Pick something you're continuing mainly because of what you've already put in (a course, a book, a subscription). Decide today as if you were starting fresh.
guard: Ask: "If I hadn't already invested anything, would I start this today?" If not, stopping isn't wasting, it's saving.
predict: Theatre-goers who paid full price, compared with those who got discounts, during the first half of the season...
* Attended more plays
- Attended fewer plays
- Attended about the same
scenario: Twenty minutes into a terrible buffet, Arjun keeps eating long past full because "we paid ₹1,500 each".
source: Arkes, H. R., & Blumer, C. (1985). The psychology of sunk cost. Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes; Klein, R. A., et al. (2014). Social Psychology.
related: loss-aversion, cognitive-dissonance, low-ball

## ikea-effect
title: The IKEA effect
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: We value things we built ourselves more highly, even when we did a mediocre job.
what: Putting effort into creating something makes us love it more, and value it above what others would pay. Effort is part of how we decide what's worth something. The effect only appears when we finish the job.
study: In 2012 Michael Norton, Daniel Mochon and Dan Ariely had people assemble IKEA storage boxes, fold origami or build Lego. Builders were willing to pay about 63% more for their own creations than for identical pre-assembled ones, and valued their amateur origami nearly as highly as experts' work. The effect vanished when people didn't finish building.
case: In 2010 Nikolaus Franke, Martin Schreier and Ulrike Kaiser studied mass customisation, the online tools that let customers design their own products. 186 participants designed their own scarves with such a toolkit and then placed binding bids, in real auctions, for their own design and for comparable ready-made scarves. They were willing to pay considerably more for the scarf they had designed, and studies with T-shirts and other products showed the same premium. The extra value came from a feeling of accomplishment, and it shrank when people felt they hadn't really contributed or disliked the result. The nuance: companies can sell effort back to customers at a profit, but only when the effort feels like authorship rather than a chore. The popular story that cake mixes sold better once buyers had to add an egg is often used to illustrate the idea, but it is poorly documented folklore.
proof: Replicated in later studies, including with children; the effect needs the task to be completed.
spot: Notice how proud people are of things they assembled, cooked or coded themselves, and how they rate them compared with outsiders.
use: Involve someone in finishing a small task today (helping cook dinner, editing a plan). Notice how they feel about the result.
guard: Get outside opinions on your own creations (code, business plans, writing). Your attachment inflates their value.
predict: How much more were builders willing to pay for boxes they had assembled themselves?
- About 5% more
* About 63% more
- About three times as much
scenario: Nisha's handmade bookshelf wobbles slightly, but she wouldn't swap it for a sturdier store-bought one.
source: Norton, M. I., Mochon, D., & Ariely, D. (2012). The IKEA effect. Journal of Consumer Psychology.
related: endowment-effect, sunk-cost-fallacy

## decoy-effect
title: The decoy effect
category: decisions
evidence: debated
hook: Adding a worse option nobody wants can make one of the original options suddenly look better.
what: When a third option is clearly worse than one option but not the other (the "decoy"), it makes the dominating option look more attractive. Menus and subscription plans are often designed with decoys. However, the effect is less robust than its reputation, especially with real products and non-numeric attributes.
study: In 1982 Joel Huber, John Payne and Christopher Puto showed that adding an asymmetrically dominated option shifted choice towards the option that dominated it. In 2014 Shane Frederick, Leonard Lee and Ernest Baskin found the effect often failed to appear with realistic stimuli and descriptions, especially when attributes were shown as pictures rather than numbers.
case: Lab studies of the decoy effect have struggled with realistic products, so Chunhua Wu and Koray Cosguner went to a real market. In 2020 they analysed sales data from a leading online diamond retailer, where thousands of stones differ in cut, colour, clarity and carat, and where some diamonds happen to be clearly dominated: worse on every attribute yet priced about the same as a nearby stone. Shoppers often didn't notice such pairs; the chance of spotting one was only about 11-25%. But when a dominated "decoy" was noticed, the better diamond beside it sold 1.8 to 3.2 times faster, and the researchers estimated decoys raised the retailer's gross profit by about 14%. The nuance: the decoy effect is real in the field but fragile. It needs the comparison to be easy to see; with pictures, many attributes or no side-by-side view, it often vanishes, which is why it is classed as debated.
proof: Debated: reliable in stylised, numeric choices; much less reliable with realistic products.
spot: Look for pricing tiers where one option seems designed to be ignored: a medium popcorn priced almost like the large.
use: When choosing a plan today, cross out the option that's clearly worse than another, then decide between the remaining options on their own merits.
guard: Ask why an obviously inferior option is on the menu. It may be there to steer you.
predict: What did researchers find when they tested the decoy effect with realistic products and pictures?
- It got stronger
* It often failed to appear
- It reversed completely
scenario: A small coffee costs ₹150, a large ₹250, and a medium ₹240. Almost everyone buys the large.
source: Huber, J., Payne, J. W., & Puto, C. (1982). Journal of Consumer Research; Frederick, S., Lee, L., & Baskin, E. (2014). The limits of attraction. Journal of Marketing Research.
related: contrast-principle, choice-overload

## left-digit-bias
title: Left-digit bias
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: ₹399 feels much cheaper than ₹400. Used cars lose value suddenly at each 10,000 km mark.
what: We read numbers from left to right and give the first digit too much weight. ₹999 feels like "nine hundred something", not "basically a thousand". Prices ending in 9 and odometers just under a round number exploit this.
study: In 2005 Manoj Thomas and Vicki Morwitz showed that a price difference seems bigger when the leftmost digit changes. In 2012 Nicola Lacetera, Devin Pope and Justin Sydnor analysed over 22 million used-car sales and found prices dropped sharply each time the mileage crossed a 10,000-mile threshold, even though 9,950 and 10,050 miles mean almost the same thing.
case: In 2020 Andrew Olenski, André Zimerman, Stephen Coussens and Anupam Jena studied US Medicare records of patients hospitalised with heart attacks. They compared patients admitted in the two weeks before their 80th birthday with those admitted in the two weeks after, people who were, medically, almost identical. Those aged 79 were noticeably more likely to receive coronary bypass surgery, about 7% compared with about 5% for those just past 80, a gap of roughly a quarter. The nuance: no doctor was consciously thinking "over 80 means no surgery". An age beginning with 8 simply felt older, and that feeling nudged a high-stakes decision. Whether surgery was right for either group isn't known, but the gap shows that part of the decision was being made by a single digit.
proof: Robust in lab studies and large real-world datasets.
spot: Look for prices ending in 9 or 99, discounts like "under ₹500", and how you describe prices to others.
use: For every price you see today, round it up: ₹1,499 is ₹1,500. Notice if it changes how you feel about it.
guard: When comparing prices, mentally round to the nearest round number before deciding.
predict: In over 22 million used car sales, what happened to prices at each 10,000-mile mark?
- Nothing; buyers weren't fooled
* They dropped sharply
- They rose slightly
scenario: Neha decides a phone is within budget because it's ₹29,999, when her limit was ₹30,000 including the case she'll need.
source: Thomas, M., & Morwitz, V. (2005). Journal of Consumer Research; Lacetera, N., Pope, D. G., & Sydnor, J. R. (2012). Heuristic thinking and limited attention in the car market. American Economic Review.
related: anchoring

## choice-overload
title: Choice overload
category: decisions
evidence: debated
hook: 24 jams attracted more shoppers but sold fewer jars than 6. Then a meta-analysis found the average effect was close to zero.
what: The idea is that too many choices can paralyse us or leave us less satisfied. The famous jam study made this mainstream. But across many studies, the average effect is close to zero. Overload seems to happen only in specific conditions: complex options, unclear preferences, a hard decision, and a wish to decide quickly.
study: In 2000 Sheena Iyengar and Mark Lepper set up a tasting stall with 24 or 6 jams. The larger display attracted more people (60% versus 40%), but only 3% of those people bought jam, versus 30% with the smaller display. In 2010 Benjamin Scheibehenne and colleagues found an average effect near zero across 50 experiments. A 2015 meta-analysis by Alexander Chernev and colleagues identified the conditions under which overload does appear.
case: In 2004 Sheena Iyengar, Gur Huberman and Wei Jiang analysed retirement-plan records for nearly 800,000 US employees across 647 company plans. The more investment funds a plan offered, the fewer employees joined it at all: participation fell by roughly 1.5 to 2 percentage points for every ten funds added, from about 75% in plans offering two funds to about 60% in plans offering 59. Many of those who didn't join were giving up free money, because employers matched contributions. The nuance: unlike the jam stall, this was a costly non-choice. People weren't overwhelmed in the moment so much as postponing a hard decision that never got made. Meta-analyses suggest overload depends on conditions: complex options, unclear preferences and no obvious default. A retirement menu of dozens of similar funds hits all three.
proof: Debated: real in some conditions, not a general rule.
spot: Notice when you can't decide between dozens of similar options (streaming, food delivery) and end up choosing nothing.
use: For a decision you're stuck on today, cut the options to three before comparing them.
guard: If you're overwhelmed, decide on your criteria first, then filter. Accept "good enough" when the stakes are low.
predict: Across 50 experiments, what was the average effect of having more choices?
- A large drop in buying
* Close to zero overall, though it appears in specific conditions
- A large increase in buying
scenario: After 45 minutes scrolling through streaming apps, Rhea and Varun give up and go to sleep without watching anything.
source: Iyengar, S. S., & Lepper, M. R. (2000). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Scheibehenne, B., et al. (2010). Journal of Consumer Research; Chernev, A., et al. (2015). Journal of Consumer Psychology.
related: maximizers, decoy-effect, replication-crisis

## mental-accounting
title: Mental accounting
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: Lose a ₹500 note and most people still buy the ticket. Lose the ₹500 ticket and many won't buy another.
what: We sort money into mental accounts (rent, fun, windfall, savings) and treat it differently depending on the account, even though money is interchangeable. A tax refund feels like "free money", birthday cash gets spent on treats, and a lost ticket feels like the entertainment budget is used up.
study: Richard Thaler developed mental accounting in the 1980s. In a 1981 study by Tversky and Kahneman, 88% of people who imagined losing a $10 bill on the way to the theatre would still buy a ticket. But only 46% of people who imagined losing a $10 ticket they'd already bought would buy another. The financial loss was identical.
case: When US petrol prices spiked in 2008, Justine Hastings and Jesse Shapiro studied years of purchase records from a large US retailer that sells petrol. Standard economics says a rise in petrol prices is like a small cut in income, so households should trim spending a little across everything, including the grade of petrol they buy. Instead, they switched from premium to regular petrol far more than an income loss of that size would justify. In the recession that followed, by contrast, when incomes fell but petrol was cheap again, many of the same households kept buying premium. Their "petrol money" behaved like a separate budget. The nuance: mental accounts aren't foolish; they are how most people keep spending under control. But they make us react to the price of one category rather than to our overall finances, which can leave us economising in the wrong places.
proof: Extensively documented in experiments and real spending data.
spot: Notice "it's bonus money, so it doesn't count", or spending more freely on a credit card than with cash.
use: Look at one "special" pot of money you have (a refund, a gift, cashback). Ask: would I spend it this way if it were my salary?
guard: Use mental accounts on purpose (a separate savings account works), but don't let a label talk you into spending.
predict: People who imagined losing a ticket they'd bought, versus losing cash of the same value, were...
- Just as willing to buy a ticket
* Much less willing to buy a ticket
- More willing to buy a ticket
scenario: Rohit carefully compares prices on groceries but spends his entire Diwali bonus on gadgets in a week because it "felt like extra".
source: Thaler, R. H. (1985). Marketing Science; Tversky, A., & Kahneman, D. (1981). Science.
related: framing-effect

## endowment-effect
title: The endowment effect
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: Once a mug is yours, you want about twice as much to sell it as you'd pay to buy it.
what: Owning something makes us value it more, partly because giving it up feels like a loss. It's why we overprice our old phone, our flat or our car when selling, and why "free trials" are so effective.
study: In 1990 Daniel Kahneman, Jack Knetsch and Richard Thaler gave half of a class coffee mugs and set up a market. Mug owners wanted about $7 to sell; non-owners would pay only about $3. Later work showed the size of the gap depends on how the market is run, but the effect appears in many settings.
case: In 2003 John List ran experiments at sports-card shows, real markets where people trade memorabilia every weekend. Traders were randomly given one of two collectibles of similar value and then offered the chance to swap it for the other. Inexperienced traders mostly kept whatever they had been given, the classic endowment pattern. Dealers and very active traders swapped far more often, about as often as you would expect if ownership made no difference. Later, List showed that giving people trading experience at random also reduced the effect, so it wasn't simply that unattached people become dealers. The nuance: the endowment effect is real, but not fixed. Practice at trading teaches people to see an object as something with a price rather than as part of what they have, a distinction that matters for every decision about selling, swapping or decluttering.
proof: Widely replicated, though its size depends on procedure and experience; experienced traders show less of it.
spot: Notice your asking price when selling something used compared with what you'd pay for the same item.
use: Before selling or decluttering something today, ask: "If I didn't own this, would I buy it at this price?"
guard: Beware of free trials and "try it at home" offers; once it's yours, returning it feels like a loss.
predict: Owners of mugs wanted about $7 to sell them. How much were non-owners willing to pay?
* About $3
- About $6
- About $10
scenario: Kishore lists his three-year-old scooter for far more than similar ones on the site, because "it's in great condition, you can tell I looked after it".
source: Kahneman, D., Knetsch, J. L., & Thaler, R. H. (1990). Experimental tests of the endowment effect and the Coase theorem. Journal of Political Economy.
related: loss-aversion, ikea-effect, default-effect

## decision-fatigue
title: The hungry judge effect
aka: Decision fatigue
category: decisions
evidence: busted
hook: Judges were said to grant parole far less often just before lunch. The explanation turned out to be how cases were scheduled.
what: A 2011 study famously suggested that Israeli parole judges went from granting about 65% of requests after a break to nearly none before the next one, implying mental fatigue drives harsh decisions. The finding spread widely as proof of "decision fatigue". But critics showed the order of cases wasn't random, and the effect was too large to be plausible.
study: In 2011 Shai Danziger, Jonathan Levav and Liora Avnaim-Pesso analysed over 1,000 parole decisions. Later in 2011, Keren Weinshall-Margel and John Shapard pointed out that prisoners without lawyers, who are less likely to get parole, tended to come last before breaks. A 2016 simulation by Andreas Glöckner showed rational scheduling alone could produce the pattern. Separately, the broader idea behind it, ego depletion, has failed large replications.
case: In 2014 Jeffrey Linder and colleagues analysed nearly 22,000 visits by adults with acute respiratory infections, such as colds, sore throats and sinusitis, to US primary-care practices. Most of these infections are viral, so antibiotics usually don't help. Yet clinicians' rate of prescribing antibiotics rose over the course of each four-hour clinic session, both mornings and afternoons. The authors suggested decision fatigue: as the session wore on, writing a prescription won out over explaining why one wasn't needed. The nuance: this is a real pattern in real decisions, but its cause is disputed. Later appointments may differ in ways the data can't capture, and the "depleted willpower" explanation behind decision fatigue has fared badly in replications. The practical lesson holds either way: important judgements made late in a long run of similar decisions deserve a second look.
proof: Busted as evidence of decision fatigue: the pattern has simpler explanations, and the underlying theory hasn't replicated well.
spot: Notice "decision fatigue" given as a scientific explanation for any bad choice.
use: Instead of worrying about "using up" decisions, notice today whether your choices are affected by hunger or time pressure, which have more support.
guard: Be suspicious of effects that seem too large to be true. Big effects from tiny causes usually have a simpler explanation.
predict: What explained much of the "hungry judge" pattern?
- Low blood sugar before lunch
* Cases without lawyers were scheduled just before breaks
- Judges were in a hurry to leave
scenario: A productivity coach says he wears the same shirt every day to save willpower for important decisions, citing the hungry judges study.
source: Danziger, S., Levav, J., & Avnaim-Pesso, L. (2011). PNAS; Weinshall-Margel, K., & Shapard, J. (2011). PNAS; Glöckner, A. (2016). Judgment and Decision Making.
related: ego-depletion, replication-crisis

## present-bias
title: Present bias
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: For next week, most people choose fruit. For right now, most choose chocolate.
what: We give the present far more weight than the future. Planning for later, we choose what's good for us; when later arrives and becomes "now", we choose what feels good. That's why we start diets on Monday, not today, and why commitment devices work.
study: In 1998 Daniel Read and Barbara van Leeuwen asked office workers to choose a snack to be delivered a week later: 74% chose fruit. A week later, when the snack was handed out, people could change their minds: 70% chose chocolate. Economists like David Laibson have documented the same pattern in savings, exercise and debt.
case: In 2004 Richard Thaler and Shlomo Benartzi published Save More Tomorrow, a savings plan designed around present bias. Employees at a mid-sized US manufacturing company who said they couldn't afford to save more were invited to commit now to raising their savings rate later: starting with their next pay rise, and by a little more with each raise after that. Because the increases arrived with raises, take-home pay never fell, and anyone could opt out at any time. Most of those offered the plan joined, and nearly all stayed in through several raises. Over about 40 months, participants' average savings rate rose from 3.5% to 13.6%. The nuance: the plan didn't fight present bias; it used it. The future self is always willing to be virtuous, so Save More Tomorrow made sure the future self was the one who paid, and inertia, usually the enemy of saving, kept the plan running.
proof: Robust in labs and real-world data on saving, health and spending.
spot: Notice "I'll start tomorrow", buying a gym membership you don't use, or setting alarms you snooze.
use: Make one decision for your future self today and lock it in now, like scheduling a workout or setting up an automatic saving.
guard: Don't trust your future self's willpower; make the good choice the easy or default one in advance.
predict: When choosing a snack for a week later, 74% picked fruit. When the time came, what share picked chocolate?
- About 25%
- About 45%
* About 70%
scenario: On Sunday, Kirti plans to study every evening this week. By Tuesday evening, one more episode always seems more appealing.
source: Read, D., & van Leeuwen, B. (1998). Predicting hunger. Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes; Laibson, D. (1997). Quarterly Journal of Economics.
related: commitment-devices, hot-cold-empathy-gap, temptation-bundling

## mere-urgency
title: The mere urgency effect
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: We choose urgent tasks over important ones, even when the urgent one pays less and the deadline is fake.
what: Tasks with deadlines grab attention, even when the deadline is short and the reward small. Important tasks without deadlines get pushed back. Our inboxes and notifications thrive on this.
study: In 2018 Meng Zhu, Yang Yang and Christopher Hsee gave people a choice between tasks. People often chose a task with a short deadline over one that paid more but had a longer deadline or none, even when both were easy and doable. When they were prompted to consider outcomes first, the effect shrank.
case: In the late 1990s Leslie Perlow spent nine months inside a team of software engineers at a large US company, racing to finish a product. Engineers were interrupted constantly by colleagues with urgent-seeming questions, so the work that mattered most, the concentrated problem-solving each engineer was responsible for, got pushed into evenings and weekends. The culture rewarded heroic last-minute firefighting. Perlow then worked with the team to try "quiet time": set mornings each week when no one could interrupt anyone. Most engineers reported above-average productivity during quiet time, and the team launched its product on schedule, a rare event in that division. But after the study ended, quiet time faded and the old habits returned. The nuance: the pull of the urgent isn't just individual weakness. It is built into how groups work, and it often takes shared rules, not personal resolve, to protect important work.
proof: Supported by several experiments in the original study; a relatively new finding.
spot: Notice how often you answer messages, emails and "quick requests" while bigger, more important work waits.
use: Before starting work today, write down your single most important task, and do it before you open messages.
guard: For each task, ask "what does this get me?" before "how soon is it due?"
predict: Given a choice between an urgent, lower-paying task and a non-urgent, higher-paying one, people often...
* Chose the urgent, lower-paying task
- Chose the higher-paying task
- Refused to choose
scenario: Prakash spends his whole morning clearing small "urgent" emails and never starts the proposal that could win the company's biggest client.
source: Zhu, M., Yang, Y., & Hsee, C. K. (2018). The mere urgency effect. Journal of Consumer Research.
related: present-bias, scarcity

## maximizers
title: Maximisers and satisficers
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: People who insist on the best possible choice get better results and feel worse about them.
what: Maximisers look for the best option; satisficers pick the first one that meets their standards. Maximising can bring better outcomes, but it takes more effort and leads to more regret and comparison. For many everyday choices, "good enough" leaves you happier.
study: In 2002 Barry Schwartz and colleagues developed a scale to measure maximising and found maximisers reported less happiness and more regret. In 2006 Sheena Iyengar, Rachael Wells and Schwartz followed graduating students on the job hunt: maximisers got jobs paying about 20% more, but felt worse about the process and their outcome.
case: In 2002 Daniel Gilbert and Jane Ebert ran a study in a university photography course. Students shot and printed two personally meaningful photos, then had to give one away and keep the other. Some were told their choice was final; others were told they could swap prints within a few days. The students who could change their minds ended up liking the photo they kept less than those whose choice was final. Yet when asked in advance, most people said they would prefer the option to change. The nuance: this is the maximiser's trap in miniature. Keeping options open feels like protection against a bad choice, but it blocks the quiet mental work, such as noticing the good points of what you chose, that makes people satisfied with almost any reasonable choice. Committing is part of what makes "good enough" feel good.
proof: Supported by several studies, though how best to measure maximising is debated.
spot: Notice who reads 200 reviews before buying a kettle, and how they feel about the kettle afterwards.
use: For one low-stakes choice today (lunch, a gift, a phone case), decide your criteria, pick the first option that meets them, and don't look back.
guard: Save maximising for decisions that really matter and are hard to reverse.
predict: Graduates who were maximisers got jobs paying about 20% more. How did they feel?
- Happier with their jobs
* Worse about their jobs and the process
- The same as satisficers
scenario: After comparing 40 hotels for a weekend trip, Sunaina finally books one, then spends the trip wondering whether another would have been better.
source: Schwartz, B., et al. (2002). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Iyengar, S. S., Wells, R. E., & Schwartz, B. (2006). Psychological Science.
related: choice-overload

## experiential-purchases
title: Experiences over things
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: Money spent on experiences tends to bring more lasting happiness than money spent on possessions.
what: Experiences (trips, concerts, classes, meals with friends) become part of our story and are often shared with others. Possessions fade into the background. We also compare experiences less and enjoy anticipating them more. There are exceptions: for people on tight budgets, practical items can matter more.
study: In 2003 Leaf Van Boven and Thomas Gilovich asked people to think about recent experiential and material purchases. Experiential purchases made people happier, both when they thought about them and in retrospect. In 2014 Amit Kumar, Matthew Killingsworth and Gilovich found that waiting for experiences is more enjoyable than waiting for possessions.
case: Two studies sharpened the "experiences beat things" rule. In 2013 Peter Caprariello and Harry Reis found that much of the advantage of experiences came from sharing them: experiences shared with others made people happier than possessions, but solitary experiences, like a film watched alone, were no better than material purchases. Then in 2018 Jacqueline Lee, Deborah Hall and Wendy Wood studied people across income levels. People with more money got more happiness from experiences, but people with less money were as happy, or happier, with material purchases, which last, have practical uses and can be resold. The nuance: the experiential advantage is partly a luxury of having enough. For someone stretching every rupee, a durable thing that solves a daily problem can be the happier buy; for someone with a cushion, a shared experience is usually a better bet than more stuff.
proof: Supported by many studies, with nuances by income and type of purchase.
spot: Notice what people talk about years later: the trip or the TV?
use: Plan one small experience this week, even a cheap one (a new café, a walk somewhere new, a class), and look forward to it.
guard: "Experiences" can also be status symbols. The best ones are shared and fit your own interests.
predict: Compared with waiting for a material purchase, waiting for an experience tends to be...
* More enjoyable
- More stressful
- About the same
scenario: Swati still talks about the road trip to Spiti with her college friends ten years ago, but can't remember what phone she had then.
source: Van Boven, L., & Gilovich, T. (2003). To do or to have? Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Kumar, A., Killingsworth, M. A., & Gilovich, T. (2014). Psychological Science.
related: prosocial-spending, buying-time, hedonic-adaptation

## buying-time
title: Buying time
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: Spending money to save time, like paying for a chore you hate, is linked to greater life satisfaction.
what: Time-saving purchases (outsourcing disliked chores, a shorter commute, delivery) can reduce time pressure and leave room for things that matter. People who do this report more life satisfaction, but surprisingly few think to spend money this way, even when they can afford it.
study: In 2017 Ashley Whillans and colleagues surveyed over 6,000 people in four countries and found those who spent money on time-saving services reported greater life satisfaction, regardless of income. In a field experiment, working adults were happier on a day they spent $40 on something that saved them time than on a day they spent it on a material purchase.
case: Ashley Whillans and colleagues tested buying time with 1,070 working mothers in Kibera, an informal settlement in Nairobi, Kenya, where housework takes many hours a week. Over six weeks, one group received vouchers for time-saving services like laundry and meal preparation, a second received the same value in cash, and a control group received neither but took part in the study. The researchers had predicted, in a pre-registered plan, that the vouchers would help most. Instead, all three groups improved by similar amounts in well-being, stress and relationship conflict. The nuance: buying time isn't a universal law. Earlier studies in wealthier countries found time-saving purchases beat material ones, but where money is tight, cash may buy relief just as well, and the attention of being part of a study may itself have helped. Whether time or money is the scarcer resource depends on the life you are living.
proof: Supported by a large survey and a field experiment; more research is still accumulating.
spot: Notice chores you hate that you could pay a little to avoid, and how you justify not doing it ("it's a waste of money").
use: Identify one disliked task you could outsource or skip this week for a small cost. Do it and notice how you use the time.
guard: Make sure the time saved goes to something meaningful, not just more work or scrolling.
predict: Working adults spent $40 to save time on one day and $40 on a material purchase on another. When were they happier?
* On the time-saving day
- On the material purchase day
- No difference
scenario: Instead of spending every Saturday morning ironing, Sapna pays the local istriwala, and uses the time to go for a swim with her daughter.
source: Whillans, A. V., et al. (2017). Buying time promotes happiness. PNAS.
related: experiential-purchases, prosocial-spending

## formulas-beat-judgement
title: Simple formulas beat expert judgement
aka: Clinical versus statistical prediction
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: In 136 studies pitting experts against simple formulas, the experts clearly won in only a handful.
what: When the task is predicting an outcome from a set of information, such as who will reoffend, which patient will deteriorate or which applicant will succeed, a simple rule that combines a few factors consistently matches or beats experienced professionals. Humans are inconsistent: the same expert given the same case twice often decides differently, and is swayed by mood, fatigue and irrelevant details. A formula is never in a bad mood.
study: In 1954 psychologist Paul Meehl reviewed about 20 studies and found that statistical predictions equalled or beat clinicians' judgements in almost all of them. In 2000 William Grove and colleagues combined 136 studies in medicine, psychology, education and finance. Formulas were substantially more accurate in about a third to half of the comparisons and about as accurate in most of the rest; clinicians were substantially more accurate in only about 6% to 16%.
case: Every year US judges decide whether people awaiting trial are released or held in jail, a decision that hinges on predicting what each defendant would do if released. In 2018 Jon Kleinberg, Sendhil Mullainathan and colleagues trained an algorithm on hundreds of thousands of New York City cases. Simulations suggested that following its risk predictions could cut crime by released defendants by up to 24.7% while jailing the same number of people, or jail up to 41.9% fewer people with no rise in crime, while also reducing racial disparities. Part of the judges' error came from releasing some of the highest-risk defendants. The nuance: formulas inherit biases in the data they're built on and miss rare factors a person might catch. The strongest approach is usually a formula as the default, with humans recording their reasons when they override it.
proof: Solid: the clinical-versus-statistical comparison is one of the most consistently replicated results in applied psychology, across more than half a century and many fields.
spot: Notice decisions made "on feel" that could be scored instead: job interviews, loan approvals, choosing which supplier or student to back.
use: For one recurring choice (a restaurant, a hire, a purchase), write down three factors that matter and score each option on them before going with your gut.
guard: Decide your scoring rules before you meet the candidate or see the case, then let intuition add to the score, not replace it.
predict: Across 136 studies comparing clinicians with statistical formulas, in what share were clinicians substantially more accurate?
- In most studies; experience beats formulas
- In about half
* In only about 6% to 16%
scenario: For years Harsh interviewed job candidates by chatting and going with his gut. When his firm starts scoring every candidate on the same five questions, the people it hires stay longer and perform better.
source: Meehl, P. E. (1954). Clinical versus statistical prediction. University of Minnesota Press; Grove, W. M., et al. (2000). Clinical versus mechanical prediction: A meta-analysis. Psychological Assessment; Kleinberg, J., et al. (2018). Human decisions and machine predictions. Quarterly Journal of Economics.
related: trusting-intuition, overconfidence, wisdom-of-crowds

## denominator-neglect
title: Denominator neglect
aka: The ratio bias
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: Offered a bowl with 1 winning bean in 10 or one with 7 in 100, many people pick the big bowl. It has more winners, and worse odds.
what: We feel a probability through its numerator: the number of winners, deaths or cases. "1,286 out of 10,000" sounds more dangerous than "24.14 out of 100", though it's about half the risk. In the same way, "the risk has doubled" sounds alarming whether it goes from 1 in 10 to 2 in 10, or from 1 in 7,000 to 2 in 7,000. The meaning is in the denominator, the "out of how many", and that's the part we skip.
study: In 1994 Veronika Denes-Raj and Seymour Epstein offered people money for drawing a red jelly bean, choosing from a small bowl with 1 red bean in 10 or a large bowl with, for example, 7 red beans in 100. Many chose the large bowl, with worse odds, some saying they knew it was irrational but felt they had more chances. In 1997 Kimihiko Yamagishi found that a cause of death described as killing 1,286 out of 10,000 people was rated riskier than one killing 24.14 out of 100.
case: In October 1995 Britain's Committee on Safety of Medicines warned that newer contraceptive pills doubled the risk of dangerous blood clots compared with older pills. "Twice the risk" was true, but the absolute risk was small, roughly from 1 in 7,000 women a year to 2 in 7,000. Many women stopped taking the pill. Use of the newer pills fell sharply, and in the first quarter of 1996 there were about 6,200 more abortions in England and Wales than in the previous quarter, a 16% rise. Pregnancy itself carries a higher clot risk than either pill. The nuance: relative risks aren't lies, but without the denominator they can't guide a decision. Ask for absolute numbers: how many out of how many, with and without.
proof: Solid: the ratio bias and denominator neglect have been replicated many times, and the harm of reporting only relative risks is well documented in medicine.
spot: Look for "doubles the risk", "50% more likely" or big raw counts with no denominator in health news, ads and safety warnings.
use: Find one statistic today that gives only a relative risk or a raw count, and work out or look up the absolute number: how many out of how many.
guard: Before reacting to a risk, convert it to "X out of 1,000 people like me" for each option, including the option of doing nothing.
predict: Which cause of death did people rate as riskier?
* One that kills 1,286 out of every 10,000 people
- One that kills 24.14 out of every 100 people
- They rated both the same
scenario: Hearing that a medicine "doubles the risk" of a side effect, Leela stops taking it at once. Her doctor shows her that it means going from 1 in 50,000 to 2 in 50,000.
source: Denes-Raj, V., & Epstein, S. (1994). Conflict between intuitive and rational processing. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Yamagishi, K. (1997). When a 12.86% mortality is more dangerous than 24.14%. Applied Cognitive Psychology; Furedi, A. (1999). The public health implications of the 1995 "pill scare". Human Reproduction Update.
related: framing-effect, base-rate-neglect, law-of-small-numbers

## certainty-effect
title: The certainty and possibility effects
aka: The fourfold pattern
category: decisions
evidence: solid
hook: Going from 99% to 100% safe feels worth far more than going from 50% to 51%. We give the edges of probability extra weight.
what: We don't weigh probabilities in proportion. A change that removes a risk completely (certainty) or creates a small chance where there was none (possibility) feels much bigger than the same change in the middle. That's why people buy lottery tickets and extended warranties, pay heavily to remove small risks, and gamble to avoid a sure loss. Kahneman and Tversky summarised the pattern in prospect theory's "fourfold pattern".
study: In 1979 Kahneman and Tversky showed that people's choices between gambles depart from expected value in systematic ways: most prefer a sure gain to a slightly better gamble, prefer a gamble to a sure loss, and overweight small chances. In 2020 Kai Ruggeri and colleagues re-ran the original problems with 4,098 people in 19 countries and 13 languages: 94% of the items replicated, with somewhat smaller effects.
case: In 2010 Justin Sydnor studied the choices of customers of a large US home insurer who could pick a deductible, the amount they pay themselves on a claim, of $1,000 or $500, among others. Customers who chose $500 paid on average about $100 a year more, though they filed claims less than 5% of the time, so the extra cover was worth under $25 a year on average. Standard economic theory can justify such choices only with absurd levels of risk aversion; overweighting the small chance of a claim, and wanting the risk gone, fits better. The nuance: paying for certainty isn't always foolish. Insure losses you couldn't afford. The overpayment happens on small, survivable risks, where a standing policy of taking the higher deductible saves money over a lifetime.
proof: Solid: the core patterns of prospect theory replicated in a 19-country study, and the overweighting of small probabilities shows up in insurance, lottery and betting data.
spot: Notice offers that sell certainty or a tiny chance: extended warranties, "100% guaranteed", lottery jackpots, "even a 1% risk is too much".
use: The next time you're offered a warranty or insurance on something cheap, estimate how likely you are to claim and how much you'd get, and compare that with the price.
guard: Set a risk policy in advance, for example: always take the higher deductible, and skip warranties on things you could afford to replace.
predict: In the 2020 replication of prospect theory across 19 countries, what share of the original items replicated?
- About a third
- About half
* About 94%
scenario: Rajiv pays ₹900 for a two-year warranty on a ₹4,000 kettle he could easily replace, and the same week buys lottery tickets because "someone has to win".
source: Kahneman, D., & Tversky, A. (1979). Prospect theory: An analysis of decision under risk. Econometrica; Ruggeri, K., et al. (2020). Replicating patterns of prospect theory for decision under risk. Nature Human Behaviour; Sydnor, J. (2010). (Over)insuring modest risks. American Economic Journal: Applied Economics.
related: loss-aversion, framing-effect, denominator-neglect

## narrow-framing
title: Narrow framing
aka: Myopic loss aversion
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: Check your investments daily and you'll often see losses. Check yearly and you'll mostly see gains. The frame decides how brave you are.
what: Because losses hurt more than equal gains please, judging each risk on its own, or checking outcomes very often, makes sensible risks feel unbearable. One at a time, small bets with good odds feel scary; seen as a bundle, they're clearly worth taking. Kahneman's advice is to "think like a trader": look at the whole portfolio of decisions rather than each one.
study: In 1997 Uri Gneezy and Jan Potters had people repeatedly decide how much to bet on a favourable gamble. Those who got feedback after every round bet less than those who got feedback, and chose, only every three rounds. In 1999 Shlomo Benartzi and Richard Thaler showed university staff the returns of a stock fund and a bond fund. Shown one-year returns, they put about 40% into stocks; shown the same data as 30-year outcomes, about 80%.
case: Professionals show it too. In 2005 Michael Haigh and John List ran the Gneezy and Potters game with traders from the Chicago Board of Trade and with students. The traders showed the pattern even more strongly: with frequent feedback, they bet much less. In 2016 Francis Larson, John List and Robert Metcalfe found the same in a field experiment with professional traders: those who saw prices less often invested about a third more in risky assets. Some replications have failed, though: when people face varied gambles rather than the same one repeatedly, or see the full range of possible outcomes, the effect shrinks. The nuance: the cure isn't to stop looking but to look through the right frame. Decide a strategy for many decisions at once, and check results on a schedule that matches how long you'll hold them.
proof: Good: replicated in the lab and the field, including with professional traders, but sensitive to how the task is set up; some replications with varied gambles find little effect.
spot: Notice when you judge one bet, one day of investing or one job interview in isolation and feel the loss sharply, instead of seeing it as one of many.
use: Choose one thing you check too often, such as a portfolio, a fitness tracker or sales numbers, and set a slower checking schedule for the next week.
guard: For repeated decisions, set a policy ("I take bets with these odds", "I check investments quarterly") and judge the policy over many outcomes, not each result.
predict: University staff saw the same stock and bond returns as one-year or as 30-year figures. How much did they put into stocks?
- About the same both ways, around 60%
* About 40% after one-year figures, about 80% after 30-year ones
- More after one-year figures, which looked exciting
scenario: Meghna checks her mutual fund app every morning, panics at each red day and finally sells everything in a dip. Her colleague, who looks once a year, has never sold and is well ahead.
source: Benartzi, S., & Thaler, R. H. (1995). Myopic loss aversion and the equity premium puzzle. Quarterly Journal of Economics; Benartzi, S., & Thaler, R. H. (1999). Risk aversion or myopia? Choices in repeated gambles and retirement investments. Management Science; Gneezy, U., & Potters, J. (1997). An experiment on risk taking and evaluation periods. Quarterly Journal of Economics; Haigh, M. S., & List, J. A. (2005). Do professional traders exhibit myopic loss aversion? Journal of Finance; Larson, F., List, J. A., & Metcalfe, R. D. (2016). Can myopic loss aversion explain the equity premium puzzle? NBER Working Paper.
related: loss-aversion, mental-accounting, certainty-effect

## less-is-better
title: Less is better
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: A set of 24 perfect dishes was valued at $33. The same 24 plus extra cups and saucers, some of them broken, was valued at $23.
what: When we judge one option on its own, we often go by its average quality rather than its total value, because totals are hard to judge without a comparison. So adding something good but less impressive can lower the overall impression: a cheap free gift with a luxury item, a mediocre extra reason in a strong argument. Put options side by side and the effect usually reverses, because then the bigger quantity is easy to see.
study: In 1998 Christopher Hsee asked people what they'd pay for a dinnerware set. One set had 24 pieces, all intact; the other had 40 pieces, including the same 24 plus cups and saucers, several of them broken. Judged separately, the smaller set was valued at about $33 and the larger at about $23. Judged side by side, people valued the larger set slightly more, $32 against $30. A close replication published in 2023 mostly confirmed the results.
case: In 2002 economist John List tested the effect in a real market, a sports-card show. List auctioned bundles of baseball cards: 10 cards in mint condition, or the same 10 plus 3 more in poor condition, which were worth more in total. Ordinary collectors bidding on one bundle alone bid more for the 10 cards than for the 13; when both bundles were offered together, they bid more for the 13. Experienced professional dealers didn't reliably show the reversal. The nuance: averaging is a shortcut for judging what's hard to evaluate. Experts who know what things are worth are less fooled, and any of us can avoid it by comparing options side by side.
proof: Good: the less-is-better pattern has replicated, including in a close replication, and appears in real markets, though it shrinks with expertise and side-by-side comparison.
spot: Notice bundles padded with filler (free gifts, bonus items, a fourth weak reason) and whether they make the main thing seem cheaper.
use: In something you write or present today, cut your weakest point or item, and see whether what's left is stronger.
guard: When you're valuing an option on its own, ask what a comparison would show, and look at the total, not just the average quality.
predict: Judged separately, which dinnerware set did people value more?
* The smaller set of 24 intact pieces
- The larger set of 40 pieces with a few broken
- Both about the same
scenario: Ankit's proposal had three strong reasons, and he added two weak ones to look thorough. The client remembers only the weak ones and calls the whole plan flimsy.
source: Hsee, C. K. (1998). Less is better: When low-value options are valued more highly than high-value options. Journal of Behavioral Decision Making; List, J. A. (2002). Preference reversals of a different kind: The "more is less" phenomenon. American Economic Review; Weaver, K., Garcia, S. M., & Schwarz, N. (2012). The presenter's paradox. Journal of Consumer Research; Vonasch, A. J., et al. (2023). "Less is better" in separate evaluations versus "more is better" in joint evaluations: Mostly successful close replication and extension of Hsee (1998). Collabra: Psychology.
related: decoy-effect, contrast-principle, framing-effect

## opportunity-cost-neglect
title: Opportunity cost neglect
category: decisions
evidence: good
hook: Remind shoppers that not buying a $14.99 DVD means keeping $14.99 for something else, and fewer buy it. Obvious, yet it works.
what: Every choice to spend money or time is also a choice not to spend it on something else, but we rarely picture the something else. Spending is framed as "buy or not", and "not" feels like nothing. Simply naming another use for the money makes people more careful. The effect is real, but smaller than first reported.
study: In 2009 Shane Frederick and colleagues offered people a DVD for $14.99. When the choices were "buy" or "not buy", 75% chose to buy; when "not buy" was described as "keep the $14.99 for other purchases", 55% did. In another study, reminding people of other uses for their money cut willingness to buy from 73% to 37%. In 2023 Allegra Maguire, Emil Persson and Gustav Tinghög combined 39 experiments with 14,005 people and found a reliable effect, but considerably smaller than in the original studies, which looked like outliers.
case: Opportunity costs are just as easy to ignore with public money. In 2020 Emil Persson and Gustav Tinghög asked members of the public and experts in healthcare priority-setting whether to fund a new health programme, reminding some that the money could fund other programmes instead. In one study the reminder reduced support for the new programme; in a similar study it made no difference. The nuance: the reminder works best when the alternative is concrete. "Other purchases" is vague; "two weeks of groceries" or "a nurse's salary for a year" makes the trade-off real, and that is the version worth using on yourself.
proof: Good: a meta-analysis of 39 experiments finds a robust but modest effect, much smaller than the first demonstrations suggested.
spot: Notice purchases framed as "buy it or not", and yeses at work that never mention what will be dropped to make room.
use: Before one purchase today, name a specific thing the same money could buy instead, and choose between those two.
guard: For any big spend of money or time, write the best alternative use next to it. If you can't name one, you haven't really decided.
predict: When "not buy" was reworded as "keep the $14.99 for other purchases", what happened to sales of the DVD?
- Nothing; it means the same thing
* They fell, from 75% to 55%
- They rose, because people felt richer
scenario: Raghav signs up for a ₹1,200-a-month streaming bundle without a thought. Only when his sister asks whether he'd rather spend the same money on his weekend football does he cancel half of it.
source: Frederick, S., Novemsky, N., Wang, J., Dhar, R., & Nowlis, S. (2009). Opportunity cost neglect. Journal of Consumer Research; Maguire, A., Persson, E., & Tinghög, G. (2023). Opportunity cost neglect: A meta-analysis. Journal of the Economic Science Association; Persson, E., & Tinghög, G. (2020). Opportunity cost neglect in public policy. Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization.
related: mental-accounting, framing-effect, sunk-cost-fallacy

# =====================================================================================
# MOTIVATION & HABITS
# =====================================================================================

## implementation-intentions
title: If-then plans
aka: Implementation intentions
category: habits
evidence: solid
hook: "I'll exercise more" rarely works. "When I get home from work, I'll put on my shoes and walk for 10 minutes" does.
what: Goals say what you want; if-then plans say exactly when, where and how you'll act: "If situation X happens, I'll do Y." Linking the action to a cue hands control to the environment, so you don't need to remember or decide in the moment. That's why this app asks when you'll try today's mission.
study: In 2006 Peter Gollwitzer and Paschal Sheeran analysed 94 studies with over 8,000 participants and found that forming if-then plans had a medium-to-large effect on reaching goals (d = 0.65). In 2011 Katherine Milkman and colleagues found that prompting employees to write down the date and time they'd get a flu shot raised vaccination rates by about 4 percentage points.
case: Before the 2008 Pennsylvania presidential primary, David Nickerson and Todd Rogers tested if-then planning on a large scale. Volunteers phoned registered voters who had been randomly assigned to different scripts. One script simply encouraged people to vote. Another, the planning script, asked three questions: around what time will you vote, where will you be coming from, and what will you be doing just beforehand? Among households with only one eligible voter, the planning call raised turnout by about 4.1 percentage points compared with no call, while the plain encouragement did much less. In households with several voters, the planning call had no clear effect, perhaps because people there already coordinate their plans with each other. The nuance: the plan works by tying an intention to a specific moment and cue, which matters most for people with no one else around to provide that cue.
proof: Strongly supported by meta-analysis across health, study and work goals.
spot: Notice the difference between vague resolutions ("eat healthier") and specific plans ("after lunch, I'll eat fruit instead of biscuits").
use: Write today's mission as an if-then plan: "When [situation], I will [action]." Use the plan box below the mission.
guard: Plans need a reliable cue. "When I have time" isn't a cue; "after I brush my teeth" is.
predict: Across 94 studies, what effect did if-then plans have on reaching goals?
- Almost none
* A medium-to-large effect
- They only helped for health goals
scenario: Instead of resolving to "read more", Divya decides: "When I get into bed, I'll read two pages before I touch my phone."
source: Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation intentions and goal achievement. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology; Milkman, K. L., et al. (2011). PNAS.
related: habit-formation, mental-contrasting, commitment-devices

## ego-depletion
title: Ego depletion
aka: Willpower as a battery
category: habits
evidence: busted
hook: Willpower was said to run out like a battery. Large replications across dozens of labs found almost nothing.
what: The idea was that self-control draws on a limited resource: resist the cookies and you'll give up sooner on the next task. Hundreds of studies seemed to show it. Then large, preregistered replications found effects near zero. People do get tired, and beliefs about willpower may matter, but the "battery" model has not held up.
study: In 1998 Roy Baumeister and colleagues reported that people who resisted chocolate chip cookies (eating radishes instead) gave up sooner on unsolvable puzzles. In 2016 a registered replication across 23 labs with 2,141 participants found an effect close to zero. In 2021 a 36-lab study with 3,531 participants, led by Kathleen Vohs, a proponent of the theory, also found no reliable effect.
case: In 2015 Veronika Job, Gregory Walton, Katharina Bernecker and Carol Dweck followed university students through demanding periods of the term, including final exams. At the start, they measured whether each student believed willpower was a limited resource that gets used up, or something that isn't easily depleted. Among students facing heavy demands, those who believed willpower was limited procrastinated more, ate more junk food and spent more impulsively as the pressure built; among those with heavy course loads, they also got lower grades, partly because of the extra procrastination. The nuance: this was a correlational study, so beliefs may partly reflect other differences between students. But given that ego depletion itself has failed large replications, the more useful lesson may be that believing you have run out is what makes you stop. Tiredness is real; the story you tell yourself about it seems to shape what you do next.
proof: Busted: two large, preregistered multi-lab tests found no meaningful effect.
spot: Listen for "I used up all my willpower today" as a reason to give in.
use: Next time you feel "out of willpower" today, try a short break, a snack or a change of scene, and see whether your resolve really was gone.
guard: Design your environment rather than relying on willpower at all: remove temptations, set defaults, use if-then plans.
predict: A 36-lab study led by one of ego depletion's proponents tested the effect with 3,531 people. What did it find?
- A large effect
- A medium effect, smaller than claimed
* No reliable effect
scenario: After a long day of saying no to office sweets, Tarun tells himself he's "used up his willpower" and orders a large pizza.
source: Baumeister, R. F., et al. (1998). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Hagger, M. S., et al. (2016). Perspectives on Psychological Science; Vohs, K. D., et al. (2021). Psychological Science.
related: decision-fatigue, replication-crisis, marshmallow-test

## fresh-start-effect
title: The fresh start effect
category: habits
evidence: good
