hook: Mondays, birthdays and new months make us more likely to start new goals. Use them on purpose.
what: Temporal landmarks, like the start of a week, month or year, a birthday or a holiday, create a sense of a new chapter. They separate us from past failures and make us feel we can be different now. People start more goals at these points.
study: In 2014 Hengchen Dai, Katherine Milkman and Jason Riis found that Google searches for "diet", gym visits and commitments to goals all rose at the start of weeks, months and years, and after birthdays and holidays.
case: In a field experiment published in 2021, John Beshears, Hengchen Dai, Katherine Milkman and Shlomo Benartzi mailed 6,082 university employees an offer to increase their retirement savings, either now or at a future date. For some, the future date was described neutrally; for others, the same date was framed as a fresh start, such as "after your next birthday" or "the first day of spring". The fresh-start framing made people more likely to commit to saving more in the future, without reducing how many increased their savings immediately, and it raised savings contributions over the next eight months by roughly 25% compared with the other mailings. The nuance: nothing about the date changed, only its label. Describing an ordinary day as a new beginning was enough to make people feel separated from their past habits, and so more willing to act.
proof: Supported by large real-world datasets and later experiments.
spot: Notice the full gym in early January, or how people plan to "start fresh on Monday".
use: Pick a small goal and attach it to the next landmark: tomorrow, the 1st of the month, or your next birthday. Write the date down.
guard: Fresh starts help you begin, not keep going. Pair the start with an if-then plan.
predict: When did gym visits increase in the study of temporal landmarks?
- Only in January
* At the start of weeks, months and years, and after birthdays
- On weekends
scenario: Shalini signs up for a guitar class on the morning of her 30th birthday, telling herself it's the start of a new chapter.
source: Dai, H., Milkman, K. L., & Riis, J. (2014). The fresh start effect. Management Science.
related: implementation-intentions

## habit-formation
title: How long habits take
aka: The 21-day myth
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: It's not 21 days. In one study habits took a median of 66 days, and anywhere from 18 to 254.
what: Habits form through repetition in a consistent context, until the cue triggers the behaviour automatically. The popular "21 days" figure has no research behind it. Real habits take longer, vary hugely by person and behaviour, and missing a single day doesn't reset the clock.
study: In 2010 Phillippa Lally and colleagues had 96 people pick a daily behaviour (like drinking water after breakfast) and track how automatic it felt. On average, it took 66 days to reach a plateau, with a range from 18 to 254 days. Missing a day now and then didn't significantly harm progress. The "21 days" figure traces back to a 1960 self-help book by a plastic surgeon about patients getting used to their new faces.
case: In 2023 Anastasia Buyalskaya, Colin Camerer and colleagues used machine learning on two huge behavioural records: more than 12 million gym check-ins from tens of thousands of members of a US gym chain, and over 40 million hand-hygiene events recorded by sensors for thousands of hospital workers. For each person, they measured how predictable the behaviour became and which cues predicted it. Handwashing typically became habitual within weeks; going to the gym typically took months. There was no magic number: the time varied widely between people. For gym-goers, the most useful predictors were simply how long it had been since the last visit and the day of the week. The nuance: habits form fastest for simple, frequent actions tied to a stable cue, like entering a patient's room. Complex actions that need planning and travel take longer, which makes "21 days" especially misleading for exercise.
proof: The study is widely cited and consistent with later research, though small.
spot: Notice anyone who quits a new routine after a few weeks because "it's still hard", or gives up after missing one day.
use: Pick one small daily action and tie it to something you already do (after brushing, after lunch). Expect it to take weeks, not days.
guard: Don't treat one missed day as failure. Consistency over weeks matters more than a perfect streak.
predict: In the habit-formation study, what was the median time for a new behaviour to become automatic?
- 21 days
* 66 days
- 180 days
scenario: Three weeks into his morning walks, Kunal is frustrated that it still takes effort to get out of bed, and wonders if something is wrong with him.
source: Lally, P., van Jaarsveld, C. H. M., Potts, H. W. W., & Wardle, J. (2010). How are habits formed. European Journal of Social Psychology.
related: implementation-intentions

## temptation-bundling
title: Temptation bundling
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Only allow yourself the gripping audiobook at the gym, and you'll go to the gym more. For a while.
what: Pair something you should do but put off (exercise, chores) with something you want to do but feel guilty about (a binge-worthy show, a podcast). The pleasure pulls you into the chore. It works, but the effect fades, so refresh the treat.
study: In 2014 Katherine Milkman, Julia Minson and Kevin Volpp gave gym-goers iPods loaded with page-turner audiobooks that they could only access at the gym. At first, they visited the gym 51% more often than a control group, but the effect faded over time. A 2020 follow-up with 6,792 gym members found a smaller boost that also faded after the programme ended.
case: Kaitlin Woolley and Ayelet Fishbach studied why people keep or abandon long-term goals. In a 2017 study, people described their New Year's resolutions, such as exercising more or eating better, and rated how important and how enjoyable pursuing them was. When followed up weeks later, enjoyment predicted who was still going; importance did not. In other experiments, people who chose an exercise they enjoyed, or healthy food they liked the taste of, persisted longer than those focused on long-term benefits. The nuance: this is the logic behind temptation bundling. People rarely abandon goals because they stop believing in them; they abandon them because the doing feels bad today. Making the activity itself rewarding, through a gripping audiobook, good company or a sport you actually like, tends to outlast willpower, though bundles too can fade when the novelty wears off.
proof: Supported by field experiments, but effects are smaller in larger studies and fade over time.
spot: Notice people who only listen to their favourite podcast while doing chores, or watch a show only on the treadmill.
use: Pair one task you've been avoiding with one treat today: a favourite playlist only while cleaning, a podcast only on a walk.
guard: Keep the treat locked to the task; if you can access it anywhere, the bundle breaks.
predict: In the original study, how much did gym visits increase at first when audiobooks were only available at the gym?
- About 5%
* About 51%
- About 300%
scenario: Nidhi only lets herself watch her favourite K-drama while she's ironing, and suddenly the ironing pile shrinks.
source: Milkman, K. L., Minson, J. A., & Volpp, K. G. M. (2014). Holding the Hunger Games hostage at the gym. Management Science; Kirgios, E. L., et al. (2020). Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes.
related: present-bias, variable-rewards

## goal-gradient
title: The goal gradient effect
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: The closer we get to a goal, the harder we push. Rats run faster near the food, and so do coffee-card holders.
what: Motivation increases as we approach a goal. We speed up near the end of a race, a book or a loyalty card. This means the middle of long goals is the hardest part, and breaking goals into smaller ones creates more "near the end" moments.
study: In 1932 Clark Hull observed that rats ran faster in a maze as they approached the food. In 2006 Ran Kivetz, Oleg Urminsky and Yuhuang Zheng studied a café loyalty programme and found customers bought coffee more often as they got closer to earning a free one.
case: Kiva is a website where people lend small amounts to entrepreneurs in low-income countries; each loan request shows a bar filling up as lenders chip in. In 2013 Cynthia Cryder, George Loewenstein and Howard Seltman used a web robot to track Kiva loans as they were funded. Contributions arrived faster as loans approached their targets: the closer a bar was to full, the more likely the next visitor was to lend. A field experiment with charitable funds found the same pattern, and a lab study suggested why: a contribution near the end feels as if it makes more of a difference, completing something rather than starting it. The nuance: the goal gradient isn't only about our own rewards. People are also more motivated when their effort visibly closes a gap, which is why fundraisers often wait until they have most of the money before going public.
proof: Replicated in animals, consumers and online behaviour.
spot: Notice how you speed up near the end of a task, a run or a reward card, and slow down in the middle.
use: Break a big task into small pieces today and track your progress visibly. Notice how each piece feels as you near the end.
guard: The middle is where projects die. Set intermediate milestones so there's always a finish line in sight.
predict: In a café loyalty programme, what happened as customers got closer to earning a free coffee?
* They bought coffee more often
- They bought less often, saving up for the free one
- No change
scenario: With only 2 km left in the half marathon, Ritu suddenly finds the energy to overtake three runners.
source: Hull, C. L. (1932). Psychological Review; Kivetz, R., Urminsky, O., & Zheng, Y. (2006). The goal-gradient hypothesis resurrected. Journal of Marketing Research.
related: endowed-progress

## zeigarnik-effect
title: The Zeigarnik effect (plot twist)
category: habits
evidence: busted
hook: Unfinished tasks are said to stick in memory better. A 2025 meta-analysis found they don't. But the urge to finish is real.
what: The Zeigarnik effect claims we remember interrupted tasks better than completed ones. It's in many textbooks and productivity blogs. When researchers pooled the evidence, the memory advantage disappeared. What does hold up is a related effect discovered at the same time: the Ovsiankina effect, a strong urge to go back and finish what was interrupted.
study: In 1927 Bluma Zeigarnik reported that people remembered interrupted tasks better than completed ones. Maria Ovsiankina, working in the same lab, found that people spontaneously resumed interrupted tasks. In 2025 Romain Ghibellini and Beat Meier published a meta-analysis: across decades of studies, there was no general memory advantage for unfinished tasks, but the tendency to resume them was consistently supported.
case: In 2011 E. J. Masicampo and Roy Baumeister tested what unfinished goals actually do to the mind. They had people think about an important task they hadn't finished, such as preparing for an upcoming exam. Afterwards, during an unrelated reading task, these people's minds wandered more and they understood less, and words related to their goal stayed unusually accessible. The unfinished goal kept nagging. But when participants were given a few minutes to write a specific plan for the task, saying when, where and how they would do it, the intrusions disappeared, even though none of the work had been done. The nuance: this fits the plot twist in the evidence. Unfinished tasks aren't reliably remembered better, but they do keep pulling at attention until there's a plan to resume them. Writing down the next step isn't just organisation; it lets the mind put the task down.
proof: Busted for the memory claim; the urge to resume (the Ovsiankina effect) is well supported.
spot: Notice how hard it is to leave something half-done, a puzzle, a series, a half-written email.
use: Use the real effect: stop today's work in the middle of a sentence or a step, so it pulls you back tomorrow.
guard: Unfinished tasks can also nag at you. Writing down the next step can quieten that pull until you're ready.
predict: What did a 2025 meta-analysis find about unfinished tasks?
- They're remembered much better than finished ones
* No memory advantage, but a real urge to resume them
- They're quickly forgotten and rarely resumed
scenario: Ankit deliberately stops writing his thesis mid-paragraph each night. Each morning, he's itching to finish the sentence and gets started straight away.
source: Zeigarnik, B. (1927). Psychologische Forschung; Ghibellini, R., & Meier, B. (2025). Interruption, recall and resumption. Humanities and Social Sciences Communications.
related: replication-crisis

## mental-contrasting
title: Mental contrasting
aka: WOOP
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Fantasising about success can sap your energy. Picture the goal, then the main obstacle, then your plan.
what: Positive fantasies feel good but can trick the mind into feeling it's already arrived, lowering effort. Mental contrasting pairs the wish with reality: imagine the outcome, then the obstacle inside you that's most likely to get in the way, then plan for it. Gabriele Oettingen calls it WOOP: Wish, Outcome, Obstacle, Plan.
study: In 2011 Heather Kappes and Gabriele Oettingen found that people who fantasised positively about the future showed less energy (measured by blood pressure) and achieved less than those who did not. Studies of mental contrasting combined with if-then plans have found benefits for goals like exercise, diet and study; a 2021 meta-analysis found small-to-medium effects.
case: In 2011 Angela Duckworth, Gabriele Oettingen and colleagues worked with 66 US high-school students preparing for the PSAT, a practice version of a college entrance exam, over the summer. All the students received a workbook of practice questions. Half also did a short written exercise: imagine the best outcome of finishing the practice, then the obstacles likely to get in the way, then write if-then plans for those obstacles (mental contrasting with implementation intentions, or MCII). The other half wrote about influential people or events in their lives. The MCII students completed about 60% more practice questions. The nuance: the exercise added no knowledge and no reward; it linked the wish to a specific obstacle and a ready response. The study was small, but similar results have since appeared for exercise, diet and study habits, and the method is now taught as WOOP: wish, outcome, obstacle, plan.
proof: Supported by experiments and a meta-analysis; effects are moderate.
spot: Notice vision boards and "manifesting" content that focuses only on the dream, never on obstacles.
use: Do a two-minute WOOP for one goal today: Wish, best Outcome, main inner Obstacle, and an if-then Plan for that obstacle.
guard: Optimism is good; ignoring obstacles isn't. If the main obstacle can't be overcome, mental contrasting also helps you let go.
predict: When people fantasised only positively about the future, what happened to their energy and achievement?
- Both went up
* Both went down
- Energy went up, achievement didn't change
scenario: Instead of just imagining crossing the finish line, Meera notes that her real obstacle is hitting snooze, and plans: "If I want to snooze, I'll put my feet on the floor first."
source: Kappes, H. B., & Oettingen, G. (2011). Positive fantasies about idealized futures sap energy. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology; Oettingen, G. (2014). Rethinking Positive Thinking.
related: implementation-intentions

## endowed-progress
title: The endowed progress effect
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: A loyalty card with 2 of 10 stamps already filled gets completed far more often than one needing 8 from scratch.
what: Feeling that you've already made progress, even if it was given to you, increases your motivation to finish. A head start makes the goal feel within reach, and quitting would waste the progress.
study: In 2006 Joseph Nunes and Xavier Drèze gave car-wash customers loyalty cards. One group needed 8 stamps for a free wash, starting with none. The other needed 10 stamps but got 2 free. Both needed 8 more washes, but 19% of the first group completed their cards, versus 34% of the second, who also finished faster.
case: In 2012 Minjung Koo and Ayelet Fishbach used a restaurant's loyalty programme to test why progress framing works. Cards showed customers' progress towards a free meal either by what they had already collected or by what they still needed. Customers who were early in the card came back more often when the card highlighted what they had already done; customers close to the reward came back more often when it highlighted how little was left. In both cases, what helped was attention on whichever number was smaller. The nuance: this helps explain the endowed-progress effect. Two free stamps shrink the remaining distance at the very point when it looks largest. The same logic suggests a practical trick for any long goal: early on, count what you've done; past the halfway point, count what's left.
proof: Supported by the original field study and later research on progress framing.
spot: Notice profile setups that start at "40% complete", and loyalty cards with free first stamps.
use: Give yourself a head start on one task today: count work you've already done as progress, or tick off the easy first step right away.
guard: Notice when a "head start" is designed to keep you buying. Ask whether you'd pursue the goal without it.
predict: Car-wash customers needed 8 more washes either way. How many with a "2 of 10 done" card finished, versus 19% with a blank 8-stamp card?
- About 19%, the same
* About 34%
- About 80%
scenario: Malini's fitness app shows her as "25% of the way to your goal" just for completing the sign-up questions, and she finds herself keen to keep going.
source: Nunes, J. C., & Drèze, X. (2006). The endowed progress effect. Journal of Consumer Research.
related: goal-gradient

## overjustification
title: The overjustification effect
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Paying children to draw made them draw less once the rewards stopped.
what: When you start getting external rewards for something you already enjoyed, you may begin to see it as work done for the reward. When the reward goes, the interest can go with it. Expected, tangible rewards for interesting tasks are most likely to backfire; unexpected rewards and praise are less risky.
study: In 1973 Mark Lepper, David Greene and Richard Nisbett told some preschoolers they'd get a "Good Player" certificate for drawing with markers. Later, in free play, those children spent about half as much time drawing as children who hadn't expected a reward. A 1999 meta-analysis of 128 studies by Edward Deci, Richard Koestner and Richard Ryan found expected tangible rewards reduce intrinsic motivation for interesting tasks; others argue the effect is narrower.
case: In 2000 Uri Gneezy and Aldo Rustichini studied an annual tradition in Israel: high-school students going door to door to collect donations for charity. 180 students were split into three groups, and all heard a short speech about the cause's importance. One group received no pay. A second was told they would also receive 1% of what they collected, and a third 10%, paid by the researchers rather than out of the donations. The unpaid students collected the most, about 239 shekels per pair on average. Those paid 1% collected far less, about 154, and even the 10% group, about 219, didn't catch up with the volunteers. The nuance: a small payment turned a civic duty into a poorly paid job. Money isn't neutral; it can change what an activity means. Hence the authors' title and advice: "Pay enough or don't pay at all".
proof: Supported by meta-analysis, though its scope is debated.
spot: Notice hobbies that turned into side hustles and stopped being fun, or children rewarded for reading who stop reading for pleasure.
use: For something you love doing, protect it today: do it purely for its own sake, with no tracking or reward.
guard: Rewards work well for boring tasks. For things people already enjoy, prefer praise, choice and recognition over payment.
predict: Children who expected a reward for drawing, compared with those who didn't, later spent how much free time drawing?
- More time
- About the same
* About half as much
scenario: Zara loved baking until she started selling cakes to neighbours. Now baking feels like a chore she does for orders.
source: Lepper, M. R., Greene, D., & Nisbett, R. E. (1973). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Deci, E. L., Koestner, R., & Ryan, R. M. (1999). Psychological Bulletin.
related: self-determination, variable-rewards

## variable-rewards
title: Variable rewards
category: habits
evidence: solid
hook: Slot machines and social feeds keep you hooked the same way: you never know when the next reward will come.
what: When rewards come unpredictably, behaviour becomes intense and hard to stop, because the next try might be the one that pays off. This "variable ratio" schedule drives gambling, refresh-to-update feeds, and checking your phone for likes and messages.
study: In 1957 Charles Ferster and B. F. Skinner showed that animals rewarded on unpredictable schedules pressed levers at high, steady rates and kept going long after rewards stopped, much longer than animals rewarded predictably. The same principle is deliberately built into slot machines and many apps.
case: In 2008 Kevin Volpp and colleagues borrowed the slot machine's logic for health. 57 adults with obesity at a Philadelphia veterans' hospital aimed to lose a pound a week for 16 weeks. One group entered a daily lottery: if they were on track, they had a small chance of winning about $10 and a smaller chance of $100, and they were told when they would have won had they been on track. A second group put some of their own money into a deposit contract; a third had only monthly weigh-ins. After 16 weeks, the lottery group had lost about 13 pounds on average and the deposit group about 14, against only a few pounds in the control group. Seven months later much of the weight had returned, and differences from the control group were no longer clear. The nuance: unpredictable rewards are excellent at starting behaviour, which is why apps use them. Without a habit or reason that outlasts the rewards, the behaviour fades when the lottery stops.
proof: One of the most reliable findings in the psychology of learning.
spot: Notice the pull-to-refresh motion, the "someone liked your post" notification, or loot boxes in games.
use: Turn off one app's notifications today, or check it only at two set times. Notice the urge to check, and how it fades.
guard: Put unpredictable rewards behind friction: log out, move apps off the home screen, or schedule them.
predict: Compared with predictable rewards, how did animals on unpredictable reward schedules behave when rewards stopped?
- They stopped sooner
* They kept going much longer
- No difference
scenario: Vivek opens Instagram "just for a second", refreshes three times hoping for a new like, and looks up 40 minutes later.
source: Ferster, C. B., & Skinner, B. F. (1957). Schedules of Reinforcement.
related: temptation-bundling, overjustification, gamblers-fallacy

## marshmallow-test
title: The marshmallow test
category: habits
evidence: debated
hook: Waiting for a second marshmallow at age 4 was said to predict success decades later. With family background accounted for, the link shrinks a lot.
what: In the famous Stanford studies, children who waited longer for a bigger reward later had better test scores. It became a symbol of willpower as destiny. Larger, more diverse studies found the link was much weaker, and largely explained by family background and early ability. Children also wait longer when they trust the adult.
study: In 2018 Tyler Watts, Greg Duncan and Haonan Quan studied over 900 children from diverse backgrounds. The link between waiting at 4 and achievement at 15 was about half the size of the original, and fell by about two thirds after controlling for family background and early cognitive ability. In 2013 Celeste Kidd and colleagues found children waited about four times longer when the experimenter had kept an earlier promise.
case: In 2018 Bettina Lamm, Heidi Keller and colleagues ran the marshmallow test, using a local food treat, with 4-year-olds in two very different settings: children of Nso farming families in rural Cameroon and children of middle-class families in Germany. About 70% of the Nso children waited the full ten minutes, compared with about 28% of the German children. Many of the Nso children waited calmly, without the elaborate distraction strategies the classic studies treated as signs of self-control. Nso parenting emphasises obedience to adults' instructions and emotional restraint from an early age. The nuance: waiting isn't a pure, culture-free measure of willpower. What the test captures depends on what a child has learned about authority, trust and food, one more reason the original links to later success shrank once background was taken into account.
proof: Debated: delay of gratification is linked to outcomes, but much more weakly than the famous story suggests.
spot: Notice stories that treat willpower as fixed destiny ("some people just have self-control").
use: Make waiting easier today instead of relying on willpower: put the temptation out of sight, or distract yourself, as the children who waited did.
guard: When judging someone's "self-control", consider their situation: is the promise of later reliable? Can they afford to wait?
predict: When the experimenter had broken an earlier promise, how long did children wait for the second treat?
- About as long as other children
* Much less: about a quarter as long as children with a reliable experimenter
- Longer, to prove themselves
scenario: A parenting influencer claims that if your four-year-old can't wait for a second sweet, they're likely to struggle in life.
source: Watts, T. W., Duncan, G. J., & Quan, H. (2018). Revisiting the marshmallow test. Psychological Science; Kidd, C., Palmeri, H., & Aslin, R. N. (2013). Cognition.
related: ego-depletion, present-bias

## commitment-devices
title: Commitment devices
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Lock away the temptation now, while you're strong. Savers with locked accounts saved 81% more.
what: A commitment device is a choice you make today that restricts your future options, so your future self can't give in. Locking savings, paying in advance for classes, or betting money on your goal all work this way. They're especially useful for people who know they're tempted.
study: In 2006 Nava Ashraf, Dean Karlan and Wesley Yin offered people in the Philippines a savings account that locked funds until a date or amount they chose. A year later, those offered it had saved 81% more than a control group. In 2010 a related programme where smokers deposited money they'd lose if they failed a urine test raised quit rates.
case: In Chennai, India, Frank Schilbach ran a three-week field experiment with 229 cycle-rickshaw drivers, men who often drank heavily during the working day. Some received a fixed daily payment for visiting a study office; others were paid more on days their breathalyser test showed they were sober. When given a choice, more than half the drivers chose the sobriety incentives even when this meant they could earn less money, a clear demand for a commitment device. The sobriety incentives reduced daytime drinking, though total drinking didn't change, and they raised savings by about 50%. The nuance: the drivers weren't naive about their drinking; they knew they had a self-control problem and were willing to pay to be held to account. Yet the device moved the timing of drinking more than the amount, a reminder that commitments target a specific behaviour, not the underlying urge.
proof: Supported by several field experiments; uptake is often low, as people underestimate their need for them.
spot: Notice fixed deposits, prepaid gym memberships, website blockers and promises made publicly.
use: Set up one small commitment device today: a recurring transfer to savings, an app blocker during work hours, or a workout booked and paid for.
guard: Make the cost of breaking it real but not ruinous. Too harsh a device can backfire if life changes.
predict: After a year, how much more had people offered a locked savings account saved than a control group?
- About 8%
* About 81%
- About the same
scenario: Knowing he'll spend any money in his account, Raman sets up an automatic transfer to a recurring deposit the day his salary arrives.
source: Ashraf, N., Karlan, D., & Yin, W. (2006). Tying Odysseus to the mast. Quarterly Journal of Economics; Giné, X., Karlan, D., & Zinman, J. (2010). American Economic Journal: Applied Economics.
related: present-bias, implementation-intentions

## self-determination
title: Self-determination theory
category: habits
evidence: solid
hook: Lasting motivation comes from three needs: choice, growing competence, and connection to others.
what: Self-determination theory says we're most motivated, and feel best, when three needs are met: autonomy (it's my choice), competence (I'm getting better) and relatedness (I'm connected to others). Rewards and pressure can get short-term compliance, but motivation that meets these needs lasts.
study: Edward Deci and Richard Ryan developed the theory in the 1980s and summarised it in 2000. Hundreds of studies in education, work, sport and healthcare have since found that environments supporting autonomy, competence and relatedness produce more lasting motivation and better well-being; meta-analyses in health behaviour support the link.
case: In 2012 Sung Hyeon Cheon, Johnmarshall Reeve and Ik Sung Moon trained physical education teachers in South Korea to teach in an autonomy-supportive way: explaining why an activity was worth doing, offering choices, acknowledging students' complaints rather than dismissing them, and using inviting rather than controlling language. Teachers were randomly assigned to the training or not, and their classes were followed over a semester. Students of trained teachers reported feeling more autonomous and competent, were rated as more engaged, and showed more motivation, while controlling teaching declined; a one-year follow-up found the benefits largely persisted. The nuance: no rewards or punishments were involved. The same PE lesson, framed as a choice with a reason rather than an order, changed students' motivation, and later work found the trained teachers themselves felt more satisfied in their jobs.
proof: One of the most extensively tested theories of motivation.
spot: Notice when you or others stop enjoying something because it became compulsory, too hard, or lonely.
use: For one task you've been avoiding, add one of the three today: choose how you do it, make it slightly easier to succeed, or do it with someone.
guard: When motivating others, avoid "you must". Offer choice, a clear path to getting better, and a sense of belonging.
predict: According to self-determination theory, which is NOT one of the three core needs?
- Autonomy
* Recognition from authority
- Relatedness
scenario: Ajit hated running when his coach forced fixed routes. When he joined a friendly club and chose his own routes, he started running five days a week.
source: Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2000). Self-determination theory and the facilitation of intrinsic motivation. American Psychologist.
related: overjustification, self-efficacy

## ironic-process
title: Ironic process theory
aka: Don't think of a white bear
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Try not to think of a white bear. You just did.
what: When we try to suppress a thought, part of the mind keeps checking whether we're succeeding, which keeps the thought active. Under stress or distraction, the suppressed thought can rebound. That's why "don't think about it" and "don't be nervous" so often backfire.
study: In 1987 Daniel Wegner and colleagues asked people to think aloud for five minutes without thinking of a white bear, ringing a bell if they did. They rang it often. Afterwards, when allowed to think of the bear, they thought of it more than people who'd never tried to suppress it. Later reviews found a reliable but modest rebound effect.
case: In 1998 Daniel Wegner, Matthew Ansfield and Daniel Pilloff had people putt golf balls on an indoor green towards a spot. Some were told to be especially careful not to overshoot it. When putting under mental load, such as holding a number in memory, those people overshot more than people given no warning, the very error they were trying to avoid. In a related experiment, people held a pendulum steady while trying not to let it swing in a particular direction; under load, it swung more in exactly that direction. The nuance: the warning didn't backfire when people had full attention. The irony appears when the mind is busy or stressed, which is exactly when athletes, surgeons and speakers are most likely to tell themselves "don't". Instructions framed as what to do ("stop it at the spot") avoid giving the mind a forbidden target to monitor.
proof: Replicated widely; the rebound effect is real but smaller than early studies suggested.
spot: Notice when telling yourself "don't check your phone" or "don't think about the exam" makes it all you think about.
use: Instead of trying not to think about something today, replace it: decide what you will focus on instead.
guard: For worries, try scheduling a short "worry time" and writing them down, rather than pushing them away all day.
predict: After trying to suppress thoughts of a white bear, what happened when people were allowed to think about it?
- They thought of it less than others
* They thought of it more than others
- No difference
scenario: Before the interview, Sanya keeps telling herself "don't be nervous, don't be nervous", and gets more nervous with every repetition.
source: Wegner, D. M., Schneider, D. J., Carter, S. R., & White, T. L. (1987). Paradoxical effects of thought suppression. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: reactance

## learned-helplessness
title: Learned helplessness (revised)
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Fifty years on, the researchers flipped their own theory: passivity isn't learned. What's learned is control.
what: The original theory said that after repeated uncontrollable bad events, animals and people learn that nothing they do matters, and give up. In 2016 the same researchers concluded, from neuroscience evidence, that passivity is the brain's default response to prolonged bad events. What can be learned is that we have control, and that lesson protects against future helplessness.
study: In 1967 Martin Seligman and Steven Maier found that dogs given inescapable shocks later failed to escape shocks they could avoid. In 2016, reviewing decades of brain research, Maier and Seligman concluded the original theory had it backwards: passivity is the default, and detecting control activates brain circuits that inhibit it.
case: In 1976 Ellen Langer and Judith Rodin ran a study in a Connecticut nursing home. On one floor, residents were told they were responsible for their own lives there: how they spent their time, how their rooms were arranged, and voicing complaints; each also chose and cared for a plant. On another floor, residents heard that staff would take care of them, and were given plants that staff watered. Three weeks later, the "responsibility" residents were more active, alert and happier. Eighteen months later, 15% of them had died, compared with 30% of the comparison group. But a parallel study by Richard Schulz, which let residents control the timing of student visits, had a darker twist: when the study ended and the visits stopped, those who had gained control declined more than those who never had it. The nuance: control is powerful, which is why losing it hurts. The mortality figures come from small groups, but the pattern fits Maier and Seligman's revision: people learn control, and environments can teach it or take it away.
proof: Supported by decades of research; the revised version is the original researchers' own update.
spot: Notice when someone who has faced repeated setbacks stops trying, and when small wins reignite their effort.
use: Give yourself one small, controllable win today: pick a task you can definitely finish, and finish it.
guard: When you feel helpless, look for any small action you control. Experiences of control build resilience.
predict: In 2016, what did the original researchers conclude about learned helplessness?
- It was confirmed exactly as first described
* Passivity is the default; what's learned is control
- It only applies to animals
scenario: After three failed job applications, Karthik stops applying. A mentor helps him land one small freelance project, and he starts applying again.
source: Seligman, M. E. P., & Maier, S. F. (1967). Journal of Experimental Psychology; Maier, S. F., & Seligman, M. E. P. (2016). Learned helplessness at fifty. Psychological Review.
related: self-efficacy

## self-efficacy
title: Self-efficacy
category: habits
evidence: solid
hook: Believing you can do a specific task is one of the best predictors of whether you will. Small wins build it.
what: Self-efficacy is confidence in your ability to succeed at a particular task. It's built mostly by mastery experiences (actually succeeding), but also by watching people like you succeed, encouragement, and how you interpret your nerves. People with higher self-efficacy set bigger goals and keep going longer.
study: Albert Bandura introduced self-efficacy in 1977. A 1998 meta-analysis by Alexander Stajkovic and Fred Luthans of 114 studies found a substantial relationship between self-efficacy and work performance (r = 0.38).
case: In 1995 Dov Eden and Yaakov Zuk worked with 25 cadets in Israel Defense Forces naval training, for whom seasickness was a real threat to performance at sea. Cadets were randomly assigned to a brief interview with a psychologist. In the experimental group, he told each cadet that, based on their questionnaires and other information, they were unlikely to suffer from seasickness and that, if they did, it wouldn't harm their performance; the control group received no such message. At sea, the cadets who had been told they could cope reported less seasickness and were rated as performing better. The nuance: no one changed the sea or the cadets' stomachs. A credible expectation of coping changed how the symptoms played out and how much they interfered. It was a small study, but it shows self-efficacy isn't just a fixed trait; a trusted voice can build it.
proof: Among the most studied concepts in psychology, consistently linked to performance and persistence.
spot: Notice "I'm just not a maths person" or "I could never speak in public", beliefs about specific abilities that shape effort.
use: Pick something you doubt you can do. Find the smallest version of it and succeed at that today.
guard: Self-efficacy should be earned through practice. Confidence without skill can lead to overconfidence.
predict: Which source builds self-efficacy most strongly, according to Bandura?
* Succeeding at the task yourself
- Being told you're capable
- Reading about people who succeeded
scenario: Terrified of driving, Meghna starts by driving around an empty school ground. A month later, she's comfortable on the main road.
source: Bandura, A. (1977). Self-efficacy. Psychological Review; Stajkovic, A. D., & Luthans, F. (1998). Psychological Bulletin.
related: growth-mindset, learned-helplessness, self-determination

## flow
title: Flow
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Total absorption, where time disappears, happens when the challenge matches your skill. Oddly, more often at work than at leisure.
what: Flow is the state of being fully absorbed in an activity, losing track of time and self-consciousness. It tends to happen when a task has clear goals, immediate feedback, and a challenge that slightly stretches your skills. Too easy and you're bored; too hard and you're anxious.
study: Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi described flow from interviews with artists, athletes and surgeons. In 1989, with Judith LeFevre, he used beepers to sample workers' experiences at random moments and found flow occurred more often at work than in leisure, yet people still said they wished they were doing something else when at work.
case: In 2005 Arnold Bakker studied 178 music teachers and 605 of their students across 16 music schools in the Netherlands. Teachers reported how often they experienced flow while teaching: absorption, enjoyment and intrinsic motivation. Students separately reported their own experiences during lessons. Teachers with more autonomy, feedback, social support and coaching in their jobs reported more flow. And teachers' flow spilled over: students of teachers who experienced more flow reported more flow themselves. The nuance: flow is usually described as private and individual, but this study suggests it is partly social. Working conditions feed it, and it can spread from one absorbed person to those around them, so a teacher or manager who protects their own focus may also be protecting their group's.
proof: Supported by decades of experience-sampling research, though flow is hard to measure precisely.
spot: Notice moments when you look up and an hour has passed: what were you doing, and how hard was it?
use: Set up one 45-minute block today with a clear goal, no notifications, and a task just beyond your comfort zone.
guard: Flow can also happen in activities that aren't good for you (endless gaming). It's a state, not a guarantee of value.
predict: In the beeper study, where did workers experience flow more often?
* At work
- In leisure time
- About equally
scenario: Sanjay sits down to fix a tricky bug at 2 p.m. When he solves it, he's surprised to see it's dark outside.
source: Csikszentmihalyi, M. (1990). Flow; Csikszentmihalyi, M., & LeFevre, J. (1989). Optimal experience in work and leisure. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: self-determination

## premortem
title: The premortem
category: habits
evidence: good
hook: Imagine it's a year from now and your plan has failed. Now explain why. That one twist surfaces risks ordinary planning misses.
what: In a premortem, before committing to a plan, a team imagines it has already failed and each person writes down why. Treating failure as certain rather than possible makes reasons easier to find, and it gives doubters permission to speak instead of looking disloyal. Psychologist Gary Klein devised it, and Daniel Kahneman recommends it in Thinking, Fast and Slow as a partial remedy for overconfidence.
study: In 1989 Deborah Mitchell, Edward Russo and Nancy Pennington found that imagining an event had already happened, which they called prospective hindsight, increased the number of reasons people came up with for it by about 30%. In 2010 Elizabeth Veinott, Gary Klein and Sterling Wiggins had 178 people evaluate a plan for responding to a flu epidemic. Those who ran a premortem reduced their overconfidence in the plan more than those who critiqued it, listed pros and cons, or listed only cons.
case: In 2016 David Gallop, Chris Willy and John Bischoff tested the premortem with 101 experienced programme managers and engineers, working in teams on a realistic plan to introduce a new technology. Teams trained to run premortems identified significantly more high-quality risks than teams using ordinary brainstorming, and proposed more effective changes to the plan. They were also better at spotting rare, catastrophic risks of the kind that had actually derailed the real project the scenario was based on. The nuance: a premortem only helps if the reasons change something. Decide in advance what you'll do with the list, and do it before enthusiasm for the plan returns.
proof: Good: supported by experiments on prospective hindsight and several controlled studies of the technique, though large field trials are still few.
spot: Notice plans that are only ever discussed in terms of how they'll succeed, and meetings where nobody has asked how it could go wrong.
use: Take one plan for this week and give it five minutes: "It's next Sunday and this failed. Why?" Write at least three reasons and fix the easiest one.
guard: Before any big commitment, run a premortem and have each person write their reasons privately before anyone speaks, so the boss's view doesn't shape the list.
predict: People imagined that a future event had already happened, rather than that it might happen. What changed?
- Nothing; imagining certainty made no difference
* They came up with about 30% more reasons for it
- They came up with fewer reasons, feeling it was settled
scenario: Before launching the new menu, Sneha asks her staff to imagine it's three months later and the menu has flopped, and to write down why. Two cooks independently write that the tandoor can't keep up at lunch.
source: Klein, G. (2007). Performing a project premortem. Harvard Business Review; Mitchell, D. J., Russo, J. E., & Pennington, N. (1989). Back to the future: Temporal perspective in the explanation of events. Journal of Behavioral Decision Making; Veinott, B., Klein, G. A., & Wiggins, S. (2010). Evaluating the effectiveness of the PreMortem technique on plan confidence. Proceedings of ISCRAM; Gallop, D., Willy, C., & Bischoff, J. (2016). How to catch a black swan. Journal of Enterprise Transformation.
related: planning-fallacy, overconfidence, mental-contrasting

## round-number-goals
title: Round numbers as goals
category: habits
evidence: solid
hook: Professional batters were nearly four times as likely to end a season hitting .300 as .299. Round numbers pull effort toward them.
what: Round numbers, such as 100, a four-hour marathon or a score of 1,500, aren't special in any objective way, but we treat them as goals. Just below one, we work harder to cross it; just above, we relax. They act as reference points, so falling just short feels like a loss and getting over the line like a win.
study: In 2011 Devin Pope and Uri Simonsohn found that professional baseball players were nearly four times as likely to finish a season with a batting average of exactly .300 as .299. Players just below the line batted better in their final appearance of the season, while players at .300 were more likely to be taken out of the line-up to protect it. Students were also more likely to retake the SAT exam after scoring just below a round hundred than just above it.
case: In 2017 Eric Allen, Patricia Dechow, Devin Pope and George Wu analysed 9,789,093 marathon finishing times. Finishes bunched just before round-number times: far more runners finished in the minutes just under four hours than just over it, and the same pattern appeared at three hours, three and a half hours and other round times. The researchers argue that round times work as reference points, just as prospect theory predicts. The nuance: round-number goals can help, because they're concrete and motivating. But they're arbitrary, and the last burst of effort can be misplaced, as when a runner pushes through an injury to break a round time or a manager pads a figure to reach 100.
proof: Solid: shown in very large field datasets from marathons, baseball and exams, and in experiments, consistent with goals acting as reference points.
spot: Notice bursts of effort near round numbers: a step count close to 10,000, a fundraiser at 95% of its target, sales just short of a round figure at the end of a quarter.
use: Set one small goal today as a round number just beyond your usual (say, 20 minutes instead of 17), and notice whether it pulls you along at the end.
guard: When a round number is driving a decision, ask whether 99 or 101 would really be different. Don't take risks just to cross an arbitrary line.
predict: How did the number of professional baseball players finishing a season at .300 compare with those finishing at .299?
- About the same at each
- More finished at .299, as pressure made them choke
* Nearly four times as many finished at .300
scenario: Kriti has walked 9,700 steps by bedtime, so she paces the corridor until her watch shows 10,000, though she'd never walk an extra 300 steps for any other reason.
source: Pope, D., & Simonsohn, U. (2011). Round numbers as goals. Psychological Science; Allen, E. J., Dechow, P. M., Pope, D. G., & Wu, G. (2017). Reference-dependent preferences: Evidence from marathon runners. Management Science; Heath, C., Larrick, R. P., & Wu, G. (1999). Goals as reference points. Cognitive Psychology.
related: goal-gradient, endowed-progress, loss-aversion

# =====================================================================================
# GROUPS & SOCIETY
# =====================================================================================

## asch-conformity
title: Conformity
aka: The Asch line experiment
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: Three-quarters of people went along with an obviously wrong group answer at least once.
what: When everyone around us says the same thing, we feel strong pressure to agree, even when our own eyes say otherwise. Sometimes we conform because we doubt ourselves, sometimes just to avoid standing out. One ally who disagrees dramatically weakens the pressure.
study: In the 1950s Solomon Asch asked people to match line lengths, a task so easy that alone, people almost never erred. But when actors in the group all gave the same wrong answer, participants went along on about a third of those trials, and about 75% conformed at least once. When just one other person gave the right answer, conformity dropped sharply. A 1996 meta-analysis of 133 studies across 17 countries found conformity was higher in collectivist cultures and had declined in the US since the 1950s.
case: In 2006 Matthew Salganik, Peter Dodds and Duncan Watts built an online music market, MusicLab, where 14,341 people could listen to and download songs by unknown bands. Some participants saw only the song titles; others also saw how many times each song had been downloaded by earlier participants. The social-influence group was split into eight separate "worlds", each building its own download counts. When people could see others' choices, popularity snowballed: hits became bigger hits, and which songs became hits differed from world to world. Quality mattered at the extremes, since the best songs rarely did badly and the worst rarely did well, but anything in between could happen. The nuance: this was conformity without any pressure. No one was watching; people simply used others' choices as information, and small early differences grew into large, arbitrary outcomes. It helps explain why success in music, books and films is so hard to predict.
proof: Replicated across many countries; levels vary by culture and era.
spot: Notice meetings where nobody disagrees with the first opinion voiced, or a WhatsApp group where everyone "agrees" with a forward.
use: In a group discussion today, be the one who voices a mild, honest disagreement, or support someone else who does.
guard: When making group decisions, collect people's views privately before discussion, so early voices don't set the tone.
predict: In Asch's experiments, what share of people went along with a clearly wrong group answer at least once?
- About 10%
- About 35%
* About 75%
scenario: Everyone at the table praises the new restaurant's food, so Kavita says she loves it too, although she found it bland.
source: Asch, S. E. (1956). Studies of independence and conformity. Psychological Monographs; Bond, R., & Smith, P. B. (1996). Psychological Bulletin.
related: social-proof, pluralistic-ignorance, groupthink, milgram-obedience

## bystander-effect
title: The bystander effect (and its twist)
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: In the lab, more bystanders meant less help. On real CCTV footage, someone helped in 9 out of 10 fights.
what: In emergencies, the presence of others can diffuse responsibility: everyone assumes someone else will act. Lab studies found this reliably. But footage of real public conflicts shows that help is usually given, and more bystanders make it more likely that at least one person intervenes. The famous Kitty Genovese case, with "38 silent witnesses", was largely a myth.
study: In 1968 John Darley and Bibb Latané had students hear someone apparently having a seizure over an intercom. Alone, 85% went to help before it ended; when they believed four others were listening, 31% did. In 2020 Richard Philpot and colleagues analysed CCTV footage of 219 real public conflicts in Amsterdam, Cape Town and Lancaster: at least one bystander intervened in about 91% of them. In 2007 Rachel Manning, Mark Levine and Alan Collins showed the "38 witnesses" story was badly exaggerated.
case: In 2000 Patrick Markey studied about 400 internet chat rooms with nearly 5,000 participants. A researcher posing as an ordinary user posted a simple request for help, such as how to look at someone's profile. The more people present in a chat room, the longer it took anyone to help. But when the request named a specific person in the room, help came quickly, whatever the group size. The nuance: the bystander effect isn't apathy; it's diffused responsibility. Everyone assumes someone else will answer, and naming one person removes the ambiguity. First-aid training uses exactly this principle: in an emergency, don't shout "someone call an ambulance!"; point at one person and say "You, in the blue shirt, call an ambulance."
proof: The lab effect is real; in real-world emergencies, intervention is the norm. Both are well supported.
spot: Notice moments when a group sees a problem (someone fallen, a spill, a lost child) and everyone looks at everyone else.
use: If you see something that needs doing today (litter, someone lost, a fallen bike), be the first to act. One person moving often unlocks others.
guard: If you need help in a crowd, point at one specific person: "You in the blue shirt, please call an ambulance."
predict: In CCTV footage of 219 real public conflicts, how often did at least one bystander intervene?
- About 10% of the time
- About 50% of the time
* About 90% of the time
scenario: A man collapses on a crowded platform. For a long moment, everyone looks at each other, until one woman kneels down, and suddenly several others rush to help.
source: Darley, J. M., & Latané, B. (1968). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Philpot, R., et al. (2020). Would I be helped? American Psychologist; Manning, R., Levine, M., & Collins, A. (2007). American Psychologist.
related: pluralistic-ignorance, social-loafing

## stanford-prison
title: The Stanford Prison Experiment myth
category: groups
evidence: busted
hook: The famous prison study is taught as proof that ordinary people turn cruel in roles. The guards were coached.
what: In 1971 Philip Zimbardo turned a Stanford basement into a mock prison with students as guards and prisoners. It was stopped after six days amid reports of guard brutality, and became a textbook story about situations making good people cruel. Archival recordings and interviews later showed guards were encouraged to be tough, Zimbardo was an active participant, and at least one famous breakdown was partly acted.
study: In 2019 Thibault Le Texier published an analysis of the experiment's archives in American Psychologist, documenting the coaching of guards and the experimenters' role. A 2002 BBC Prison Study by Alexander Haslam and Stephen Reicher, with ethical safeguards, found guards did not spontaneously become tyrannical; how groups formed and who led them mattered more.
case: In 2004, photographs from Abu Ghraib prison in Iraq showed US military police guards abusing detainees. Philip Zimbardo, who had run the Stanford Prison Experiment, testified as an expert witness for one of the guards, Staff Sergeant Ivan Frederick, arguing that the situation, not just individual character, explained the abuse. Frederick was still sentenced to eight years in prison, and Zimbardo went on to write The Lucifer Effect (2007), drawing close parallels with his 1971 study. The nuance: archival research later showed the Stanford guards had been coached towards toughness, so the story that a bad situation automatically turns good people cruel is weaker than Zimbardo claimed. Alexander Haslam and Stephen Reicher argue both cases fit another account: abuse happens when people identify with leaders who encourage it and come to believe they are doing something worthwhile. That puts responsibility back on leadership and on those who choose to follow, without denying that situations matter.
proof: Busted as scientific evidence: it was not a controlled experiment, and the behaviour was shaped by the experimenters.
spot: Notice when a dramatic story about human nature is cited as science without details of how it was done.
use: Next time you hear "studies show people are naturally cruel/selfish/lazy", ask how the study was set up, and who influenced the participants.
guard: Situations matter, but so do leadership and group identity. Cruelty usually needs someone encouraging it.
predict: What did archival research reveal about the guards in the Stanford Prison Experiment?
- They became cruel entirely on their own
* They were encouraged to be tough by the researchers
- None of the guards were cruel
scenario: A management trainer tells a room of new managers that "power always corrupts, as Stanford proved", and moves on.
source: Le Texier, T. (2019). Debunking the Stanford Prison Experiment. American Psychologist; Reicher, S., & Haslam, S. A. (2006). British Journal of Social Psychology.
related: milgram-obedience, replication-crisis

## pluralistic-ignorance
title: Pluralistic ignorance
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: Everyone privately disagrees, but each thinks they're the only one. So nobody speaks.
what: When people misjudge what others privately think, they follow a norm that hardly anyone actually supports. Silence in a classroom ("Any questions?") or support for an unpopular custom can persist because everyone assumes everyone else is on board.
study: In 1993 Deborah Prentice and Dale Miller found that Princeton students were, on average, less comfortable with campus drinking than they believed the average student was. In 2020 Leonardo Bursztyn, Alessandra González and David Yanagizawa-Drott found that most young married men in Saudi Arabia privately supported women working outside the home, but greatly underestimated how many other men did. Telling them the real numbers made them more willing to help their wives find jobs.
case: In 1998 Christine Schroeder and Deborah Prentice tested whether exposing pluralistic ignorance could change behaviour among first-year students at Princeton. In their first weeks, students attended small peer-led discussions. In one version, the discussion explained pluralistic ignorance: many students privately feel less comfortable with heavy drinking than they assume their peers do, and everyone acting on that false belief keeps the drinking culture going. The other version took a more conventional approach focused on individual decisions about drinking. Four to six months later, students who had learned about pluralistic ignorance reported drinking less. The nuance: they weren't told drinking was bad. They were told something about their peers: you're not the only one who feels uncomfortable. When a false norm loses its power, private preferences can finally show up in behaviour.
proof: Documented in many settings, including large field studies.
spot: Notice when nobody asks a question in a meeting, then several people ask you afterwards in private.
use: Ask the "silly" question in a meeting today, or say the thing you suspect others are thinking.
guard: Use anonymous polls to find out what people really think before assuming you're in the minority.
predict: When Saudi men learned how many others privately supported women working, what happened?
- Nothing changed
* They became more willing to help their wives find jobs
- They became less supportive
scenario: After the lecture, the professor asks "Any questions?" and nobody speaks. Outside, six students admit they were completely lost.
source: Prentice, D. A., & Miller, D. T. (1993). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Bursztyn, L., González, A. L., & Yanagizawa-Drott, D. (2020). American Economic Review.
related: asch-conformity, social-proof, norm-boomerang, bystander-effect, groupthink

## milgram-obedience
title: Obedience to authority
aka: The Milgram experiment
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: Two-thirds of people kept giving what they thought were dangerous shocks because a man in a grey coat told them to.
what: Ordinary people will follow instructions from an authority figure to a shocking degree, especially when responsibility seems to lie with the authority and the steps are gradual. But obedience isn't automatic: it depends on how much people identify with the authority's goals, and on whether anyone else resists.
study: In 1963 Stanley Milgram had participants, as "teachers", give increasing electric shocks to a "learner" (an actor) for wrong answers. Prodded by the experimenter, 26 of 40 went to the maximum, 450 volts. In 2009 Jerry Burger ran a safer partial replication stopping at 150 volts: 70% were willing to continue past that point. Historians reviewing Milgram's archives note that many participants doubted the shocks were real, and that obedience varied hugely between versions of the study.
case: In 1966 psychiatrist Charles Hofling arranged for a stranger calling himself "Dr Smith" to phone 22 nurses on duty in US hospitals. He told each to give a patient 20 mg of an unfamiliar drug, Astroten, placed on the ward for the study, and said he would sign the order later. The box stated that the maximum dose was 10 mg, and hospital rules forbade taking phone orders from unknown doctors. 21 of the 22 nurses were stopped on their way to give the drug. But in 1977 Steven Rank and Cardell Jacobson ran a version with a familiar drug (Valium, at a high dose), the name of a real doctor on staff, and nurses free to consult colleagues: only 2 of 18 were fully prepared to comply. The nuance: obedience is real, but it depends on conditions. Ambiguity, isolation and unfamiliarity push people to defer; knowledge and colleagues to check with make it far easier to say no. That is the logic behind modern speak-up and double-check rules in hospitals.
proof: The basic finding replicates; its interpretation is debated.
spot: Notice "I was just following orders" or "the policy says so" as reasons for doing something that feels wrong.
use: The next time you're asked to do something you're unsure about today, ask the person to explain why, and take responsibility for your own choice.
guard: Watch for gradual escalation. Small steps make it hard to know where to stop; decide your limit in advance.
predict: In Milgram's original study, how many of 40 participants went to the maximum 450 volts?
- 4
- 14
* 26
scenario: Told by her manager to backdate some documents "because everyone does it", Rupa complies, even though it makes her deeply uncomfortable.
source: Milgram, S. (1963). Behavioral study of obedience. Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology; Burger, J. M. (2009). Replicating Milgram. American Psychologist.
related: authority, asch-conformity, stanford-prison

## social-loafing
title: Social loafing
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: In a group, each person tends to put in less effort than they would alone, especially if nobody can tell who did what.
what: When individual contributions are hidden in a group effort, people tend to coast. It's not always laziness: people may feel their effort doesn't matter or that others will do it. Making each person's contribution visible and meaningful reduces loafing.
study: In the 1910s Max Ringelmann found that people pulling a rope in groups exerted less force per person than when pulling alone. In 1979 Bibb Latané, Kipling Williams and Stephen Harkins found people clapped and shouted less loudly when they thought they were part of a group. A 1993 meta-analysis of 78 studies by Steven Karau and Kipling Williams confirmed the effect and showed it shrinks when contributions are identifiable and the task matters.
case: In 1989 Christopher Earley compared management trainees from the United States and the People's Republic of China on a realistic in-basket task, working through a pile of memos, messages and requests. Some worked believing their output would be pooled anonymously with a group's; others believed their individual performance would be identified. The American trainees did less when they thought their work would disappear into the group, classic social loafing. The Chinese trainees didn't loaf. Across both countries, people who held more collectivist values loafed less. The nuance: social loafing isn't a fixed law of groups. It depends on whether people see the group's success as their own. Meta-analyses that confirm loafing also find it smaller in collectivist cultures, in groups people value, and on tasks that matter to them.
proof: Robust and confirmed by meta-analysis.
spot: Notice group projects where one or two people do most of the work, or group chats where nobody replies to a request.
use: In a group task today, give each person a specific, visible responsibility, including yourself.
guard: In group requests, name names: "Can Riya and Amit handle this?" works better than "Can someone handle this?"
predict: When contributions in a group are identifiable, what happens to social loafing?
- It increases
* It shrinks
- No change
scenario: In a five-person college project, Ankur and Sonia write almost the entire report, while the others just add their names.
source: Latané, B., Williams, K., & Harkins, S. (1979). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Karau, S. J., & Williams, K. D. (1993). Psychological Bulletin.
related: bystander-effect, brainstorming

## minimal-group
title: The minimal group effect
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: Split strangers into groups by a coin toss and they start favouring their own group within minutes.
what: It takes almost nothing to create "us" and "them". Even meaningless group labels make people favour their own group, sometimes even sacrificing total gains to stay ahead of the other group. Group identity is powerful because it's part of how we see ourselves.
study: In 1971 Henri Tajfel and colleagues told schoolboys they'd been grouped by their preference for paintings by Klee or Kandinsky (in fact, it was random). Then they divided money between anonymous members of each group. They consistently favoured their own group, and often chose options that maximised the difference between groups over options that gave their own group more in absolute terms.
case: In 2005 Mark Levine and colleagues ran a field experiment with Manchester United fans at Lancaster University in England. After writing about their love of the club, each fan walked to another building and passed a jogger, actually an actor, who fell and appeared to hurt an ankle. When the jogger wore a Manchester United shirt, 92% of fans stopped to help; in a rival Liverpool shirt, 30% did; in a plain shirt, 33%. In a second version, fans first wrote about what they shared with football fans in general. Now the jogger in the Liverpool shirt was helped about as often as the one in the United shirt, while the plain-shirted jogger was still helped less. The nuance: group boundaries aren't fixed. The same people became more or less helpful depending on which identity was made salient, and redrawing the line slightly turned a rival into one of "us".
proof: Replicated many times and supported by meta-analyses of in-group favouritism.
spot: Notice how quickly rivalries form between departments, hostel wings or WhatsApp groups over trivial differences.
use: Notice one "us versus them" you're part of today. Find something you share with someone on the other side.
guard: Build bigger shared identities ("we're all on this project") to soften divisions between smaller groups.
predict: When dividing money, boys in minimal groups often chose options that...
- Gave both groups the most money
* Maximised their group's advantage over the other group, even at a cost
- Gave equal amounts to everyone
scenario: Two teams at a company offsite are formed by drawing names from a bowl. By lunch, the "Blue Team" is joking about how the "Red Team" is a bit dim.
source: Tajfel, H., Billig, M. G., Bundy, R. P., & Flament, C. (1971). Social categorization and intergroup behaviour. European Journal of Social Psychology.
related: contact-hypothesis, similarity

## group-polarization
title: Group polarisation
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: Like-minded people who discuss an issue together come out with more extreme views than they went in with.
what: When a group of people who lean the same way talk, they hear more arguments for their side and want to be seen as good members, so the group shifts further in that direction. It's why online echo chambers, juries and committees can drift to extremes.
study: Psychologists described the "risky shift" in the 1960s and later showed it was a general polarisation effect. In 2005 David Schkade, Cass Sunstein and Reid Hastie brought together groups of liberals in Boulder and conservatives in Colorado Springs to discuss issues like climate change. After discussion, liberals became more liberal and conservatives more conservative, and opinions within each group became more uniform.
case: In 2000 David Schkade, Cass Sunstein and Daniel Kahneman had more than 3,000 jury-eligible Americans decide cases in over 500 mock juries. Before discussing a case, each juror privately chose a punishment rating and a dollar amount for punitive damages; then the jury deliberated to a unanimous verdict. For dollar awards, deliberation produced a "severity shift": juries' awards were systematically higher than the median juror's view before deliberation, and in 27% of cases the jury's award was as high as or higher than the highest figure any individual juror had wanted before talking. The nuance: no single juror dragged the group to an extreme. Arguments for punishing more simply sounded more persuasive in discussion than arguments for punishing less, so the group ended up somewhere nobody had started. It is a sobering finding for any committee that assumes discussion produces moderation.
proof: Robust and replicated in labs, juries and online settings.
spot: Notice a group chat or forum where opinions get gradually more extreme and dissenting voices disappear.
use: Before a discussion with like-minded people today, invite one person who disagrees, or argue the other side yourself for a minute.
guard: Mix groups deliberately and seek out sources you disagree with, especially on issues you feel strongly about.
predict: After like-minded groups discussed political issues, what happened to their views?
- They moved towards the centre
* They became more extreme and more uniform
- They stayed the same
scenario: A neighbourhood WhatsApp group starts out mildly annoyed about stray dogs. After weeks of discussion, some members are calling for drastic measures.
source: Myers, D. G., & Lamm, H. (1976). Psychological Bulletin; Schkade, D., Sunstein, C. R., & Hastie, R. (2007). What happened on deliberation day? California Law Review.
related: groupthink, confirmation-bias

## deindividuation
title: Deindividuation
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: Anonymous children in groups were seven times more likely to take extra Halloween sweets than named children alone.
what: When we feel anonymous and part of a crowd, our sense of individual responsibility weakens. But rather than making people simply worse, anonymity makes them follow the norms of the group or situation more closely, for good or bad. Uniforms, masks and usernames all have this effect.
study: In 1976 Edward Diener and colleagues watched hundreds of children trick-or-treating at homes where they were told to take one sweet. Anonymous children in groups took extra 57% of the time, compared with 8% of children who were alone and had been asked their names. A 1998 meta-analysis by Tom Postmes and Russell Spears found anonymity mainly increases conformity to whatever norms are present.
case: In 1981 Leon Mann analysed 21 newspaper reports of crowds watching a person threatening to jump from a building, bridge or tower. In 10 of them, members of the crowd baited the person, shouting at them to jump. Baiting was more likely when the crowd was large, when it happened at night, and when the crowd was far from the person, all conditions that make individuals in a crowd harder to identify; it was also linked to hot weather and long-running episodes. The nuance: the same conditions don't always produce cruelty. Later research, including Postmes and Spears's meta-analysis, suggests anonymity makes people follow whatever norm the crowd is drifting towards. In some crowds that norm is jeering; in others, such as crowds at disasters, anonymous strangers help one another. Anonymity amplifies a group's norm; it doesn't decide it.
proof: Supported by experiments and meta-analysis, with the important correction that it amplifies group norms.
spot: Notice how people behave in anonymous online comments versus in person, or how fans in matching jerseys act together.
use: Use your own name and face in an online discussion today, and notice whether it changes how you write.
guard: Platforms and groups where anonymity is paired with bad norms get worse; make good norms visible.
predict: How often did anonymous children in groups take extra sweets, compared with 8% for named children alone?
- About 10%
- About 25%
* About 57%
scenario: Under anonymous accounts, members of an online fan club post insults they would never say to anyone's face.
source: Diener, E., Fraser, S. C., Beaman, A. L., & Kelem, R. T. (1976). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Postmes, T., & Spears, R. (1998). Psychological Bulletin.
related: social-proof

## brainstorming
title: The brainstorming myth
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: People working alone and then pooling ideas come up with more, and better, ideas than the same people brainstorming together.
what: Brainstorming in groups feels productive, but people have to wait their turn, fear judgement, and anchor on the first ideas. Individuals generating ideas alone, then combining them, consistently outperform group brainstorming. Groups are better for evaluating ideas than generating them.
study: Alex Osborn popularised brainstorming in the 1950s, claiming groups produce more ideas. In 1987 Michael Diehl and Wolfgang Stroebe found that "nominal groups" (people working alone, with ideas pooled afterwards) produced about twice as many ideas as real groups. A 1991 meta-analysis by Brian Mullen and colleagues confirmed group brainstorming produces fewer ideas.
case: In 1992 Brent Gallupe and colleagues tested electronic brainstorming, in which group members type ideas into a shared system at the same time, seeing others' ideas but never waiting for a turn to speak. In ordinary spoken brainstorming, adding people adds waiting: only one person can talk at once, and ideas are forgotten or held back while others have the floor. In the electronic version, larger groups produced more ideas, the opposite of face-to-face groups, because the bottleneck of turn-taking had disappeared. The nuance: the problem with brainstorming was never groups as such; it was "production blocking", having to wait to speak, plus fear of being judged. Comparisons with people working alone remain mixed, but formats like brainwriting, where everyone writes ideas silently before discussing them, keep much of the benefit of a group without the bottleneck.
proof: Robust and confirmed by meta-analysis.
spot: Notice in group brainstorms how a few voices dominate and many people say nothing.
use: Next time you need ideas today, have everyone write down ideas alone for five minutes first, then share.
guard: Use groups to combine, critique and choose ideas, not to generate them from scratch.
predict: Compared with group brainstorming, how many ideas did people working alone and pooling them produce?
- Fewer
- About the same
* About twice as many
scenario: In the marketing team's brainstorm, the manager speaks first, and all the following ideas turn out to be variations on his.
source: Diehl, M., & Stroebe, W. (1987). Productivity loss in brainstorming groups. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Mullen, B., Johnson, C., & Salas, E. (1991). Basic and Applied Social Psychology.
related: social-loafing, groupthink, anchoring

## wisdom-of-crowds
title: The wisdom of crowds
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: 787 fairgoers guessed an ox's weight. The middle guess was within 1% of the truth.
what: When many people make independent estimates, their errors tend to cancel out, and the average or median is often remarkably accurate, better than most individual experts. The key word is independent: when people see each other's guesses, the crowd loses its wisdom.
study: In 1906 Francis Galton analysed 787 guesses of an ox's weight at a country fair. The median guess, 1,207 pounds, was within 1% of the true weight of 1,198 pounds. In 2011 Jan Lorenz and colleagues found that when people could see others' estimates, the range of guesses narrowed without becoming more accurate, and people grew more confident in worse answers.
case: From 2011 to 2015, IARPA, the research agency of the US intelligence community, ran a forecasting tournament on questions such as whether a country would hold an election by a certain date. The Good Judgment Project, led by Philip Tetlock and Barbara Mellers, recruited thousands of volunteers and won decisively. Its methods were telling. It combined many independent forecasts, gave more weight to forecasters with good track records, then "extremised" the average, pushing it away from 50%, to counter the caution that averaging introduces. Brief training in probabilistic reasoning helped, and the top 2% of forecasters, the "superforecasters", were put together in elite teams; they were reported to be about 30% more accurate than intelligence analysts who had access to classified information. The nuance: crowds are wise when errors are independent and aggregation is smart. Simply letting people see each other's guesses, as Lorenz found, can destroy that wisdom; structured teams of accountable, diverse forecasters can enhance it.
proof: Robust when estimates are independent; well documented in forecasting.
spot: Notice prediction markets and averaged polls, and also meetings where everyone hears the boss's estimate first.
use: For an estimate today (time, cost, quantity), ask three or four people separately before sharing yours, and average them.
guard: Protect independence: collect estimates before discussion, and don't announce the senior person's view first.
predict: When people could see each other's estimates, what happened?
- The crowd became more accurate
