* Guesses became more similar but not more accurate
- Nothing changed
scenario: Instead of asking her team to agree on a deadline in the meeting, Shilpa collects everyone's estimate privately and uses the median, which turns out to be spot on.
source: Galton, F. (1907). Vox populi. Nature; Lorenz, J., Rauhut, H., Schweitzer, F., & Helbing, D. (2011). PNAS.
related: asch-conformity, anchoring

## false-consensus
title: The false consensus effect
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: We assume most people share our opinions and choices, whatever they are.
what: We overestimate how common our own views, habits and preferences are. Our friends tend to be like us, our own choice feels obviously right, and our perspective is the one we know best. So whatever we'd do, we think most people would do too.
study: In 1977 Lee Ross, David Greene and Pamela House asked students to walk around campus wearing a sandwich board saying "Eat at Joe's". Those who agreed estimated that 62% of their peers would also agree. Those who refused estimated that only 33% would agree. Each group thought its own choice was the common one. A 1985 meta-analysis confirmed the effect.
case: In 1983 Steven Sherman, Clark Presson, Laurie Chassin and colleagues asked adolescents to estimate how many of their peers smoked. Almost everyone overestimated it, and teenagers who smoked overestimated it most. Later work by Gilbert Botvin and colleagues found that teenagers' inflated beliefs about how common smoking was predicted their own tobacco use later on. The nuance: false consensus isn't harmless self-flattery. Smokers use it to feel normal, inflated estimates make smoking look normal to everyone else, and the belief then feeds the behaviour. Prevention programmes now often include "normative education", showing young people the real, lower rates, as a deliberate correction, with mixed but encouraging results.
proof: Replicated widely across topics and populations.
spot: Listen for "everyone thinks so", "nobody likes that" or "any normal person would...".
use: Before assuming others agree with you today, ask one person directly what they think.
guard: Use real data (surveys, polls) rather than your social circle to judge what most people think.
predict: Students who agreed to wear an "Eat at Joe's" sign estimated what share of peers would agree?
- About 33%
- About 50%
* About 62%
scenario: Sure that "everyone" hates the new office seating plan, Rahul is surprised when a survey shows most colleagues like it.
source: Ross, L., Greene, D., & House, P. (1977). The false consensus effect. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology.
related: naive-realism, pluralistic-ignorance

## groupthink
title: Groupthink
category: groups
evidence: debated
hook: Cohesive groups can make terrible decisions because nobody wants to break the harmony. The evidence is mostly case studies.
what: Irving Janis argued that tight-knit groups under pressure, with a directive leader and no outside input, suppress doubts, overestimate their own rightness and stereotype outsiders, leading to disasters like the Bay of Pigs invasion. The warning signs are useful, but controlled studies have not consistently supported the full model.
study: Janis set out groupthink in 1972, based on historical case studies of US foreign policy failures. Later reviews, such as James Esser's in 1998, found case studies offer some support, but lab experiments have produced mixed results, and cohesion by itself doesn't reliably cause bad decisions. Leadership style and closed decision processes seem to matter more.
case: On the night before the space shuttle Challenger's launch in January 1986, engineers at the contractor Morton Thiokol warned that rubber O-ring seals might fail in the forecast record cold, and initially recommended against launching. NASA managers pushed back, effectively asking for proof that launching was unsafe rather than proof that it was safe. During an off-line discussion, a senior Thiokol manager told the engineering vice-president to "take off your engineering hat and put on your management hat", and the company reversed its recommendation. In 1989 James Esser and Joanne Lindoerfer coded statements from the official Rogers Commission report and found signs of groupthink twice as often as counter-signs, increasing in the 24 hours before launch. The nuance: sociologist Diane Vaughan argued that the deeper cause was the "normalisation of deviance": years in which small amounts of O-ring damage came to be accepted as normal. Both accounts point to the same remedy: decision processes that make dissent cheap and put the burden of proof on safety.
proof: Debated: an influential idea with weak experimental support; parts of it, like suppressing dissent, are supported.
spot: Notice meetings where doubts are voiced privately but never in the room, and the leader's view is known before discussion.
use: In your next group decision, assign someone to argue against the favoured option, or ask "what would make this fail?"
guard: Leaders should share their opinion last and invite criticism explicitly.
predict: What did later reviews conclude about evidence for groupthink?
- It's one of the best-supported theories in psychology
* Case studies offer some support, but experiments are mixed
- It has been completely disproven
scenario: The startup's founders are so close-knit and excited that nobody raises the obvious cash flow problem, until it's too late.
source: Janis, I. L. (1972). Victims of Groupthink; Esser, J. K. (1998). Alive and well after 25 years. Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes.
related: group-polarization, asch-conformity, brainstorming, pluralistic-ignorance, psychological-safety

## contact-hypothesis
title: The contact hypothesis
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: Prejudice falls when groups mix as equals working towards shared goals. In one Indian study, cricket teams did it.
what: Contact between groups reduces prejudice, especially when people meet as equals, share goals, cooperate, and have support from authorities. Casual or hostile contact doesn't work as well. Cooperation is the key ingredient.
study: Gordon Allport proposed the hypothesis in 1954. In 2006 Thomas Pettigrew and Linda Tropp analysed 515 studies with over 250,000 people and found contact reliably reduced prejudice. In 2021 Matt Lowe ran a cricket league in Uttar Pradesh with randomly mixed-caste teams. Playing with people from other castes increased cross-caste friendships, while playing against them did not.
case: In 2020 Salma Mousa ran a football league in northern Iraq for Christians who had been displaced by ISIS. Christian players were randomly assigned either to all-Christian teams or to teams that included Muslim players. By the end of the season, Christians with Muslim teammates were 26 percentage points more likely to vote for a Muslim player from another team for a sportsmanship award, 13 points more likely to sign up for a mixed team the next season, and, six months later, 49 points more likely to train with Muslims. But their attitudes towards Muslims in general barely changed, and they were no more likely to visit Muslim-dominated public spaces, such as a restaurant in Mosul. The nuance: contact with equal status and a shared goal changed behaviour towards the specific people and setting involved, but didn't generalise. Contact works, but more narrowly and locally than its hopeful version suggests.
proof: Strongly supported by meta-analysis and field experiments, though rigorous long-term trials are fewer.
spot: Notice where groups mix as equals (sports teams, project teams, hostels) and where they don't.
use: Spend time today working or playing alongside someone from a different background, on a shared goal.
guard: Contact needs cooperation and equal status. Forced or competitive contact can make things worse.
predict: In the Uttar Pradesh cricket study, what increased cross-caste friendships?
- Playing against other castes
* Playing on teams with other castes
- Watching matches between castes
scenario: After a semester of working on the same college project team, Imran and Vikram, from very different backgrounds, become close friends.
source: Pettigrew, T. F., & Tropp, L. R. (2006). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Lowe, M. (2021). Types of contact. American Economic Review.
related: minimal-group

## just-world
title: The just-world belief
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: We want to believe people get what they deserve, so we sometimes blame victims to keep that belief intact.
what: Believing the world is fair is comforting: if bad things happen to bad people, we're safe. But when innocent people suffer and we can't help, we may protect the belief by deciding they must have done something to deserve it.
study: In 1966 Melvin Lerner and Carolyn Simmons had people watch a woman apparently receiving electric shocks in a learning task. When observers couldn't stop the shocks, they rated her more negatively than when they could compensate her. Lerner developed the just-world theory from this and later studies, which a 2005 review by Carolyn Hafer and Laurent Bègue found well supported.
case: In 2007 Claudia Dalbert and a colleague studied prisoners to examine a more hopeful side of just-world thinking. Researchers distinguish two beliefs: that the world in general is just, and that the world is usually just to me personally. Prisoners who strongly held the personal belief judged the legal process that convicted them, their treatment by prison officers, and decisions about prison life as fairer, and other studies link the personal belief to better coping and well-being. The general belief, by contrast, is the one most associated with blaming victims. The nuance: just-world thinking cuts both ways. Believing life will treat you fairly can help you endure hardship and keep working towards long-term goals; believing it treats everyone fairly makes it easy to conclude that other people's suffering must be deserved. The danger lies mainly in applying it to others.
proof: Well supported across many studies, though measured in different ways.
spot: Listen for "she must have done something to deserve it", "poor people are just lazy" or "he had it coming".
use: The next time you hear about someone's misfortune today, notice your first explanation. Is it fair, or comforting?
guard: Bad things happen to good people. Before blaming someone for their situation, look for the full story.
predict: When observers couldn't stop a woman's suffering, how did they rate her?
- More positively
* More negatively
- No change
scenario: When a colleague is laid off, Neeraj quickly concludes she must not have been working hard enough, though the whole department was cut.
source: Lerner, M. J., & Simmons, C. H. (1966). Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Hafer, C. L., & Bègue, L. (2005). Psychological Bulletin.
related: fundamental-attribution-error

## psychological-safety
title: Psychological safety
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: Better hospital teams reported more errors, not fewer. They felt safe enough to admit them.
what: Psychological safety is a shared belief in a team that it's safe to take interpersonal risks: to ask questions, admit mistakes or disagree without being humiliated. Teams with it learn faster and catch problems earlier.
study: In 1996 Amy Edmondson studied hospital teams and was surprised to find that better-led teams reported more medication errors. They weren't making more mistakes; they were more willing to report them. Her 1999 study of work teams linked psychological safety to learning. In 2015 Google's Project Aristotle, studying 180 teams, found psychological safety was the most important factor in team effectiveness.
case: In 2001 Amy Edmondson, Richard Bohmer and Gary Pisano studied 16 leading hospitals adopting a new, minimally invasive technique for heart surgery. It changed the operating room: nurses, anaesthetists and perfusionists took on new tasks and had to speak up when something looked wrong, because the new procedure depended on information spread across the whole team. All the hospitals had excellent reputations and similar resources, yet some adopted the technique smoothly while others struggled or gave up. The difference wasn't the surgeons' technical skill. In successful teams, the surgeon framed the change as a team learning challenge, explicitly invited input, chose team members deliberately and kept teams stable, so that a nurse could say "stop" without fear. The nuance: psychological safety isn't about being nice. In these teams it was a technical requirement, because the information needed to operate safely sat with people lower in the hierarchy.
proof: Supported by many studies and a meta-analysis; most evidence is observational.
spot: Notice who speaks in meetings, who stays quiet, and what happens to the person who admits a mistake.
use: In a conversation today, admit a small mistake or ask a "basic" question. Thank someone else who does.
guard: Safety doesn't mean comfort or low standards. The best teams combine safety with high expectations.
predict: Why did better hospital teams report more medication errors?
- They were overworked
* They felt safe enough to report mistakes
- They had more complex patients
scenario: When a junior analyst points out a mistake in her manager's report, the manager thanks her publicly, and others start raising issues earlier.
source: Edmondson, A. C. (1996). Journal of Applied Behavioral Science; Edmondson, A. (1999). Administrative Science Quarterly.
related: groupthink, beautiful-mess

## hawthorne-effect
title: The Hawthorne effect myth
category: groups
evidence: busted
hook: Workers were said to improve whenever lighting changed, just because they were watched. The original data show no such thing.
what: The Hawthorne effect is the idea that people change their behaviour simply because they know they're being observed. It comes from studies at the Western Electric Hawthorne Works in the 1920s, where productivity supposedly rose whatever lighting change was made. Re-analyses of the original data found little evidence for the story, though being observed can affect behaviour in some situations.
study: In 2011 Steven Levitt and John List re-analysed the original Hawthorne lighting data and found no clear evidence of the famous effect. A 2014 systematic review by Jim McCambridge and colleagues found that research participation can affect behaviour, but effects are inconsistent and the "Hawthorne effect" label is too vague to be useful.
case: In 2014 Jennie Srigley and colleagues measured the effect of being watched with sensors in a teaching hospital. For eight months, a real-time location system recorded every use of hand-rub and soap dispensers on two hospital units, along with when the hospital's hand-hygiene auditors were visibly present. When auditors were in sight, hand-hygiene events happened about three times as often as when they weren't. The nuance: the original Hawthorne factory data may not show what textbooks claimed, but being observed clearly changes behaviour in specific, measurable ways. That has a practical sting: hospitals that measure compliance with visible auditors may be recording behaviour that exists mainly while someone is watching.
proof: Busted as told: the original data don't support the story, though observation can matter in specific situations.
spot: Notice "it's just the Hawthorne effect" used to dismiss any improvement after a change.
use: When something improves after a change today, ask what else changed at the same time, before crediting either the change or "being watched".
guard: Use comparison groups to test whether a change really worked.
predict: When economists re-analysed the original Hawthorne lighting data, what did they find?
- Strong evidence that being observed raised productivity
* Little evidence of the famous effect
- That productivity dropped when lights changed
scenario: A school dismisses improved test scores after introducing a new teaching method as "just the Hawthorne effect", without checking.
source: Levitt, S. D., & List, J. A. (2011). Was there really a Hawthorne effect at the Hawthorne plant? American Economic Journal: Applied Economics; McCambridge, J., Witton, J., & Elbourne, D. R. (2014). Journal of Clinical Epidemiology.
related: replication-crisis

## peter-principle
title: The Peter principle
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: People get promoted for being good at their current job, until they reach a job they're not good at.
what: The Peter principle says that in a hierarchy, people rise until they reach a level where they're no longer competent, because promotions reward current performance, not the skills the next job needs. It started as satire, but data now support it in some settings.
study: Laurence Peter and Raymond Hull proposed it humorously in 1969. In 2019 Alan Benson, Danielle Li and Kelly Shue analysed data on tens of thousands of sales workers across many firms. The best salespeople were more likely to be promoted to manager, but their teams then performed worse than teams of managers who had been weaker salespeople.
case: In 2011 Amanda Goodall, Lawrence Kahn and Andrew Oswald looked at a field where promoting the best performers seems to work: professional basketball. Analysing NBA coaches over several decades, they found that coaches who had been outstanding players, especially All-Stars, went on to produce better team results; having a former All-Star as coach was worth roughly six places in the league standings. The nuance: the Peter principle depends on how different the new job is from the old one. A basketball coach's job still revolves around deep knowledge of the game and credibility with players. A sales manager's job is mostly about other people's work, not personal selling, which is why Benson's study found the opposite. Before promoting a star, ask how much of the new job is really the old job.
proof: Supported by a large real-world study in sales; more research is needed in other fields.
spot: Notice brilliant engineers who become frustrated managers, or star teachers made principals who miss teaching.
use: When thinking about your own next step today, ask what skills that role actually needs, and whether you want to use them.
guard: Promote based on skills needed for the new role, and create ways to advance without becoming a manager.
predict: When top salespeople were promoted to manager, how did their teams perform?
- Better than other teams
* Worse than teams led by weaker salespeople
- The same
scenario: Raj, the company's best coder, is promoted to team lead. He spends his days in meetings, misses coding, and his team struggles.
source: Peter, L. J., & Hull, R. (1969). The Peter Principle; Benson, A., Li, D., & Shue, K. (2019). Promotions and the Peter principle. Quarterly Journal of Economics.
related: dunning-kruger

## panic-myth
title: The panic myth
aka: Normalcy bias
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: In disasters, people rarely stampede. The more common danger is the opposite: waiting, collecting things and checking with others.
what: Films show crowds panicking in emergencies, and officials sometimes hold back warnings to avoid causing panic. Disaster research finds that mass panic is rare. People more often under-react: they assume things are normal, look for confirmation, talk to others and finish what they were doing, sometimes losing precious minutes. In real crowds, strangers usually help each other.
study: After 11 September 2001, the US National Institute of Standards and Technology interviewed more than 1,000 survivors from the World Trade Center towers. Before leaving, 70% talked to others, 46% gathered personal items, 23% searched for others and 16% made phone calls. In the north tower, people below the impact zone waited a median of about three minutes before starting to evacuate, and about five minutes near the impact floors. More than 99% of the people below the impact floors got out.
case: On 7 July 2005, bombs exploded on three London Underground trains and a bus. In 2009 John Drury, Chris Cocking and Steve Reicher analysed survivors' accounts from interviews and contemporary reports. Selfish behaviour was rare and mutual help was common: strangers comforted, guided and gave first aid to one another while waiting for rescue, and survivors described a sense of "we" with people they'd never met. The researchers argue that shared danger creates a shared identity, which makes crowds cooperate. The nuance: deadly crowd crushes do happen, but mainly because of density and poor exits rather than panic. The practical risks are delay and disbelief, so the useful habit is to treat an alarm as real and move first, asking questions on the way.
proof: Good: decades of disaster research agree that mass panic is rare and delay is common, though the size of delays varies with the event and the building.
spot: Notice alarms everyone ignores, people looking around to see whether anyone else is worried, and news stories that blame disasters on "panic".
use: The next time you enter a building today, find the nearest exit that isn't the way you came in.
guard: When an alarm sounds or something seems wrong, move first and confirm later. The urge to finish the email or collect your bag is the more dangerous instinct.
predict: World Trade Center survivors were asked what they did before evacuating. What did nearly half of them do?
- Push past others in a rush to get out
* Gather personal items
- Leave immediately without stopping
scenario: When the office smoke alarm goes off, Nikhil saves his spreadsheet, unplugs his charger and hunts for his umbrella before heading for the stairs, sure it must be another drill.
source: National Institute of Standards and Technology (2005). Final report on the collapse of the World Trade Center towers, NCSTAR 1-7; Kuligowski, E. D., & Mileti, D. S. (2009). Modeling pre-evacuation delay by occupants in World Trade Center Towers 1 and 2. Fire Safety Journal; Drury, J., Cocking, C., & Reicher, S. (2009). The nature of collective resilience: Survivor reactions to the 2005 London bombings. International Journal of Mass Emergencies and Disasters.
related: bystander-effect, pluralistic-ignorance, groupthink

## hidden-profiles
title: Hidden profiles
aka: The shared-information bias
category: groups
evidence: solid
hook: When the facts were split between group members, only about 12% of groups picked the best candidate. When everyone had every fact, 83% did.
what: Groups are meant to pool what each member knows, but discussion drifts toward what everyone already knows. Shared facts are mentioned more, repeated more and trusted more, because others confirm them; facts known to only one person often never come up. When the best choice depends on those unique facts, the group misses it, and everyone leaves more confident than before.
study: In 1985 Garold Stasser and William Titus had four-person groups choose among three candidates for student body president. When every member had all the information, 83% of groups chose the best candidate. When the facts favouring that candidate were split among members, so that the full picture appeared only if they shared, as few as 12% of groups chose the best candidate. A 2012 meta-analysis of 65 studies by Li Lu, Y. Connie Yuan and Poppy McLeod found that groups facing such hidden profiles were eight times less likely to find the right answer than groups with full information.
case: Doctors do it too. In 1998 James Larson and colleagues formed 24 teams, each made up of a resident, an intern and a medical student, and had each member watch a different version of a video interview with a patient played by an actor. Some facts appeared in all three versions and some in only one. When the key clue to the correct diagnosis appeared in only one member's video, teams gave a wrong diagnosis significantly more often. Unique facts were raised less often, and later, than shared ones, though the senior doctors were more likely to repeat them. The nuance: the fix is procedural. Have everyone write down what they know before discussion, ask each person "What do you know that the rest of us might not?", and give someone the job of tracking unique facts.
proof: Solid: the hidden-profile effect is one of the best-documented findings in group research, confirmed across 65 studies and thousands of groups.
spot: Watch meetings where everyone nods along to points already known, and where the quiet specialist's one unusual fact is mentioned once and forgotten.
use: In your next group conversation today, ask one person directly: "What do you know about this that the rest of us might not?"
guard: Before a group decision, have everyone list the relevant facts privately, then share every list. Treat a fact that only one person knows as a reason to dig deeper, not to dismiss it.
predict: When the facts favouring the best candidate were split among group members, how often did groups choose that candidate?
- About 80%; discussion pooled the facts
- About 50%, as if by chance
* As few as 12% of groups
scenario: The hiring panel spends an hour on the points all four interviewers heard. The one member who phoned the candidate's references, and heard they were poor, mentions it once; nobody follows it up, and he gets the job.
source: Stasser, G., & Titus, W. (1985). Pooling of unshared information in group decision making. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Lu, L., Yuan, Y. C., & McLeod, P. L. (2012). Twenty-five years of hidden profiles in group decision making: A meta-analysis. Personality and Social Psychology Review; Larson, J. R., et al. (1998). Diagnosing groups. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.
related: groupthink, wisdom-of-crowds, psychological-safety

## broken-windows
title: Broken windows
aka: The spreading of disorder
category: groups
evidence: debated
hook: With graffiti on the wall, 69% of cyclists dropped a flyer on the ground. With a clean wall, 33% did. Disorder seems to invite more disorder.
what: The broken windows idea is that visible signs of disorder, such as graffiti, litter and broken windows, signal that rules aren't enforced and so encourage more rule-breaking. Field experiments show that seeing one rule broken can make people break others. Whether this explains serious crime, and whether cracking down on minor offences reduces it, is far less clear.
study: In 2008 Kees Keizer, Siegwart Lindenberg and Linda Steg ran field experiments in the Dutch city of Groningen. When the wall of an alley where people parked their bicycles was covered in graffiti, 69% of cyclists threw away a flyer attached to their handlebars, against 33% when the wall was clean. In another test, an envelope with a 5-euro note showing was taken from a letterbox by 27% of passers-by when the box was covered in graffiti, against 13% when it was clean. In 2023 a set of replications and a meta-analysis found the effect real but weak, and dependent on conditions.
case: In the 1990s New York police, inspired by broken windows theory, cracked down on minor offences such as fare dodging and public drinking, and crime fell sharply. But crime fell in many cities that didn't adopt the approach too. In 2015 Anthony Braga, Brandon Welsh and Cory Schnell reviewed 30 rigorous tests of disorder policing. Overall there was a modest fall in crime, but it came from community problem-solving that fixed physical and social disorder in particular places. Aggressive order maintenance, arresting people for minor offences, produced no significant reduction. A 2019 meta-analysis of nearly 300 studies found no consistent evidence that disorder makes residents more aggressive. The nuance: cleaning up places can help; mass arrests for small offences don't, and they carry real costs for the people arrested.
proof: Debated: the field effect replicates weakly and conditionally, and the evidence that disorder causes serious crime is mixed and often confounded.
spot: Notice how people treat clean and scruffy spaces: the litter that gathers around one overflowing bin, the wall that attracts more posters after the first one.
use: Fix one small sign of disorder in a shared space today, such as a pile of shoes, a cluttered counter or a dead plant, and notice whether others follow.
guard: Before blaming disorder for bigger problems, or punishing small offences to prevent big ones, ask what the evidence says about that specific link.
predict: In the Dutch field experiment, how many cyclists dropped the flyer on the ground when the alley wall was covered in graffiti?
- About the same as with a clean wall, 33%
- About half
* About 69%
scenario: The office kitchen stays spotless for months. The week someone leaves a pile of dirty mugs in the sink, the counters fill with crumbs and the fridge with forgotten lunches.
source: Keizer, K., Lindenberg, S., & Steg, L. (2008). The spreading of disorder. Science; Braga, A. A., Welsh, B. C., & Schnell, C. (2015). Can policing disorder reduce crime? A systematic review and meta-analysis. Journal of Research in Crime and Delinquency; O'Brien, D. T., Farrell, C., & Welsh, B. C. (2019). Broken (windows) theory: A meta-analysis of the evidence for the pathways from neighborhood disorder to resident health outcomes and behaviors. Social Science & Medicine; Bergquist, M., et al. (2023). Are broken windows spreading? Journal of Environmental Psychology.
related: social-proof, norm-boomerang, asch-conformity

## reflected-glory
title: Basking in reflected glory
category: groups
evidence: good
hook: After a team wins, fans say "we won". After a loss, it's "they lost". We borrow glory and hold failure at arm's length.
what: We publicly link ourselves to successful people and groups, even when we did nothing to bring about their success, and distance ourselves from failures. Wearing the team shirt, name-dropping, mentioning that someone famous went to our school: all of these borrow status. The urge is strongest when our own self-esteem has just taken a knock.
study: In 1976 Robert Cialdini and colleagues counted students wearing their university's clothing at seven US universities on the Monday after football games: more wore it after a win than after a loss. In phone interviews, students were more likely to say "we" when describing a victory and "they" when describing a defeat, especially if they had just been told they'd done badly on a quiz.
case: Politics shows the same pattern. Two days before a general election in Flanders, Belgium, Filip Boen and colleagues recorded 482 houses displaying election posters and 180 with lawn signs, then checked them again the day after the vote. The better a party had done, the more of its supporters' houses still displayed its posters and signs; supporters of the losing parties took theirs down faster. They published the study in 2002. The nuance: basking isn't pure vanity, because belonging to groups is good for well-being. The trap is when borrowed glory stands in for your own achievements, or when your loyalty lasts only as long as the winning streak.
proof: Good: replicated in sport, politics and education, though most studies are observational fieldwork.
spot: Listen for "we" and "they" after a match, an election or a company's good or bad news, and for people mentioning famous connections.
use: Notice once today when you, or someone near you, say "we" about a success you didn't contribute to, or "they" about a failure you were part of.
guard: When you feel the urge to distance yourself from a group that's losing, ask whether you'd still say "we" if it were winning. Loyalty that lasts only through wins isn't worth much.
predict: In phone interviews after a football game, how did students describe the result?
- "We" for wins and losses alike
* "We" more often after a win, "they" after a loss
- "They" both times, to sound objective
scenario: When the national team wins the final, Siddharth posts "WE ARE CHAMPIONS!" all day. When they lose the next series, his post reads "these players have no spine".
source: Cialdini, R. B., et al. (1976). Basking in reflected glory: Three (football) field studies. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology; Boen, F., et al. (2002). Politics and basking-in-reflected-glory: A field study in Flanders. Basic and Applied Social Psychology.
related: minimal-group, self-serving-bias
